# Finding Purpose Through the Bhagavad Gita and Research

## Applicant Profile

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2023/2024 |
| GPA (Unweighted) | 4 |

## Decisions

- ✅ Harvard University
- ✅ Stanford University
- ✅ University of Pennsylvania
- ✅ Columbia University
- ✅ Johns Hopkins University
- ✅ University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill

## Essay — Prompt #5

> Discuss an accomplishment, event, or realization that sparked a period of personal growth and a new understanding of yourself or others.

My spirit animal in middle school was a rat. I had all the characteristics. I was quick and witty, yet constantly unsatisfied. I worked tirelessly, running an endless rat race to excel in whatever I did—school, Science Olympiad—attracted solely by the insidious bait of being able to think that I was somehow better than someone else. And like most rats, I got trapped by my own thinking, especially when I realized that simply being better than someone never accomplished anything. Yet I didn't learn—I ran the race over and over again, and I was never left contented. I finally came to my senses in a dingy, musty room with hardwood flooring, barely the size of a walk-in closet. This was the classroom for Group 4, a group of high school students—including myself—receiving religious education as part of a grassroots organization known as the Sathya Sai Baba Center of Cleveland East. I've been part of this organization since first grade, but Group 4 opened my eyes to an entirely new universe of thought. Every Sunday, Group 4 students gather shoulder to shoulder, pressed up against the walls of the classroom, and read the Bhagavad Gita. At first, I thought I was too simple-minded to understand the Gita's abstruse ideas, but if it really was a "user's manual to life," curiosity beckoned me to make the most of it. With my attention glued to its thin, cream-colored pages, I read the Gita meticulously, sometimes at a snail's pace of three pages an hour, the curious gears of my brain insatiably moving to reason through the Gita's often arcane ideas. It was as if I were drawn into a perpetual dance with the author, who presented new ideas and forced me to respond, lockstep, with my own understanding. But after two years of reading the Gita and wrestling with its ideas, all of the thoughts floating unbridled in my head coalesced into one, definite realization: Each person has their niche. Each person is crafted for their own unique purpose, with their own interests and talents. As if all of humanity were just a gargantuan puzzle, every human being an irreplaceable piece. But I could never find my place in the puzzle if I lived the life of a rat instead. So I finally took responsibility for my purpose in the world—a responsibility I was determined to fulfill. Research is a perfect reflection of the Bhagavad Gita. Because while the Gita invites me to fill my distinct niche within society, research invites me to fill gaps within human knowledge. I realized this in my AP Research class. I vividly remember poring over more than 50 articles, scrupulously narrowing my focus until I found a gap that I could fill: examining determinants of HIV PrEP usage in

rural counties, addressing crucial, geographic disparities in PrEP access. What fascinated me was the replicability of this process, especially when I ventured to design and carry out an award-winning molecular docking study addressing the HIV-1 K65R mutation with no university backing. I was still just a piece in the puzzle of humanity, yet I was filling holes in the puzzle of human knowledge, insatiably driven by a sense of purpose. And that has become my place in the world. Everything I do—such as writing innumerable reports for Science Olympiad and even leading Speech and Debate—has become focused on filling the tapestry of research, using my voice to make an impact well beyond the track I was once locked within. The Gita is the ideal I aspire to live by, and research paves my way forward to a life I can be proud of. This pride radiates from a newfound sense of satisfaction, grounded in my intellectual curiosity and the discoveries I aspire to make. Satisfaction with a life led not by a rat's aimless struggle, but by my own captivating purpose—for college and beyond.

---

# The Strength of Perseverance

## Applicant Profile

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| SAT Total | 1490 |
| SAT Math | 720 |
| SAT Reading/Writing | 770 |
| GPA (Unweighted) | 3.72 |

## Decisions

- ✅ Cornell University
- ✅ Dartmouth College

## Essay — Prompt #1

> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

Sunday, July 22nd, started like any other. My mom and I were gearing up for our usual visit to my brother's Waikiki apartment, a quick catch-up planned for the afternoon. But that crisp morning, everything took a turn. A frantic call from his roommate shattered the routine. My brother was on his way to the ER. The drive to Queen's Hospital was a blur. My mind raced with a million questions as worry gnawed at me. Although I tried to assure myself that nothing serious could have happened to him, anxiety clouded my mind. My brother, Tyson, emigrated from Vietnam with my mom and my other older brother to the United States in 1990, with dreams of a new life and fresh opportunities. He enrolled in high school with virtually no knowledge of the English language. Even though he had to simultaneously manage a part-time job at McDonald's, he excelled in academics and was the top of his class in calculus. At 34 years old, he was the epitome of health: he ran marathons every year, had a healthy diet, and never smoked or drank alcohol. When I got to the ER and saw him lying in the hospital bed, he looked like the Tyson that I always knew. Nothing seemed wrong. He just seemed tired, and he didn't have the energy to speak. When I was a kid, I was a crybaby. I cried when I didn't get the toy I wanted. I cried when I didn't get the food I wanted. However, at some point during my childhood, around the age of six, I stopped crying. No matter how much I was teased or pushed around, I never cried. No matter how much I was mocked about my clothes, or my ethnicity, I didn't cry. August 11 last year was the first time since childhood that I cried. It was the day that my brother passed away. And it was the first time that I ever saw my mom cry. It was a traumatizing experience, and for a while I was depressed that such a tragedy could occur so arbitrarily to someone like my brother: someone who was strong, someone who was healthy, someone who lived by a strong moral code and never sacrificed his values for material rewards. But after a while, I realized that the circumstances of his death were not a refutation of his beliefs, but instead, a reminder of their importance. Even though we cannot control the twists and turns of life, we must deal with them as best we can. My brother, even though he didn't know English, enrolled in school and ultimately excelled. And at the same time, to help our mother go to school on the side, he took on a part-time job. Certainly he must have wished that he hadn't faced those disadvantages, but he didn't complain. Rather, he faced the realities of his situation head-on, and succeeded. Tyson's death was a tragic reflection of the cold, random chance of nature, but it was in no way any verdict on his philosophy: instead, I realized, it served as a clear reminder to me that the worst can happen to even the best, and that the strength of an individual lies in his ability to maintain his values when faced with such difficult situations. Today, I still hold onto the lessons that my brother taught me through his actions: to put the needs of your family first, to always persevere in the face of adversity, and to never compromise your ideals for petty desires. To lose heart in these values because of his death, then, would be a harsh disservice to Tyson's legacy.

---

# Transforming My Lifestyle: A Journey to Clear Skin

## Applicant Profile

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| SAT Total | 1550 |
| SAT Math | 800 |
| SAT Reading/Writing | 750 |
| GPA (Unweighted) | 3.96 |

## Decisions

- ✅ Cornell University
- ✅ Yale University
- ✅ Columbia University
- ❌ Stanford University
- ❌ University of Miami

## Essay — Prompt #3

> Reflect on a time when you questioned or challenged a belief or idea. What prompted your thinking? What was the outcome?

Everyone preached about inner beauty, but staring at my reflection, a battlefield of blemishes, felt like a betrayal of that message. By high school, the acne had escalated, leaving my confidence in tatters. So, that summer after ninth grade, I embarked on a journey for clear skin— a quest that turned out to be far tougher and more emotionally draining than I anticipated. But in the end, it became a source of immense pride. My typical daily diet consisted of sugary cereal for breakfast; salty turkey sandwiches, soda, and chips for lunch; a candy bar for snack; and rice and fried noodles for dinner. I never thought that my diet would be a cause of my acne, but the possibility first came to me when I was reading a skincare article. Desperate for a cure, I searched "clear skin diet" on the Internet. The websites that turned up all echoed the same message: a balanced and healthy diet is crucial for beautiful skin. The recommended foods listed included large portions of fruits, vegetables, grains, nuts, and fish; 8 glasses of water daily and only small amounts of high-sodium, high-fat, and high-sugar foods. I researched further and found out that the expensive chemical cleansers that I had been using were not the answer—gentle face washes and a coat of sunblock were inexpensive products that could reduce breakouts. I knew that changing my entire diet and skincare routine would require a tremendous amount of commitment and willpower. But determined to improve my skin and my self-confidence, I began to transform my lifestyle. I started by incorporating fruits and vegetables into my meals and replacing sugary cereal with whole grains, chips with carrots, and fried noodles with salmon. For a long, painful week, I stuck to this diet and restrained myself from any junk food. My skin condition changed gradually but substantially. By the end of the week, my skin was noticeably smoother, clearer, and brighter. Yet I found myself relapsing, unable to continue for long without indulging myself, unable to swallow the horribly plain salads and chewy carrots. Every time I stopped, the acne came back. It came to the point where I despised the acne and coveted the clear skin enough to force myself back on track. It became an excruciating pattern of cravings and self-restraint. I struggled with these two impulses until I became used to healthy eating, even enjoying it. Now, two years later, my skin is better than ever and I have never gone back to eating the way I had before. I have no awards or medals to show for my particular achievement. But no academic distinction in the world can match what I gained from my experience with changing my diet: healthier skin, self-confidence, and newfound mental strength. Above all, I realized that by improving my outer appearance, I had enriched my inner appearance.

---

# Autumn to Spring: A Journey of Self-Discovery and Acceptance

## Applicant Profile

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2023/2024 |
| SAT Total | 1540 |
| SAT Math | 770 |
| SAT Reading/Writing | 770 |
| GPA (Unweighted) | 3.9 |

## Decisions

- ✅ Harvard University
- ✅ Stanford University
- ✅ University of Pennsylvania
- ✅ Northwestern University
- ✅ Brown University
- ✅ Emory University
- ✅ Vanderbilt University
- ❌ Yale University
- ❌ Princeton University
- ❌ Johns Hopkins University
- ❌ Duke University

## Essay — Prompt #7

> Share an essay on any topic of your choice. It can be one you've already written, one that responds to a different prompt, or one of your own design.

My dad once told me, “When I was younger, I was a tree, and my mother would cut down all my branches [passions] until I was left with only one STEM.” Autumn: The leaves fall. My dad raised me with this exact mindset, cutting down all my extracurriculars to study math. His expectations—so large—kept me in the shade away from the shining possibilities of my talents in other fields, especially the arts. A dark hole consumed me: I started casting everyone out and spent all my time studying. But, no matter how many nightly math sessions, I could not improve my AMC8 score above 10. Quickly, even though I was only 12, I started doubting my self-worth, and my childhood excitement burnt out. The more math questions I couldn't solve, the larger the strain was in my family and the more pressure my dad put on me. To rebel, I ran away from academics and focused on the very thing my dad hated: the arts. Winter: The rebirth. Theater changed my life. I learned to empathize with my character's emotions and perspective, opening up new doors to myself and sparking my journey to create a positive impact on both my family and community. Opening night of the event of our year—Robin Hood—my dad was in the audience. With emotional prep, I took the stage and immediately snapped into character. In only two hours, I felt my fire for living light back up. I left backstage and even now I remember the exact words my dad said to me that day, "You were good. I didn't know you could do something like that." Spring: When flowers bloom. That was the moment my dad and I first started to understand one another. With mutual empathy, I applied my passion for the arts back to math, giving myself

another chance at the AMC. My dad and I doubled our course load; test after test we competed against each other, adding fuel to my fire for improvement. And right before my last AMC8—I beat him with a score of 21! This experience taught me to value myself and my endeavors. That I am enough. That I don't have to give up my boisterous, emotion-driven theater personality for my quiet, calculation-based math background, or vice versa. In fact, both helped me transition from my past hiding-in-the-corner self. A brighter future. Of course, I still had my insecurities, but rather than keeping the trauma bottled in, I embodied them in my performances for a realistic retelling. In a dramatic interpretation, I performed as Claire—a teenager struggling with her sister Rachel's death. Starting with a smile, I embodied Claire's denial. With tears dripping down, I harnessed her anger. And through music, I held onto our very last connection: from a slow ballad to a crescendo with hands around my neck, I sang my climactic revelation—leaving my past behind me with a breath of acceptance. Silence. Slowed claps. Both my judges in tears. That's when I realized how impactful my art form could be. Both judges had different ages, races, body types, and most definitely lived different lives, but both of them came together at that moment, understanding the pain we all try to conceal. But that's exactly it: our pain shouldn't define who we are, instead, it should aid our growth. Express it—just as Claire expressed herself—and then look forward to the light ahead. From

brightening up a passerby's smile with a compliment to initiating group huddles, I promise to always be myself and a pillar of support for everyone to express their narratives and emotions. Soon, it'll be Fall again, restarting my growing journey. But this time I'll start college not as a broken branch burnt by the smoke but as a beautiful flower shining under the sun.

# The Power of the Tabla: Finding Grace and Force

## Applicant Profile

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| SAT Total | 1500 |
| SAT Math | 720 |
| SAT Reading/Writing | 780 |
| GPA (Unweighted) | 3.92 |

## Decisions

- ✅ Duke University

## Essay — Prompt #1

> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

Witnessed by Brahma, the Hindu deity, a society writhed under the poisonous grip of envy and avarice. In an act of compassion, he bestowed upon a young man a mystical drum, perceptible only to the touch, unseen by the eye. This divine instrument, imbued with the power to cultivate serenity and discipline, was destined to become the cornerstone of India's illustrious classical music tradition. Yet, the boy remained unaware of the drum's true potential: a conduit for the human spirit, its vibrant rhythms echoing unspoken messages from the depths of the soul. My guru told me this story when I began learning the tabla, a north Indian classical drum. Though he never revealed the ending, he offered one clue. "It doesn't matter how fast your hands can beat the drum if your mind cannot keep up with them." A typical American teenager, I had always yearned for raw strength and speed on the tabla. I thought those were the qualities that made the perfect percussionist. During concerts, I saw musicians' fingers gliding across the head of the drum producing the extraordinary impression of force, immediacy of genius and intensity of passion that left audiences spellbound. I knew that to become the best at tabla, I needed to display my talent in a similar way. I wanted to play equally as fast and forcefully, even if it meant sacrificing precision for power. I asked my guru to train me to play like the maestros, but he said I lacked mental focus; I didn't listen to or enjoy my music. He believed the mind and body must be in sync in order to master the instrument. I never fully understood this concept until the imagination of one visually handicapped child attuned my mind to the beat of the drum. As a summer volunteer at the Braille Institute in Anaheim, I developed the musical talents of blind elementary school children through teaching the tabla. My friends and I bought instruments and music with the money that we made during a summer recycling program in our neighborhood. During our first class, the children jumped into action, thumping erratically on their instruments. I chuckled at the sight of nine jubilant youngsters producing musical mayhem. Chaandni was different. Shy and reserved, she felt the drum's smooth texture and stroked the soft middle portion of the drum head. I stood astonished as she performed her ritual. Tap. Listen. Smile. Tap. Listen. Smile. I could see her contemplating the emerging rhythm and resonance of each successive pulse. Watching a budding musician feel the delicate timbre of each drumbeat is a thrilling sensation. I learned more from observing than I did through years of erratic practice. Chaandni would eagerly listen to a musical piece, then strike incessantly until she produced a matching pitch. When she stumbled, she would sit patiently pinpointing her mistake. She didn't speak; her insight and emotion glowed through her music. For the first time in my life I felt the depth of the drums. For the first time, I truly comprehended and loved the instrument. She was indeed a special soul. Her curiosity was insatiable; her regard for the drum was incomparable; her smile was inspiring. Her joyful progress inspired me to perform in London this summer with my guru. Driven by the spirit of a young girl who never let her disability impede her

innate talent, I played gracefully in front of hundreds of spectators and cherished the inner triumph. I am a living example of Brahma's story. The combination of grace and force on the tabla is my channel for communicating emotions. My tale ends with the appreciation that the mind is a beautiful vehicle for the drum.

---

# The Power of Childhood Innocence

## Applicant Profile

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| Intended Major | Aerospace Engineering |
| SAT Total | 1580 |
| SAT Math | 800 |
| SAT Reading/Writing | 780 |
| GPA (Unweighted) | 3.98 |

## Decisions

- ✅ Massachusetts Institute of Technology
- ✅ Stanford University

## Essay — Prompt #2

> The lessons we take from obstacles we encounter can be fundamental to later success. Recount a time when you faced a challenge, setback, or failure. How did it affect you, and what did you learn from the experience?

Beneath a sky of cotton candy fluff, a monstrous brown bear slunk silently. Unaware, the vibrantly pink duck paddled serenely, her feathers shimmering in the dappled sunlight. Little did she know, a monstrous paw, the color of rich, dark chocolate, was about to clamp down on her delicate neck. A simmering pot bubbled nearby, promising an unwelcome, steaming fate. We turned the page. While I chuckled at the impracticality of a bear boiling water to eat a duck, especially a pink duck, I lifted my head to fnd Matthew tiptoeing about the room exhibiting the meanest, most ferocious look his cute face could conjure. All the while, little Monica sat huddled next to my arm honestly afraid to turn the page and fnd her favorite pink creature in a bear's "tummy." It was one of those moments of my hours spent reading with children at the library when it dawned just how much, as a "grown up," I was missing. I remembered the Thursday when victoria bounded into the reading room showcasing in her small hands a golden certifcate from school."Best reader," it glistened. A smile came across my face as I looked into the eyes of the petite 7-year old who just last year had been held back in frst grade because her reading was not up to par. It was the same smile that had flled my face six months prior, when victoria joined the reading program and I saw the other volunteers instantly point at me. Our supervisor had agreed with them, knowing I would use my patient disposition and friendly way with children to motivate our new student. I had nodded vigorously, smiling, not only because I was proud of the confdence they had in me, but because nothing would make me happier than taking on the challenge of helping victoria improve her reading. After spending the frst session responding to an unrelenting stream of questions, I recognized that victoria's talkative nature and impatience for answers overshadowed her desire to sit down and read. I treasured her energy and insatiable curiosity and fueled it with my own enthusiasm. Yet, for every interest she presented, I took her to scan the library shelves in search of a related book. I watched her eyes grow with excitement as I tirelessly helped her press through the stories, a journey in search of her answers. Some of the books I chose were diffcult for her, but we read through them together, challenging limits and quenching the thirst for knowledge. In books, I told her time and again, she would fnd all she wanted to know. Whether it is victory celebrations or talking in different voices, whenever I am with these kids, I fnd myself being pulled into their childhood world—a world of simplicity, of undying curiosity, and of pure innocence. It is a world in which if everything is not perfect, it defnitely can be. And with a simple "prayer to god" or "kiss on the boo-boo" it will be. Though I go in each week to be these kids' teacher, I come out, having been their student. They have introduced me to a side of me I never realized existed.

As I enter college, it is not only my intelligence or my accumulated knowledge, but also the kid in me who will bring success. This child will jump to try every new activity with an enthusiasm that cannot fade. She will ask questions of everything she sees, of everything she hears and of everything she reads. She will dream big and for every step she stumbles upon towards that dream, she will get right back up and step again, this time, a little more carefully. And she will do all this, approach every life hurdle or triumph with a smile- a big contagious smile.

---

# Finding My Voice: From Quiet Observer to Environmental Advocate

## Applicant Profile

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| Intended Major | Business Management And Administration |
| SAT Total | 1500 |
| SAT Math | 760 |
| SAT Reading/Writing | 740 |
| GPA (Unweighted) | 3.93 |

## Decisions

- ✅ University of Pennsylvania

## Essay — Prompt #7

> Share an essay on any topic of your choice. It can be one you've already written, one that responds to a different prompt, or one of your own design.

A booming voice shattered the classroom calm. "HELLO! WHAT'S YOUR NAME?" I stammered out a string of unfamiliar sounds, "PEN-YUAN HSING." The response was immediate, a confused frown crinkling the teacher's face. "What?" I understood nothing. Starting first grade in the US was a baptism by fire—a world of indecipherable sounds. Three years later, returning to Taiwan felt equally disorienting. The once-familiar characters on a page now resembled cryptic symbols. Blending in, in either language, seemed an impossible feat. For many years, I was always the quiet one sitting in the corner, the one who few people talked to, the one out of the loop. I was the "local alien." I opted to join the Earth Science Club during my first year in Lishan High School, as I always had an interest in astronomy, which happened to be the focus of that year's club activities. I didn't know the weekly gathering time of our club was also an elective Earth Science course that students from other high schools could attend. I certainly did not realize what a big impact this arrangement would have on me. Near the end of the first semester, I was approached by a girl from a neighboring school who attended this earth science course. She asked if I wanted to join her on an environmental survey of Taiwan's Keelung coast conducted by an organization called Taipei Wetnet. For a moment I hesitated, I literally had no experience in responding to invitations. What suddenly came out of my mouth surprised me, "Sure, what time?" For the next three years I spent in Taipei Wetnet, I gained not only a close friend, knowledge about the problems our environment faced, but perhaps most importantly found a group of people who shared the same convictions, who are passionate about the same thing, the plight of our precious planet. A year after being introduced to this organization, I was its coordinator and presented my first academic paper at an environmental education conference. I learned that I don't always have to be the quiet one in the corner, that I do and can have things to share with everyone else. I don't have to fear. I often think about how I managed to say yes on that fateful day. Was it just because of a pretty face? Or maybe there has always been a special part of me that wanted to get out, and she was instrumental in "flipping the switch." This eye-opening experience and what I learned from it is what I desperately want to share with the world. Perhaps, somewhere out there is another quiet person in the corner just waiting to be found. A switch waiting to be flipped. You just have to find it, flip it, and make the world a brighter, warmer place.

---

# Empowering Youth Through Advocacy: My Journey Toward Racial Equality

## Applicant Profile

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2023/2024 |
| SAT Total | 1480 |
| SAT Math | 700 |
| SAT Reading/Writing | 780 |
| GPA (Unweighted) | 3.82 |

## Decisions

- ✅ Harvard University
- ✅ Yale University
- ✅ Columbia University
- ✅ Duke University
- ✅ Emory University
- ✅ University of Virginia
- ❌ Princeton University

## Essay — Prompt #7

> Share an essay on any topic of your choice. It can be one you've already written, one that responds to a different prompt, or one of your own design.

"They could have been from my village." That's what I thought when my 6th-grade teacher spoke about the completion of the Canadian Pacific Railway—the first transcontinental railroad in the country—and briefly mentioned the brutal treatment of the 17,000 Chinese workers who risked their lives to connect the country. Even though my classmates celebrated the railway's construction, I was conflicted. While I felt a sense of pride knowing that my ancestors had helped connect the nation I called home, I also felt betrayed by Canada's targeted abuse of vulnerable workers who looked like me, spoke the same language as me, and practiced the same customs as me. I had always thought of Canada as a welcoming place. My parents immigrated to Vancouver in 1999 to seek economic opportunity. This country welcomed them with open arms and gave them plenty of resources to set them up for success. Sadly, this wasn't always how Canada treated its immigrants. When I learned about Canada's dark history, I was forced to reexamine my perceptions about my birthplace; after all, I was living in one of the most diverse cities in one of the most diverse countries in the world—I had grown up with friends, classmates, and adults from all walks of life. I could never fathom how any individual, let alone an entire government, would believe someone to be "lesser" simply due to their ethnicity or national origin. In elementary school, I had only a classroom-level, basic understanding of intolerance. At the time—a decade ago—Canadian classrooms only started briefly mentioning instances of historical oppression against marginalized groups. Until I almost entered high school, I wasn't aware of my province's long practice of anti-Chinese segregation and Canada's 24-year ban on Chinese immigrants. Today, most students do, in fact, learn about racism in the classroom. However, the classroom will never be able to thoroughly teach others about the experiences of the millions of people who've been harassed, abused, or locked out of this country. Between the limited coverage of historical wrongs and the rare acknowledgement of current instances of systemic bigotry, the education system fails marginalized communities by

declining to teach the truth about the history and current impacts of discrimination in society. As a passionate debater, I combined my love for debate and my desire for advocacy to found an international debate organization, GYP Debate Club, to empower youth, especially ones from vulnerable or low-income backgrounds, from all across the world to find their voices and learn how to use them to fight for equality and their community's interests. To accomplish this mission, I've organized classes, summer camps, and tournaments to mentor the young leaders of tomorrow to become great debaters and global citizens. By filling in the gaps of knowledge that others refuse to cover to mobilize the next generation of activists, the club has been my way of working toward a better future. While teaching the importance of racial equality during meetings, I observed some students' faces undergoing the same experience that I had in my sixth-grade classroom. However, I also saw many faces determined to make things right. My experience at GYP Debate Club has reaffirmed my goal of pursuing a career in policy where I can learn how to craft and implement progressive policies to heal our broken world. More importantly, it has also made me more confident in breaking the taboo around uncomfortable topics such as racism and poverty, which I hope to continue doing at a larger scale, on campus and in clubs, to spark constructive and informative discussions around pivotal issues, ultimately leading to long-overdue reform. In doing so, I hope to fix the intergenerational wounds of discrimination so that when kids in the future learn about their country's dark history, they won't have to feel conflicted about what's happened—they'll feel proud of what's been done.

---

# The Bacon Mentality: A Hunger for Knowledge

## Applicant Profile

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| SAT Total | 1580 |
| SAT Math | 780 |
| SAT Reading/Writing | 800 |
| GPA (Unweighted) | 3.94 |

## Decisions

- ✅ Stanford University
- ✅ University of California, Berkeley

## Essay — Prompt #1

> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

Many high schoolers might picture their alarm clock as a screeching beast, designed solely to torment the sleep-deprived. But hold on, grumpy morning faces! This might be a bit of a dramatic take. Sure, that first jolt awake after a long night isn't always pleasant. We get it, fumbling around for the source of that disruptive racket can be frustrating. But why the negativity? It isn't because the only thing they desire is to sleep a few extra hours, as many would presume. No, these kids are groggy and irritable because they are waking up to what they think will be another horribly boring day of school. If one of these foolish Sallys or Joes were, say, sleeping comfortably on a Saturday morning, I could certainly see something different happening. A beautiful breakfast of tantalizing vittles—eggs, hash browns, and the like—would be ready and waiting for them on their kitchen tables. But the scrumptious delight to outshine them all would be a slab of bacon, piled proudly for the taking. It would be that wafting, wondrous bacon smell that would draw dear, sweet Sally abruptly from her slumber—long before an alarm clock has the chance to pierce the air. Oh, bacon: what a marvelous, glorious thing! I live for those heartstoppingly good strips of succulence, so crispy and crunchy, so packed with perfection. The thought of having a plate of bacon every day, perhaps every school day, sends me into sheer waves of ecstasy! To be sure, many others would also wax poetic about this lovely breakfast food. When I board my school bus Monday through Friday, it is still pitch black outside. Busmates will groan about how even the day has not yet dragged itself out of bed; I only chuckle through their thirty-minute rant fest as we chug down the freeway. Opting to be part of a faraway Magnet school, after all, has its benefits. My peers may still not look forward to waking up earlier, but when we are all together in a classroom, we take on the "bacon mentality." I have the opportunity to choose from a wealth of diverse classes, and love arriving to school each day with the prospect of having a new Spanish History lesson—taught to me in Spanish, for a change. Teachers, driven by the enthusiasm of their Magnet students, are inspired to create new classes for advanced students, including those who have completed AP Spanish Literature and are still eager to learn more, or those who want to learn about a specific aspect of a subject—we now have a Middle Eastern History class. Not to be outdone, the post-AP exam period of my English Language class included an intensive literature study, where we laughed at good ol' Yossarian in Catch-22, and developed a strong attachment to Jay Gatsby. I'd like to think that The Great Gatsby's pursuit of Daisy is not unlike my own pursuit of bacon. I've

gobbled up new knowledge rapidly, hankering after it like any elusive bacon strip, and happily digesting any new bits of information. But six classes a year are simply not enough to satisfy my hunger for knowledge. Just as I eat bacon all three meals of the day (when possible), I attempt to learn all days of the week. Rather than make another trip to some lackluster movie theatre on the weekend, I dedicate my time to reading another good book, or reviewing Economics with my friends. But high school is starting to smell like leftovers to me now; I want fresh, new, crisp learning. I want not to read a textbook written by a renowned professor: I want to hear him speak directly. I'm ready for the university, and hunger for all the new opportunities waiting for me! I've finished my breakfast, and now it's time to get going to school.

---

# Embracing Spanish: A Love Story

## Applicant Profile

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| SAT Total | 1570 |
| SAT Math | 770 |
| SAT Reading/Writing | 800 |
| GPA (Unweighted) | 3.99 |

## Decisions

- ✅ Stanford University

## Essay — Prompt #7

> Share an essay on any topic of your choice. It can be one you've already written, one that responds to a different prompt, or one of your own design.

At the tender age of four, I wasn't smitten by a classmate or a cartoon character. This love was far deeper, a bond that transcended the fleeting fancies of childhood. It wasn't a person who stole my heart, but an entire language: Spanish. Having been born and raised behind the Iron Curtain, in a country where Western influence was limited and the official and only language was Romanian, I was on my own. Everyone around me, especially my family, had trouble understanding what could possibly draw me to such a foreign and, in their opinion, unattractive language. But as they say, love is blind, and the truth of the matter is that I wasn't even sure what it was exactly that made Spanish so fascinating to me. The only thing I knew was that I absolutely adored hearing its perfectly articulated phrases, and trying to make sense of its sweet and tender words: serenades to my innocent ear. Spanish entered through my door on June 16th, 1994, when a man from the local cable company came to connect our living room to the rest of the world. That day, I was introduced to "Acasa," a Romanian cable network dedicated to broadcasting Spanish language telenovelas (soap operas) to Romanian audiences. As I learned to read, I started associating the Romanian subtitles with the Spanish dialogue, and little by little, I began understanding the language. There are no words that can describe how proud and greatly accomplished I feel today at my ability to speak Spanish. During a recent trip to Mexico, I was mistaken more than once for one of the natives. One man, after seeing my Romanian last name, asked me if it was my husband's, for undoubtedly, he believed, I was Mexican. Given to a Romanian girl, whose family members were oblivious to the language, and who had learned it on her own despite their objections, this was the greatest compliment of all. In the United States, Spanish is the second most spoken language and a great asset for anyone who speaks it. It is not "nonsense," as my father had dubbed it, and being able to prove this to him has made me even prouder for loving Spanish. My love of Spanish has influenced much of who I am today. The fight that I led against family objections and immigration to a new land has allowed me to develop an ambitious and aggressive spirit in the face of adversity. It has made me stronger, and taught me that I must always fight with unstoppable perseverance for all that is important to

me. I am determined to use my love and passion for Spanish to make an impact on the world. Currently, Spanish is the primary language of 21 nations around the globe, and one of the six official languages of the UN. I want to be the link that connects these nations to the United States, and to the 40 million Americans whose native language is Spanish. I want to use my ability to speak Spanish to learn more about the people of these nations, both on a professional and personal level. No matter where the path of life takes me, I wish for Spanish to always be a part of me. Through the years, Spanish has evolved into one of my most remarkable accomplishments. Today, I am prouder than ever of loving Spanish—of having something that distinguishes me from the rest, something that makes me unique. It is not often the case for a Romanian-American girl living in Los Angeles to exhibit such passion and devotion towards a language that is foreign to both her native and adoptive countries. Nevertheless, Spanish is a big part of whom I am today, and an even bigger part of who I will be in the future.

---

# The Power of Words: Overcoming Obstacles and Finding Purpose

## Applicant Profile

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| SAT Total | 1600 |
| SAT Math | 800 |
| SAT Reading/Writing | 800 |
| GPA (Unweighted) | 4 |

## Decisions

- ✅ Stanford University
- ✅ Yale University
- ✅ Columbia University
- ✅ Duke University
- ✅ Brown University
- ✅ Vanderbilt University
- ❌ Princeton University
- ❌ Washington University in St. Louis
- ❌ Dartmouth College

## Essay — Prompt #2

> The lessons we take from obstacles we encounter can be fundamental to later success. Recount a time when you faced a challenge, setback, or failure. How did it affect you, and what did you learn from the experience?

Everyone says that your first impression is your last impression; your way with words and perfect posture tell the world what it needs to know about you. But as a child, my words didn't flow off the tip of my tongue like silk; they bent over each other at odd angles as if they couldn't wait to finally leave me. My biggest hurdle was the letter r, which came out sounding like "v" or sometimes refused to come out at all. My name lost its power when I introduced myself; I was always trying different variations until I gave up and forced my sister to say my name for me. Before class presentations, I would practice in front of a mirror and rehearse over and over again until my throat ached; I wanted to be perfect. Still, I would become flustered and rush through my flashcards until the final word sprinted from my mouth. Often, when I looked up, I saw looks of pity accompanied by a resonating sort of silence. Eventually, I became my own speech therapist; I added more pauses when I spoke, listened to recordings of myself speaking, and practiced correcting every slightly malformed syllable. Every correct word was a drop of confidence that eventually overflowed into a sense of purpose. I met Emily Dickinson during junior year of high school when my teacher printed "The Soul selects her own Society" on the board. Dickinson's literary gifts gave me my first glimpse into the true power of the words I had struggled to say all my life. Each word seemed carefully hand-picked by Dickinson to create a message capable of multiple interpretations. In Teen Court, I use Dickinson's approach to wording in my opening statements and questionings, referring to the defendant by name and using euphonic syllables and innocent adjectives to encourage empathy from the jury. I forge personal connections between my client and the jury through my subtle changes in verbiage, just as Dickinson did between herself and her readers. The dramatic dashes and commas that riddled the lines of Dickinson's poetry inspired me to integrate dramatic stops in my speeches, pauses that not only commanded attention but also gave me time to better enunciate and attack

the tricky words that used to trip me. I learned to speed up and slow down when explaining important ideas and add emphasis through my tone. I imagined my cases were like the poetic puzzles; the edges were the regrets and hopes that encompassed the facts of the case and outlined a future that could reset any known boundaries. My delivery and language were crisp cuts on the corners that folded to meet at the heart of every case. Each word became a piece of a unique puzzle that reveals the story of a misguided teen. More importantly, Dickinson's poem warned me that not even the most formidable of forces should stand in the way of the path I choose. Her words sowed the roots of feminism while subtly nodding at the strict Calvinism that surrounded her daily life and reflected a sense of self-reliance and strength that stood in contrast to the constraints of society. Dickinson inspired me to push against the boundaries that limit so many young minds by championing youth offenders in Teen Court, where I hope to lead those who struggle down a better path in spite of the economic and social conditions that suppress them. Today, I use the voice I crafted with self-assurance to help others find the confidence in their own voice by starting a club focused on promoting youth activism and creating a tutoring program teaching teenagers how to read and write. As I push forward, I know I am making my younger self proud by finding the right way to finally raise my voice.

---

# The Symbiotic Relationship of Science and Art

## Applicant Profile

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2023/2024 |
| Intended Major | Biochemical Sciences |
| SAT Total | 1510 |
| SAT Math | 780 |
| SAT Reading/Writing | 730 |
| IB Score | 45 |

## Decisions

- ✅ Duke University
- ✅ Pennsylvania State University
- ✅ Merrimack College

## Essay — Prompt #7

> Share an essay on any topic of your choice. It can be one you've already written, one that responds to a different prompt, or one of your own design.

Bright lights waiting to attack me. The mixture of glycol and water (known as theatrical smoke) is creeping across the stage with its sneaky molecules. The audience is staring, noticing every little imperfection, a strand of misplaced hair or my mismatched socks. I stare back at them and suddenly the individuals blur together, with the colors reminding me of my rack of test tubes with colorful liquids. I now have the same inquisitive look, staring at my ever so slightly pink titration, wondering if it has hit its endpoint. The bunsen burner seems as angry as always and the clamp stand looks judgmentally towards me. I am trying to take in every detail of the lab, wondering whether DCPiP would give me a better titration result than using iodine, or what color the precipitate would be in a reaction between sulfuric acid and potassium carbonate. Empty test tubes and an empty stage both motivate my creativity to roam freely, encouraging me to create pear-smelling propyl butanoate or my modern version of Romeo and Juliet. Science and art are often segregated and placed against each other. I believe, however, that my frontal lobe and frontal cortex are a pair, working together to allow me to try my best in all aspects of my life from investigating bizarre hypotheses to pushing my creative limits. Acting has allowed me to use my brain to its full capacity in the scientific and academic fields. That may seem peculiar, however for me the two are inseparable. For example, my formulaic, even robotic, approach to learning lines has allowed me to fully immerse into the worlds beyond the ‘black box,’ taking in every second of my presence in 13th century Spain or Willy Wonka's chocolate-filled world. My approach, of repeating every word or sentence, was definitely not invented by the right hemisphere of my brain. Following patterns in my scientific experiments, I recognized skills I could apply to my acting ‘career.’ Acting has also helped me in the lab. Whenever an experiment goes wrong (unfortunately this happened a few times) I pretended that everything was ok. Weirdly enough, this acting pushed me to continue with my investigations, and ultimately helped me find the optimum temperature for storage of Vitamin C serums. Finally, acting has allowed me to overcome my slightly ‘dramatic’ amygdala. This amygdala is responsible for many things and it used to sneak up on me at my worst, in exam halls and on stage. Nevertheless, through acting I learned to transfer myself to the previously mentioned worlds in which some of my characters reside. These worlds would help me calm down rather than derail my progress. I once saw art and science as very individual pursuits; an actor often stands alone on a stage and a scientist is often only joined in the lab by their experiments and ongoing research. I saw the error in this thinking as both became a bigger part of my life. I realized that working alone, in science or art, is actually harming my performance because I only saw things my way. Through trial and error, including many failed experiments, I embraced group work and developed tactics to ensure the best outcomes for each team I worked with, especially as I know not all group members are created equally. Acting has taught me to sometimes forget about the rationale of things, to dream about the future or not think about the negatives in the present. On the other hand, science has taught me to see the small details in my work and to never make the same mistakes. Students I've engaged with through these activities have become my sounding boards and surprisingly it's often in theater class that I think of the variable I haven't yet tried in a certain experience, or in biology class where I imagine a new interpretation of a theatrical classic to try on stage.

---

# Embracing Technology: A Journey of Empathy and Connection

## Applicant Profile

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2023/2024 |
| Intended Major | Economics |
| SAT Total | 1540 |
| SAT Math | 790 |
| SAT Reading/Writing | 750 |
| IB Score | 41 |

## Decisions

- ✅ Northwestern University

## Essay — Prompt #5

> Discuss an accomplishment, event, or realization that sparked a period of personal growth and a new understanding of yourself or others.

Summer is demanding in Singapore with sweltering heat and high humidity. With reluctance and trepidation, after a half-hour metro ride and a long walk, I reached the polyclinic to help the elderly navigate digital devices. Across the globe, we have seen a digitalization push adopted by nations. Accelerated by Covid 19, the pandemic propelled us into a realm where technology increasingly became an integral part of our daily lives. However, Singapore's silver generation struggled to cope with the rapid digital advancements. To help them navigate technology, I embarked on a community engagement. I worked with elderly people to impart know-how on basic computer, mobile, and internet skills. During my stint, I discerned a resistance to embrace technology that stemmed from an apprehension of failure, and a fear of making mistakes. Senior citizens found it challenging. Having lived the majority of their lives without the digital conveniences that surround us today, they did not understand the need to adapt to the change. Additionally, they were also the soft target for scams and hence distrusted the systems. However, over time, I was able to help the elderly get over their apprehensions and get accustomed to using different mobile apps. I particularly worked to familiarize them with HealthHub, a government-run initiative, to book and track medical appointments and order medicines from the confines of their homes. I also made it a point to help them differentiate between scam and phishing messages. We engaged with the community through workshops designed to educate them on cybersecurity practices. These workshops aimed to not only safeguard them in technology usage but also foster stronger community bonds. It was a slow process but eventually they started stepping outside their comfort zones. During this experience, I discovered the power of social interactions and found absolute delight in the stories and experiences that the elderly individuals shared. Their resilience had a great impact on me. Despite finding technology overwhelming, they stayed tenacious and persevered through challenges. What began as a summer project transformed into an experience teaching me about the boundless potential for social connections, and how learning does not ever stop. An elderly Chinese woman, with a mop of silver hair on her head and a twinkle in her eyes became my unlikely teacher for

life. Her poignant strength while enduring numerous hardships inspired me through the depth of her unbroken spirit. Teaching at community centers and local polyclinics did come with its challenges. I had to overcome language constraints and even to teach the simplest activities demanded patience. However, I was determined to navigate these challenges. In guiding the Silver generation in technology usage, I found myself undergoing a transformation as well. I was able to develop powerful connections and communication skills, as the experience underscored the importance of empathy and patience. It was also incredibly gratifying to be able to make a contribution to society that nurtured me into who I am today. The volunteering experience helped me gain a new understanding of the learning process. I found immense joy and satisfaction in the simple process of sharing with the community. It also taught me the art of using education and available resources to make a positive impact around us. By the end, I developed a deep desire to look for larger goals outside of my immediate gains. I hope to carry this experience of interacting with different age groups and different ethnicities into my future life. I am confident this would greatly benefit me going forward, interacting in the diverse communities in the United States, strengthening the community and my sense of belonging.

---

# Building Bridges: The Power of Digital Connections

## Applicant Profile

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2023/2024 |

| SAT Total | 1480 |
| SAT Math | 800 |
| SAT Reading/Writing | 680 |
| IB Score | 41 |

## Decisions

- ✅ University of Pennsylvania
- ✅ Michigan State University
- ✅ Virginia Polytechnic Institute and State University
- ✅ Iowa State University of Science and Technology
- ✅ University of Texas at Dallas
- ❌ Harvard University
- ❌ Princeton University
- ❌ University of Illinois at Urbana-Champaign
- ❌ Georgia Institute of Technology

## Essay — Prompt #7

> Share an essay on any topic of your choice. It can be one you've already written, one that responds to a different prompt, or one of your own design.

To many, Roblox is merely a digital playground of blocks and pixels. Yet, for me, these pixels and blocks transcend their virtual bounds, blossoming into conduits for real, human connections. This profound insight dawned on me not in the throes of gameplay, but rather in the quieter, reflective aftermath of my first Roblox Event Organizer Program interview. The particulars of the interview itself have since slipped from my memory, yet what followed remains vividly etched in my mind. It was after we had stopped recording that the interviewer, Matt, and I casually started chatting. This light-hearted conversation unexpectedly delved into our shared frustrations with Roblox Studio, the game development engine. It was in these moments of shared grievances and laughter that I discovered a kindred spirit; someone whose experiences echoed my own. Matt and I, starting from that serendipitous conversation, have maintained our connection, often reaching out to each other when faced with another peculiar glitch or challenge in Studio. Our dialogues had a unique rhythm, an enthusiasm that transformed a seemingly mundane topic into an intimate exchange. It was like stumbling upon an old record in a thrift store, a tune forgotten by many but cherished by those who recognize its value. Be it remembering to send a ‘Happy Birthday’ message at precisely 12 a.m., or being one of the first people a friend turns to in times of joy or despair, I've come to realize that the connections I forge extend well beyond superficial interactions. Even with my fellow developers of Drift Paradise, my team members and I share a bond so genuine that our off-hours often find us competing in video games or collaborating on school studies over calls. To me, the people I interact with are just others trying to navigate this insane world that throws different challenges at us every day. It doesn't really matter to me if they're behind pixels on a screen, that is a real person somewhere in the world, even if they are thousands of miles away. For me, these connections aren't simply a byproduct of my work or social interactions; they are the very essence of my being. Just as every line of code I write is purposeful, every conversation, every shared moment, adds a distinct hue to the world I am creating for myself—a world where I am more than a developer or a gamer; I am a confidant, an ally, a friend. While digital connections came easy to me, growing up, communicating with my family was like trying to navigate in the dark. My family, loving but traditionally reserved, often found it challenging to hold deep conversations. Here is where my online interactions became a training ground, equipping me with the skills to express myself more openly. This lit pathways back into my family life, enabling me to share my world with them more clearly and confidently. In turn, our family conversations grew richer, bridging gaps that once seemed insurmountable. Roblox, therefore, is more than a platform of infinite blocks and pixels; it represents a world crafted by the relationships and connections I've nurtured—a melody of that tune from the thrift store, playing over and again in my head. As I look forward to university and what lies beyond, my aspiration isn't just to be another name on a list or another face in the crowd. I aim to be that unforgettable melody—a symbol of camaraderie, support, and authentic interest in the lives of those around me. And while I am passionate about immersing myself further in the digital realm, I am acutely aware that it is the connections I forge, not the code I write, that will stand the test of time. For, in the end, it isn't about how many blocks I've stacked, but about the bridges I've built.

---

# Breaking Free: From Chess to Public Speaking

## Applicant Profile

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2020/2021 |
| Intended Major | Computer Programming And Data Processing |

## Decisions

- ✅ New York University
- ✅ Purdue University

## Essay — Prompt #1

> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

"Will I ever be more than what I am?" was the phrase I spent most of my childhood asking myself. Quiet, reserved, and anxious are the words I would use to describe the nine year old me. Time and again, I would find myself disengaged from conversations with aunties and uncles during family gatherings and dinner parties simply because I wanted to be left unnoticed. Instead, I only engaged in conversations with my parents, especially my father. One of the highlights of my childhood was learning chess from my father. It started off with me losing to him everyday, until the miraculous day the tables turned. Since then, I have played chess for more than 5 years and attended over 25 competitions, winning more than 20 awards. Apart from these achievements, a new community had formed for me; it helped an anxious girl gain her confidence and made her feel like she fit in. I was surrounded by people who like me, didn't need to engage in loud conversations and tell delightful stories. This allowed me to fully embrace staying out of the spotlight. For 5 years, I was comfortable with chess until I was encouraged to join the Model United Nations (MUN) Club by my mother. Prior to this, I had never been exposed to public speaking—I can still recall my frenzied heart as I approached the podium at my first MUN conference. Distraught, breathless, and sweaty, I stuttered through my opening statement until the very end when it hit me, this wasn't so bad after all; I just needed to get the words out. This pushed me outside my comfort zone of sitting quietly in front of the chess board. It gave me the courage to initiate conversations with my peers. These conversations revealed that most of them were also nervous about public speaking. The bold students I was intimidated by, were in fact a different version of myself so why was I scared of talking to them? With that newfound perception, I took on higher responsibilities and went from Delegate, to Chair, to Conference Organizer, and to President of the MUN Club today. I sought out additional liberating experiences of self-development by continuously challenging myself to step out of my comfort zone such as competing at the "World Scholar's Cup". It was an opportunity to continue to pursue my love for debating and communicating with students of various backgrounds. Today, I have successfully turned all my anxieties and fears into my strengths. I now mentor students of different ages for MUN, World Scholar's Cup, and Chess—

the game that started my whole journey to breaking free from feeling like I would never be more than what I was. I teach chess in hopes that one day, it may do for another student what it did for me.

---

# The Unbreakable Bond

## Applicant Profile

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2023/2024 |
| Intended Major | Sociology |
| IB Score | 32 |

## Decisions

- ✅ University of Southern California
- ✅ Oregon State University
- ❌ Yale University
- ❌ Columbia University

## Essay — Prompt #5

> Discuss an accomplishment, event, or realization that sparked a period of personal growth and a new understanding of yourself or others.

At a young age, I had always wondered why I never had a father. Being raised in a household of women in Mexico I have always questioned my upbringing, asking questions such as, "Why do I not have a dad?" My mother would respond by saying, "Not everyone has a mother." It never made sense to me. In my point of view, a complete family meant having a mother and a father. Coming from the town of Jocotepec, located in Jalisco, Mexico, and throughout my time living in the United States since 2010, I have always questioned my father's absence and never given up on seeking closure. As months progressed, I became upset at the thought of not being good enough for my biological father. Being an immigrant was difficult as it is; living with my mother's friend in a full house in the Live Oak area of Santa Cruz with only one parent by my side, I felt alienated from everyone. As time passed, we eventually moved in with my aunt for about a month. The first thing I noticed was that my cousins had their father with them, a complete household. Once the month had passed, my mother and I moved into a rental manufactured home. As a result of my mother working long hours as a housekeeper, sometimes she would bring me along, knowing that she would get home very late. She attempted to prioritize my well-being while managing to balance being a housekeeper and a mom, yet I would often question the idea of why we had to live the way we lived and why she had to work the way she worked. It felt unfair and I felt cheated. I believed that my father was with another family, providing for them; meanwhile, my mother and I had to endure difficult times with barely enough to make ends meet. I did not hate him, though I always wished my mom did not have to struggle to survive. With hardly any stable income, sometimes I would hear my mother crying gently while I pretended to be asleep. Our

one-bedroom home did not mask the reality of being an immigrant, nor did it overcome the difficulty of being raised by one parent in a foreign country. One year had passed. With very little money, my mother took me to a McDonald's on foot to celebrate Thanksgiving. I recall eating chicken nuggets, while my mother ate some of my food because she was not able to afford her own meal. On the way back home the very same thought developed: "Why do I not have a father?" However, on our walk back home, an epiphany hit me. My mother raised me all on her own, without help from anyone. She had sacrificed her personal life back in Mexico in order to give me a better life. She put food on my plate even if it meant she would have an empty stomach for the day. She would use her bus fare money if it meant I would get to school faster, resulting in her having to walk home from wherever her last job was. My mother sacrificed so much for me, and for the first two years, I did not realize it; I did not need a father because my mother was enough. The story of my upbringing is not defined by absence but by presence—the presence of a mother who, against all odds, carved a path for us through the wilderness of uncertainty. This epiphany encapsulates the essence of my growth and the unbreakable bond I share with my mother—the true source of meaning and completeness in my life.

---

# From Video Games to Victory: Overcoming Health Challenges Through Coding

## Applicant Profile

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| Intended Major | Computer Programming And Data Processing |
| SAT Total | 1510 |
| SAT Math | 800 |
| SAT Reading/Writing | 710 |
| GPA (Unweighted) | 3.81 |

## Decisions

- ✅ University of California, Los Angeles
- ✅ University of Michigan - Ann Arbor
- ✅ University of Texas at Austin
- ✅ University of California, San Diego
- ✅ University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill
- ✅ University of Florida
- ✅ University of Miami

## Essay — Prompt #2

> The lessons we take from obstacles we encounter can be fundamental to later success. Recount a time when you faced a challenge, setback, or failure. How did it affect you, and what did you learn from the experience?

The weight of the situation hadn't hit me until my doctor and parents stood in front of me, faces stern. When the kids at the YMCA called me "chunky" or "big boy," it bothered me, but I just laughed it off. I couldn't laugh it off in front of my parents and especially not in front of my doctor. My future health was at risk if I didn't make major changes. I never dreamed these changes would not only help me but also make a difference in my community. I've always been fascinated with video games. After elementary school, I would race through my homework to play on my desktop. I played it all: sports games, math games, racing games, and even pointless clicker games. Without many friends at school, I found comfort in feeding chickens in Minecraft or running alongside Luigi in Super Mario Bros. While video games didn't stop me from staying on top of my schoolwork, they kept me away from any physical activity. Discovering coding in middle school only enriched my love for video games. I made websites about all sorts of topics that interested me including my favorite athlete, Dwyane Wade (LeBron had already left Miami). I designed another website about exciting math facts (Did you know that the Fibonacci sequence often appears in nature?). Eventually, I explored beyond static web pages and taught myself how to write JavaScript. These new skills allowed me to create dynamic web pages with color-changing backgrounds and scrolling text. Over time, my hobbies and interests grew, but they only contributed to my health complications. I was sharpening my mind with my new passion for coding, but my lack of physical activity caught up to me, making me slow, unmotivated, and insecure about my weight. My parents eventually told me they needed to schedule a doctor's appointment. I had to make a change, but I didn't want to stop doing what I loved. I discovered that I could combine my passion for video games and programming by coding my own games. One day, while reading a JavaScript tutorial online, I learned how to code my very first game: Setting Simulator. It was challenging at first, but after the struggle, I marveled at the volleyball game I had created. Even though I would beat my dad in Setting Simulator, he would always destroy my friends and me at the community volleyball court. Learning about different sports when making games not only taught me about programming but balancing time with family, friends, and the outside world. Rather than playing video games all day, I became interested in playing these sports. Daily practice with the varsity volleyball team whipped me into shape. I used to play basketball and volleyball with an Xbox controller; now, I shoot three-point shots and set volleyballs with the other kids in my neighborhood during the day and code fun games with my friends at night. With a new love for video game design, I wanted to share my passion with others in my community. I saw video game design as a way to engage the younger generation in a fun, productive way. A year ago, I created a non-profit organization called "Flamin' Gamin' Design" and recruited others to join me. Every week since then, we have gone to my local Boys and Girls Club to teach underprivileged students about coding, specifically making games. Working

with my peers and the students in my non-profit allowed me to continue my love for coding while helping others in the community also find their passion. The next time I walked into the doctor's office, my doctor was impressed to see how fast I had lost weight. When asked how I did it, I just responded, "By making video games." Confusion swept his face, but if he had seen my development over the past year, it would all make sense.

# The Ripple Effect of Decisions

## Applicant Profile

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| Intended Major | Mechanical Engineering |
| SAT Total | 1550 |
| SAT Math | 780 |
| SAT Reading/Writing | 770 |
| IB Score | 43 |

## Decisions

- ✅ University of Pennsylvania

## Essay — Prompt #5

> Discuss an accomplishment, event, or realization that sparked a period of personal growth and a new understanding of yourself or others.

As Apollo astronaut Ed Baldwin sat at the counter of the Outpost bar in Houston watching the Soviets make their landing on the Moon, he mulled over his missed opportunity to land first with Apollo 10. This premise of the show For All Mankind, which looked into the effects of the alternative outcome of the Space Race, prompted me to reflect on the potential of choices made differently. What if I had taken a different decision? My dad insisted on weekly walks around the park with me - tortuously long ones. The prospect of being told off for something I had done, or worse, not done, nearly kept me from what could be the most defining moments of my life. One of those dreaded days, as I changed out of school clothes, one half of my 13-year-old mind was trying to fabricate excuses, while the other half contemplated the importance of it. Luckily, the conscientious part chose the walk. As we set off on our walk, I had my guard up, expecting the regular programme of sermonizing, but what I heard struck a far more resonant chord than expected. He wanted to discuss my anxiety about changing schools. My apprehension stemmed from a fear of flux. As someone who depended on the comfort of structure, I simply had no idea of how I might navigate without a plan informed by my experiences. He spoke of the art of calculated risk-taking, through the conduit of his own experiences. From his missed opportunities to his peer's successes, this perspective comforted me to take the leap of faith, and move to my new school with confidence. Yet, that wasn't the end. His words came alive in my actions once again when I completed an ambitious engineering project, while all my peers doubted me up until the moment of reckoning. That magical ability to discern the impossible from the risky, but possible - imparted that one fateful evening in the park, has only favored me in my ventures. That evening, my dad also elaborated on the importance of finding the right people. While it did serve to alleviate my anxieties of fitting in at school, I aimed to apply his advice. By observing those around me in different environments, I developed a habit of reading the room. Speaking up when needed, but also taking the time to listen, intentionally. Subconscious practice of this has helped me make the right connections at research conferences and virtual summits, as well as in school groups and classrooms. My dad was enacting Inception - planting ideas into my brain that have turned into defining choices. As I ruminate on the countless, tiny decisions which came to define my person, I am struck by the serendipity encoded in all of them. It was the gift of a space encyclopedia, which nudged me to an exploration of a career in the space sciences. It was the spontaneous decision to download an exercise app which motivated me to weight train. It was the grace of YouTube's recommended algorithm that sparked an interest in working

with the disabled community. It is the understanding that so many abstract moments have brought me where I am now, creating an odd sense of gratitude wherever I go. As for that question of having made a different decision, I have come to realize that as I hurtle through space and time - one in a galaxy of countless - what actually matters is my ability to accept agency for my decisions and learn from every one of them. Just as Cooper realized the effects of time dilation upon leaving Miller's Planet in Interstellar, I now recognize the fragility of the world we live in, and the overarching importance of living in the present, while being receptive to change.

---

# Resilience, Grief, and Self-Discovery: A Journey to Balance

## Applicant Profile

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2023/2024 |
| Intended Major | Business Management And Administration |
| SAT Total | 1410 |
| SAT Math | 660 |
| SAT Reading/Writing | 750 |
| GPA (Unweighted) | 4 |

## Decisions

- ✅ University of California, San Diego
- ✅ University of Washington
- ✅ University of California, Davis
- ✅ Pacific Lutheran University
- ✅ Western Washington University
- ✅ Earlham College
- ✅ Whitworth University
- ❌ Johns Hopkins University
- ❌ University of California, Los Angeles

## Essay — Prompt #2

> The lessons we take from obstacles we encounter can be fundamental to later success. Recount a time when you faced a challenge, setback, or failure. How did it affect you, and what did you learn from the experience?

Inflicted by chilling winds atop the bleachers of my coach's memorial service, I embarked on a journey of resilience, grief, and self-discovery. With every word spoken by the keynote speakers that faintly emanated from the amplifiers above me, I was reminded of Coach Ruston. How his wisdom empowered my introverted, scrawny self at the line before every race. How his boisterous laughter lit up the bus rides home. I couldn't imagine my life without the sheer confidence and compassion he instilled that defines me today. The creeping in of devastation was interrupted by a concurrent THWACK. Along the track, the collages he had awarded his best runners with had begun falling like dominoes in the harsh wind's attempt to play the role of comedic relief. Except whereas others were relaxed, I felt immense pressure from the realization that my collage was one of the few still left standing and that amidst runners from generations of teams coached by him, I was too. At our end-of-season banquet last year, Ruston asserted that we had big plans for my senior year. Now with the XC season approaching, I was desperately determined to fulfill them in his honor. As my perfectionism often dictates, my intense commitment to running also encompassed the rest of my life. Suddenly, the strain caused by the pounding of my steps against unrelenting asphalt had become my sole source of catharsis, self-approval, and control. For the next three months, not a day would pass in which I didn't lace up my Brooks. Nothing could deter me. Fatigue from Covid-19 was helpless to prevent me from traversing the streets of Olympia. A lack of GPS and an inability to decipher Japanese street signs only lengthened my grueling ventures through the mountainsides of Sapporo, Hokkaido. There were countless instances where I wanted to stop. But I needed the validation that Ruston's tutelage was not in vain, and that I'd pushed beyond my limits to be the best. Unfortunately, my myopic thinking prevented me from realizing the dooming effect of this desire: pain.

Throughout the XC season, I was plagued by confusion as to what the source of my unexpected plateau in performance was. It seemed ridiculous that I'd lost my edge. Then on my 18th birthday, my legs finally gave out and I finished 18 places behind the final state qualifier. Nonetheless, rather than soak in sadness after having qualified just a year ago, I accepted the gift of realization. My disappointing finish wasn't the result of a lack of fortitude, but rather of diminishing returns. I realized that fear of expectations had driven me to overtrain, as I longed for an unattainable level of control. And that the wiser person is not the one who never gives up, but instead the one who knows when to let go. Recognizing my overexertion would've led me to alter my training in favor of balance, thus saving me from future failure. This led me to discover that achieving success requires embracing firm self-belief - no matter the circumstances - and that despite how invested influential figures like Ruston were in my achievement, they never would've wanted it to come at the expense of my health. With this in mind, I was inspired to honor Ruston not by running in grief, but by imparting the confidence and altruism he instilled in me by leading my XC team and by joining my school's leadership program to become a mentor. In college, I'm bound to face these same challenges. The expectation of perfection placed on me all my life will be applied to increasingly difficult assignments and decisions with life-changing implications. However, with a newfound commitment to self-compassion, balance, and a realistic perspective on personal growth, I won't allow these expectations to consume me. Freedom to embrace a life of happiness and success will be mine.

---

# Empowering Creativity and Organization: My Journey with IKEA

## Applicant Profile

| Field | Value |

| Application Year | 2023/2024 |

| GPA (Unweighted) | 3.9 |

## Decisions

- ✅ University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill
- ✅ University of Massachusetts at Amherst
- ✅ Indiana University at Bloomington
- ✅ The Tulane University of New Orleans
