- ❌ Pomona College

## Essay — Prompt #1

> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

IKEA, the well-known furniture giant, is spinning around in a tornado. The background music intensifies as the furniture and wood chippings in the wind transform into highlighters, lip-balm and french fries. IKEA has turned into a little person. To be more precise, into me. I snap back to reality as IKEA emerges from the tempest back in its familiar form. This store is my comfort place. Even though that may be solely based on its revolving entrance, it's still my second home. Home and IKEA can sound like a quirky oxymoron. However, there is an atmosphere of homeliness in every store, and not just because of its life-like room displays. IKEA harnesses the power of humour to peel away its robotic aura, integrating the famous quote by Victor Borge that says, "Laughter is the shortest distance between two people''. When I walked into the store, my eyes landed onto a poster with the iconic meatballs that said "Have a bite, no assembly required". Like the brand's marketing team, I like to use small quips and witticisms to lighten my environment. For example, once, while volunteering at a children's hospital, my friend Sayan (a 7-year-old in the cancer ward) refused to eat. He had been recently relocated, was undergoing intense treatment and was extremely upset. I sat by him and asked, 'Why do French people eat snails?' He looked curious. I handed him his plate and said, 'Because they don't like fast-food.' He rolled his eyes, but I stayed. Later, he softly asked 'knock-knock?' That day, he finished every bit of his food. I have visited the store for a purpose. With a colour-coded list in hand, I stride to find "Dronas" or foldable containers. I resonate with how each object has a specific task, no matter how small or big. I've always believed that my penchant for organisation empowered me into taking on, and seeing through significant projects. Be it leading my house for an inter-school competition, leaving no task unturned for my semester exams or relentlessly perfecting my gymnastics for the district finals in the same week, I have relied on timetables, lists and deadlines to juggle my work. Planning helped me be a good leader without sacrificing my other priorities. I find my containers instantly as IKEA's level of organisation mirrors mine. Content with having 25 minutes before I begin calculus homework, I go explore other wonders. My mind makes little cloud annotations for the room-layouts. As someone who dreams of living in a cleverly designed shoe-box, I have deep appreciation for anything that is creatively efficient. Being brought up in an economically-conscious household has taught me to be a problem-solver and to utilise freely available resources, similar to how IKEA encourages the DIY spirit. For example, I love Lego. But instead of buying expensive sets, I would buy individual piece packets and use a website to form sets. I would rent and buy second-hand Lego, while reselling my sets. This innovation allowed me to have charming Lego-flowers on my desk, a mini ferris-wheel and a Lego Starry Night painting above my bed. My motivation empowers me into taking up the largest tasks with few resources. When I see small objects improving people's day-to-day lives, I feel encouraged knowing I too can help make an impact. IKEA's pocket-friendly pricing makes it a store for the community and while it may seem like another multinational corporation, it's simply a well-developed idea, a place of solutions. I aim to learn and grow so one day, I can build my own ideas into an empire that supports my people. As I exit those glass doors with my heart (and tote-bag) full, I feel a sense of optimism, hope and most importantly, I feel inspired.

---

# Lessons Learned: The Impact of Family Camp

## Applicant Profile

## Decisions

- ✅ Duke University
- ✅ University of Wisconsin - Madison
- ✅ Indiana University at Bloomington
- ✅ University of Illinois at Chicago
- ✅ University of Kansas **(Enrolled)**
- ✅ University of Georgia
- ❌ University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill
- ❌ Rice University
- ❌ Washington University in St. Louis
- ❌ University of Virginia

## Essay — Prompt #7

> Share an essay on any topic of your choice. It can be one you've already written, one that responds to a different prompt, or one of your own design.

Family Camp has been the most consistent thing in my life. My family has moved multiple times around the city, friends have come and gone, but every year of my life, my whole family from all across the country gathers for one week each summer. We have done this for decades, even before I was born, and my cousins and I will continue the tradition when we have families of our own. I am one of the few who have never missed it. Family Camp is so important to me because all I need to know in life I learned at Family Camp. Family Camp has taught me the benefits of a real friendship. Each summer, my cousins pile out of their cars and we barely say hello before going off to do something fun. We always hit it off immediately, and do nearly everything as a group. At the start of every Family Camp week, we scramble to pick rooms and figure out sleeping arrangements (though it usually ends up the same every year). With true friends like my cousins, everything comes easy. There is no arguing over who gets to go tubing first or what movie to watch that night. We always look out for each other and make sure no one is excluded. I'm never afraid to try something new during Family Camp. Surrounded by the support of my family, Family Camp gives me a space where I feel comfortable taking risks. It's a courage that I have applied to my everyday life. I don't find myself nervous about new experiences anymore. Going into college, I strive to be as adventurous as possible so I never miss out on any opportunity. Over the years, Family Camp has taught me how to appreciate responsibility. As a kid, I'd be the last one out of the pool or always want to sleep in late. Now that I'm becoming an adult, expectations have changed. I'm usually chosen to refuel the boat or start the campfire but I view it as more of an honor than a chore. Knowing that people I care about count on me motivates me to lug one more heavy canister of fuel and ignore the smoke in my eyes. Taking on extra responsibility at Family Camp made me want to seek it elsewhere in my life, knowing the pride I always felt after completing a task that went above and beyond. Being the first one out of my friends to get a job was a little scary. It was a nerve-wracking new experience for me, but I approached it with confidence. When I first started working at Bad Apple, I had to earn the respect of the owner, manager, and my coworkers. I was the youngest person on staff, with no experience working at a large restaurant. I found myself thinking back to my time at Family Camp. I learned how to earn respect and work with those who were not necessarily my peers. Now, when I am at work, I make an effort to be the best team player and uplift my coworkers. Just like my cousins at Family Camp, I want them to feel that I'm looking out for them and won't leave anyone behind. I feel confident that respect for those around me, support for others, and courage to take on something new will help me throughout life, especially as I transition into an adult. From life skills to developing my personal values, the greatest teacher has been Family Camp. This tradition has helped me grow into a better friend, responsible adult, and positive contributor to my community. I know the torch will pass to me to continue Family Camp for future generations, and I look forward to that, but in the meantime, I'm grateful for the experience and how it has prepared me for entering this next stage of my life.


---

## Essay — Prompt #1
> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

When I walked into Michael's 9 years ago, the smell of scented pinecones permeated the

air. My mom insisted that we go to purchase a box to keep all my birthday letters in. Not

caring about sentiment, I picked up the first box I saw. It was a teal polka-dotted box

that said, "Explore and have an adventure.= At first, these words didn't hold much value,

but now they shape who I am today, along with the mementos inside the box. Now, the

box is filled with more than just birthday cards, containing music, language, friendship,

memories, and pieces of my identity. My memory box is like a time travel machine,

teleporting me to memories and manifesting future experiences.

The birthday cards comfort me when I'm upset and remind me there are always people

who care about me. I'm reminded of the card with cute stick figures from my best

friend's little sister. As a kid, I was excited to receive candy money in cards. Now, my

excitement comes from the heartfelt words written in them.

# The Healing Power of Art

## Decisions
- ✅ Harvard University
- ✅ Boston University
- ✅ Tufts University
- ❌ Yale University
- ❌ Princeton University

## Essay — Prompt #5
> Discuss an accomplishment, event, or realization that sparked a period of personal growth and a new understanding of yourself or others.

The first caress of a pigment-laden brush against a pristine canvas

ignites a feeling unlike any other. The initial plunge into a decadent

birthday cake or the satisfying crunch of a first footstep in fresh snow

might offer fleeting pleasures, but they can't replicate the immersive

experience I find at my easel. The pure joy of finger painting as a child

still resonates within me, even if my technique has matured

considerably.

Technique aside, the value of self-expression through artistic endeavor has

only grown for me as I mature. Many find cathartic release through

journaling or sharing their thoughts with others in conversation, but I feel

most connected to my feelings and the world when I put paint brush to

canvas. Not all sentiments can be captured in words, which is where art

takes over for me. Just as a piece of music can engender poignant

emotions in its listener, a piece of art can make a person feel seen in a

large and often lonely world.

Nobody knew this better than my middle school art teacher Mrs. Williams.

She often let me stay in the studio after school to put continuous rounds of

final touches on my latest masterpiece, knowing that sometimes my piece

did not need those additional strokes, but my soul did. A true artist herself,

Mrs. Williams understood how art could tell a story and that sometimes the

artist's need to tell their story in color and shape was more important than

the finished product. Over the years following middle school, I visited Mrs.

Williams every once in a while and each time was always like no time had

passed. We would set our easels side by side and paint, sometimes

chatting a bit, but often sitting in comfortable silence as we watched colors

blend and form new hues with the flick of a paint brush.

In the middle of my junior year of high school, I received the tragic news

that Mrs. Williams had suffered a massive heart attack and passed.

Devastated and trying to make sense of the first death I had ever

experienced, I turned to my mother for advice. <Well, how would you deal

with this if Mrs. Williams were here?= she asked me. Of course. I should

have known that was the answer to working through my grief. Grabbing my easel and a stool, I set up on the front porch where I could see the sun

filtering through the oak leaves in green and yellow shards of glass, smiled

at the memory of Mrs. Williams, and began to paint.

The world became a canvas of vibrant emotions as I poured my grief onto

the blank space in front of me. Each stroke was a tribute to Mrs. Williams, a

testament to the power of art as a universal language, and a reminder that

the creative spirit can endure even in the face of loss. The brush became

an extension of my heart, forever grateful for the teacher who nurtured my

artistic soul and showed me the profound connection between art and the

human experience.

## Decisions

- ❌ California Institute of Technology

## Essay — Prompt #1
> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

Many of the differences between people are on the outside. Visible. Like a lot of others who were

considered <different=, I knew it about myself from an early age. But my differences weren't on the

outside, and I never had a way to explain them until recently. Being neurodivergent is still a

misunderstood concept, and it applies to so many various conditions that it can be difficult to categorize.

For me, though, it encapsulates that feeling I've always had of being different.

Being a neurodivergent person has meaning to me because it has affected so much of my life, in good

ways and bad. But it has taught me many life lessons, too, that I don't think neurotypical people

experience often. As a child, without a way to communicate my way of thinking and experiencing the

world, I was given a few different labels. Some people thought I was a gifted child, because I loved to

read and I had an excellent memory. Others called me a problem, accused me of not paying attention or

listening. A standout incident for me, and that many others can relate to, I think, is when my sixth grade

English teacher forbade me from reading the books I chose during class free reading time. She thought it

was unfair of me to read what she considered too advanced. I had brought a well-loved copy of Lord of

the Rings with me to class, but since it was well above the reading level of my classmates, it was banned

by my teacher. At that time, I couldn't understand being punished for doing something I loved, and didn't

have the communication skills to express my frustration to her. Instead, I felt forced to sit in sullen silence

during class reading time, deliberately choosing not to read the approved sixth-grade reading level books

and being labeled as having a problem with authority.

These sorts of judgments made my scholastic life tricky, but my way of processing things made social

interactions awkward for me, too. Plenty of kids struggle with these things, but not all of us were able to

explain why4and that was the worst part. Not having an explanation for being the way you are and being

told all these negative things about yourself with no other alternative to believe. In sixth grade, I didn't

have the tools to explain that I was just doing what I loved, and that with the option taken away from me,

I didn't have an outlet for my feelings of frustration.

My experiences were a sort of blessing in disguise as I've grown older, though. Because I've had to

develop myself. To work on myself, specifically my social skills and communication style. And I've had

to learn how to turn my differences and my supposed weaknesses into strengths in ways others don't. I've

practiced turning my bluntness into clarity. My apparent aloofness into level-headedness and coolness

under pressure.

I've discovered ways to fit into the world in a way that works for me. It's given me not only my sense of

identity, but my assurance in myself and invaluable soft skills like empathy, self-management,

self-discipline, and collaboration. Being a neurodivergent person has also allowed me to develop my

creative, innovative mind, to see the world through different lens. My perspective is unique, and it's

opened my eyes to possibilities that others don't see.

My identity as a neurodivergent person and my background are incredibly meaningful to my past and to

my future. I think the world needs more people like me, who see and experience things a bit differently.

And I know the self-discovery I've gone through with this process is a solid foundation on which to build

my future.


---

# Standing Up for What's Right: My Experience with Moral Dilemmas

## Applicant Profile

## Decisions

- ✅ Stanford University

## Essay — Prompt #2
> The lessons we take from obstacles we encounter can be fundamental to later success. Recount a time when you faced a challenge, setback, or failure. How did it affect you, and what did you learn from the experience?

I sit in a hot SUV winding it's way over a bumpy African road, a scarf protecting my nose and mouth as dust streams in through the window. Returning from a teaching session with the Maasai women, the other students' excited chatter dances around me as they discuss our invitation to the Maasai coming of age ceremony. The ceremony centers on the circumcision of pubescent males and females; often performed with a sharp rock and no anesthetic. It is a rite of passage for the Maasai. My stomach is a tight knot, picturing the children we met today and imagining the painful procedure they will soon undergo. The other students, excited about the feast and intricate costumes, hope that accepting the invitation will strengthen our bond with the community. I, however, am weighed down by a profound sense of unease when it comes to the main attraction, the circumcisions. Further, the leader of the organization is absent; should she not be consulted? Do I go along with the group, and participate in something that I am morally opposed to? Or do something about it?

For me, the strength of a person's character is defined by their ability to act on their values and stand up for what they believe in. Having strong moral values only becomes a powerful agent of change when one is willing to follow through on them with action. Situations, such as this one, where I feel a sinking sensation deep in my gut, help to cue me to conflicts with my own values, prompting me to gather more information, thus taking the first step towards informed action.

In this situation, the knots in my stomach came from being asked to participate in the celebration of female genital mutilation; a practice which is decidedly against my personal values of reducing human suffering and promoting women's rights. My visceral reaction came specifically from the idea of watching while doing nothing to intervene. Further, I worried that, as students, our group would be woefully ill-equipped to navigate the nuances of the situation, potentially resulting in harm to our relationship with the community. Plus, due to our association with a medical organization, our presence could be mis-interpreted as an endorsement of the safety of these procedures. With the potential to do harm and without an actionable plan in place for stopping genital mutilation, I concluded that I could not, in good conscience, attend the ceremony.

Though I had decided that I could not go, I still felt concerned about the potential impact of the group's attendance, and wanted to gain more insight into the situation before deciding on a course of action. I shared my concerns with my partner and another student. My partner agreed with me, and we decided to consult his physician father. We quickly learned that Canadian physicians are not legally permitted to condone female genital mutilation, meaning that our attending the ceremony could have legal ramifications for our physician-run organization. With this information in hand, I knew I had to contact the organization lead about the excursion. She forbid our group from attending, requesting that I inform the other students, who were obviously disappointed that I had gotten the trip cancelled.

Though I believe my course of action was the right one and I would not change the outcome, looking back, I wish I had voiced my concerns earlier; it may have made the end result easier for the other students to swallow. In spite of this, being honest when expressing my discomfort with a situation and choosing an alternative course of action that is aligned with my values has never led me to make a decision that I regret. Though standing up for what you believe in, and doing what is right, is not always easy, it is always worth it, and arguably the only way of living a life without regrets.


---

## Essay — Prompt #7
> Share an essay on any topic of your choice. It can be one you've already written, one that responds to a different prompt, or one of your own design.

Gioacchino Rossini, the famous nineteenth-century composer, frantically waves his arms at me. I'm

warbling through one of his pieces, music filling my entire body and shimmering in the air around me,

connecting my entire being to the floor, the ground, the earth.

But when I complete the aria, all he says is, Not good enough.

I sigh, exasperated, and as my voice subsides, so does the vision. And just like that, I'm back in front of a music stand.

When I started taking singing lessons from Simona, an Italian woman with a spectacular voice that

could shatter glass, I certainly didn't expect to be singing arias. I'd imagined opera as booming songs

sung by women in Marie Antoinette-like costumes and huge wigs. But four years ago, Simona played a

YouTube video of world-famous opera singer Cecilia Bartoli gracefully gliding from note to note, then

turned to me and said, You can do that.

It seemed ludicrous, but as I waded into opera, all I could think about was being like Bartoli, filling a

room with beauty and sound, sending shivers down listeners' spines, evoking emotions that ripple

through every single note.

Since then, my life has been an endless cycle of learning new music, rehearsing, performing, and

competing. Though at times tedious, this journey brings me that much closer to the musical giants of the

past, and, therefore, closer to grasping the brilliant, shining center of music itself.

Singing opera transports me to the composer's era. As I jump up the scale in La Pastorella delle Alpi,

my clothing transforms into an eighteenth-century shepherdess frock and my arms follow delicate hand

movements as if I am the character in the song. I'm no longer standing before a piano,

but in a field of tall grasses swaying in a calm breeze, sheep lazily grazing, the sun casting a warm glow

over the world. The music seems to break all constraints of the space-time continuum.

Opera is a story, one that I have the privilege of embodying as soon as the music starts. But

simultaneously I feel like a fraud. I'm not part of some big opera company. I'm a regular teenage girl with

a never-ending rotation of t-shirts and jeans, not a professional opera singer.

Two years ago, my choir performed Carmina Burana with the Oakland Symphony. That night, a

millennial male opera singer soloed chunks of the piece, but all I could focus on was his waist-length

hair, button-down shirt that hung open throughout the performance, and Elton John sunglasses.

His appearance sent my fellow choristers and me into hysterics as soon as we filed off stage. But he was

waiting for us, offering congratulations and even blessing me with a fist bump. That strange little

interaction proved to me that opera singers come in all shapes, sizes, and appearances.

From soaring arias to baroque coloratura portamentos, opera is and will always be a core part of who I

am, and I strive to improve until I arrive in that little Italian villa, or sun-dappled meadow, or ornate

chateau.


---

## Decisions
- ✅ Yale University
- ✅ Columbia University
- ❌ Harvard University

## Essay — Prompt #3
> Reflect on a time when you questioned or challenged a belief or idea. What prompted your thinking? What was the outcome?

Throughout my childhood, I was bombarded with a relentless chorus of shoulds, steered down paths paved with my parents' expectations. Frustration gnawed at me, leaving me disoriented. It felt like my destiny was preordained, a script I was forced to follow. But I craved escape.

My refuge became the vibrant world of two-dimensional animation. There, Miku Hatsune captivated millions with her flawless performances, her adorable emotions radiating through the screen. Homura Akemi, fueled by love, wielded magic to protect her world. And by day, Victorica buried herself in books, transforming into a brilliant detective by night. Inspired by their unique outfits, extraordinary abilities, and thrilling adventures, I found myself captivated by cosplay. It wasn't just dressing up; it was the chance to forge new, exciting identities for myself, to rewrite the preordained narrative.

Hunting for gorgeous costumes online, designing accessories, and sporting colorful wigs, I began to fulfill my wildest imaginations. Soon I discovered these cute, vivacious teenage-girl characters enjoyed a tremendous popularity, as all the posts of my cosplay photos received countless likes on social media. Drunk off compliments, I clung to the belief that cosplayer had become my newfound identity.

My self-appreciation in cosplaying was, however, soon challenged when a senior cosplayer asked me a simple question. Who is the favorite character you've cosplayed as?

Though a self-proclaimed cosplay zealot, I was startled that my mind went blank. I had cosplayed most of the characters solely because they were attractive and popular, and for the number of likes popping up on my personal page. Even in my safe haven, I was doing nothing different than in the three-dimensional reality: fulfilling other's expectations and becoming who they wanted me to be.

Yet, this time I resolved not to escape. I began to search for a character I truly adored, and finally came across Reborn. Cosplaying this character was an unprecedented challenge: for starters, Reborn was a male character, which meant I needed to cross-dress; and like his pet chameleon, he was constantly shifting his image. As I strutted in man's attire, with the cruelest grin one moment and the sweetest laughter the next, I felt not at all awkward but instead empowered. The magic within cosplay lies not in pleasing people's eyes, but in the incessant exploration into identities that sometimes cannot be accurately defined.

In retrospect, I see all endeavors in locating one's identity as acts of cosplaying. We don garments sometimes of our own choice, and sometimes out of others' expectation. Yet in both situations, we are down the path of exploring ourselves. And as I look beyond, I aspire to see what further characters I am about to cosplay as when I set foot in the States.


---

## Decisions

- ❌ Stanford University

## Essay — Prompt #1
> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

My pristine M7652-000 Chuck Taylors, a beacon of highlight yellow, were a symbol of wanting to fit in during my freshman year. But life has a way of scuffing the best-laid plans. Dirt, guitar strings, and late-night conversations became the paint on this ever-evolving canvas. Back in eighth grade, moving to Houston, I clung to the white-sneaker ideal. Yet, as the city's rhythm clashed with my Portland roots, just like the discord between LCD Soundsystem and Ed Sheeran, I learned the power of embracing different perspectives. Houston had stories to tell, lessons to share, and I craved the same for myself.

As I walk, one can first make out Cheyenne yo yo engulfing the right inner canvas, weaving through clasps and eventually boarding PORTLAND!!! I met Cheyenne through Freshman year volleyball and we were friends because I tried; I borrowed cowboy boots for football games, didn't discuss my quirky music, and washed my shoes. As I grew, however, it was our differences that brought us together. She forced me to see the other side, forced me to make my own conclusions without the influence of my background or parents.

In Portland, opinions are liberally voiced. When I moved, I was bombarded by a completely foreign culture. By sophomore year, however, I realized that compromising myself in order to fit in was a mistake. I began vocally expressing my sentiments towards the world to my friends as I learned from theirs. While I introduced my friends to thrift-shopping and wrote articles about more environmentally friendly methods of transportation, they took me to my first line-dance.

My left shoe houses various meme references, chords from songs I have written, sketches of the latest NASA star cluster discoveries, practice lines of Italian greetings from when I was set on learning it, and Lorrie Lake Ln. in small cursive letters. Sandalwood, my friends and I call it--a late-night, post-fast food, teen-angst polluted lake. Sandalwood is the cosmos and the meaning of God and the Sisyphus-like emotions that we discuss there.

When I was hurled into Texas, I was miserable when I didn't express myself within the bubble. However, I quickly began to realize that I didn't have to like Ed Sheeran or keep my shoes M7652-000 to enjoy life. Learning to embrace and assess so many dissonating ideas has enabled me to grow more into myself--it makes me more nonpartisan and has educated me on what it truly means to listen to the other side.

## Decisions

## Essay — Prompt #1
> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

The tiny kitchen barely contained Titi Nana as she smashed an egg onto the sizzling pan. The sunshine yellow yolk stood out starkly against the dark, worn metal. With a flick of her wrist, she sent a stream of fiery liquor dancing across the surface. Get rid of it all, she commanded, her eyes twinkling. I chuckled, a touch of unease in my laugh, as I brushed the lingering troubles invisible but heavy from my shoulders, bit by bit, into the cleansing flames.

I left Titi's apartment that day confused about how our family's practice of Santeria fit in with my outward embrace of my heritage. I felt as if the parts of my Latina identity I claimed openly -- dancing salsa to Celia Cruz or enjoying lechon y arroz con habichuelas en Navidad -- were contradicted by my skepticism towards Titi's rituals. My experience with Santeria wasn't new, as proven by my mother's kitchen altar lit dimly by prayer candles and adorned with evil eyes, statues of San Miguel, and offerings to Elegua; however, I'd never before witnessed such a tangible demonstration of my family's ritualistic beliefs.

The sterile halls of the mostly white boarding school swallowed me whole. Separated from the rich tapestry of traditions woven by my family, I refused to acknowledge the growing chasm between myself and my roots. Everything Latina became a forbidden zone, fueled by a terror of failing to live up to my Puerto Rican ancestry.

In an effort to build myself back up, I began to practice the small bits of Santeria that I comprehended: lighting candles for good luck, placing a chalice of water by my bedside to absorb all maldad, and saying my prayers to San Miguel and my guardian angels each day. To my disbelief, the comments that attacked my Latinidad faded along with the aching feeling that I had failed to represent my heritage.

Santeria once served as my scapegoat; I blamed the discomfort I felt towards black magic for the imposter syndrome festering inside me. Until I embraced Santeria, it only served as a reminder that I wasn't Latina enough. Now, I understand that while intangible, ethereal, even, the magic of Santeria is real; it's the strength of my belief in myself, in my culture, and in my commitment to protect others.

# From Rejection to Success: A Journey of Resilience

## Applicant Profile

| Application Year | 2023/2024 |

| Ethnicity | N/A |

| IB Score | N/A |

## Decisions

- ✅ Harvard University
- ✅ Princeton University
- ❌ Stanford University
- ❌ Columbia University

## Essay — Prompt #2
> The lessons we take from obstacles we encounter can be fundamental to later success. Recount a time when you faced a challenge, setback, or failure. How did it affect you, and what did you learn from the experience?

The day my debut novel was rejected, I found myself immersed in pie-baking. To be exact, I was gearing up for our church's yearly pie sale. Ten grueling hours of rolling dough, peeling apples, and blending butter and sugar into crumble topping, all while enveloped in a cloud of cinnamon aroma and encircled by towering heaps of pies we were strictly forbidden to sample.

I sat on my couch and counted the minutes until the agony of pie-making, almost forgetting the novel that was currently with the acquisitions board of one of the biggest publishing houses in the world.

To be fair, I hadn't known that the acquisitions meeting would be held that day. I did know that two senior editors wanted to make all of my impossible dreams come true. I knew that the marketing and sales people had already looked over my manuscript. I knew the meeting had been pushed back twice already by an unsympathetic hurricane that had left downtown Manhattan under several feet of water. This had to be it. It was my turn.

I had slogged through the query trenches in search of an agent. I had collected enough rejection letters to wallpaper my room. I had found an agent who hadn't run away when I finally told her that I was 15, who loved my story almost as much as I did.

Hello, future? I'm ready for my happily ever after. Love, Amy.

Phone call from my agent. Sweaty palms and dizziness. Small talk and stalling. A sigh and, at last, the news, that the publisher had a similar novel on her list and vetoed the editors. Stomach in throat, swallow. False laugh, assurances of next time. End call. Tears.

Hello, Amy? Sucks, doesn't it? Love, the future.

It sucked so monumentally that I bought a pie and ate it in one sitting.

It'll be okay. It'll be okay. I fell asleep like that: okay, okay, okay, and I almost believed it. After all, the next day was the beginning of National Novel Writing Month.

A ringing in the ungodly hours of morning. Phone call from a friend. Bleary eyes and words still spinning: okay, okay, okay. A classmate, a car out of control, a crash into a tree.

We used to have gym together, I didn't know him too well, and I never would. The words were gone. I sat at my computer with my fingers on the keys, shaking, sweating, smudging, but there was nothing to say.

Everyone went to the memorial service and everyone brought flowers, and in the silence, we cried. I went home after the service and threw my laptop open and wrote about all that was unfair, and there was a lot to write about. The month passed, and I won NaNoWriMo. I revised the novel and sent it to my agent who began the submission process once again.

It sold in three days.

Hello, future? I'm not afraid. Love, Amy.


---

## Applicant Profile

## Decisions

- ✅ Johns Hopkins University
- ✅ University of Georgia
- ✅ Vanderbilt University
- ✅ University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill
- ✅ University of Virginia
- ✅ Princeton University
- ✅ University of Wisconsin - Madison
- ✅ University of Michigan - Ann Arbor
- ✅ University of Miami
- ✅ Pennsylvania State University

## Essay — Prompt #3
> Reflect on a time when you questioned or challenged a belief or idea. What prompted your thinking? What was the outcome?

For most of my life, I lived in the comfortable rigidity of the binary. As a competitive coder and a student of formal logic, I believed that every problem possessed a discrete, verifiable solution. My world was constructed of 1s and 0s, if-then statements, and the satisfying click of a closed circuit. I applied this Logic First dogma to everything: my friendships were balanced like ledgers, my hobbies were optimized for maximum efficiency, and my belief system was a fortress of objective facts. I was convinced that if an idea couldn't be quantified or proven, it was a distraction. This belief was challenged not by a grand philosophical debate, but by a 19th-century oil painting and a very patient art conservator.

The summer before my junior year, I volunteered at a local museum's restoration lab. I expected to find a world of chemical formulas and precise wavelengths of light. Instead, I met Elena, a conservator who was working on a heavily damaged landscape. When I asked her what the correct color was for a faded section of the sky, she didn't give me a HEX code or a pigment ratio. She leaned back and said, There is no correct color, only the one that honors the ghost of the original.

I felt a surge of intellectual irritation. Honoring a ghost was not a metric. I argued that we could use spectral analysis to determine the exact chemical composition of the 1880s paint and replicate it with 100% accuracy.

Elena smiled and handed me a palette. If you paint it with 100% accuracy, you create a lie. You'd be painting what it looked like on day one, but the rest of the canvas has lived through a hundred years of smoke, sunlight, and humidity. If you make that one spot perfect, you make the whole painting look wrong.

That moment was the catalyst for a total collapse of my binary worldview. I realized that my obsession with objective truth was actually a form of myopia. The truth of the painting wasn't found in the chemical formula of the paint, but in the relationship between the faded pigments and the rest of the aging canvas. It lived in the in-between.

This realization rippled through my life outside the lab. I began to see where my logic-only belief had failed me. In my robotics club, I stopped dismissing aesthetic design choices as inefficient and started seeing how the form of a machine influenced how humans interacted with it. In my personal life, I stopped treating disagreements with my younger brother as debates to be won.

I have not abandoned logic. I still find peace in an elegant line of code or a solved equation. However, I no longer believe that the binary is sufficient to describe the human experience. I've traded the fortress of 1s and 0s for the workshop of the conservator. I've learned to sit with ambiguity, to appreciate the faded parts of a story.

Now, when I look at a problem, I don't just ask What is the answer? I ask, What is the context? I've learned that life isn't a program to be debugged; it's a canvas to be restored, one nuanced, subjective stroke at a time.


---

## Essay — Prompt #5
> Discuss an accomplishment, event, or realization that sparked a period of personal growth and a new understanding of yourself or others.

I always hated when people colored outside the lines. In first grade, it absolutely infuriated me when my classmates lacked the simple spatial awareness skills to stay inside the lines. To me, stray marks were mistakes, and mistakes meant a loss of control. Coloring inside the lines was my way of controlling things because my life was so chaotic.

My biological mother was never constant in my life, so it wasn't unexpected when I came home from school one day to an emptier house than usual. I didn't fully understand what had happened until I saw my Papa cry for the first time. At six years old, I couldn't control that, and that helplessness terrified me. And so I clung to the few things I could control, like making sure I colored inside the lines.

As I grew older, my need for control spilled over into my life. I made it my mission to make others happy, believing that if I could bring joy to those around me, I could somehow keep everything from falling apart again. I smiled through my pain, convinced that pretending everything was fine was better than the reality of my broken family. I was living my life for everyone else but me.

Everything changed when I met Amma, my stepmother, who became more of a mother to me than anyone else ever had. In third grade, I asked her what her dream daughter would be like, expecting a list of qualities I could strive to meet. Instead, she told me, If you can look at yourself honestly and be happy with who you are, then you are my dream daughter. That simple statement made me realize: I needed to start living for myself.

With this realization, I began to let go of my need to please everyone else and started focusing on what genuinely made me happy. I played sports because I like being proud of my own accomplishments. I picked up the guitar because it brought me peace. I challenged myself academically, excited about a future where I could do something meaningful and impactful.

# Calm Persistence: Lessons from the Lake

## Applicant Profile

## Decisions

- ✅ Duke University
- ✅ Brandeis University
- ✅ Amherst College
- ✅ University of Pennsylvania
- ✅ University of Wisconsin - Madison
- ❌ Cornell University
- ❌ Princeton University

## Essay — Prompt #1
> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

Keep going! my uncle shouted as the current pulled me sideways. My arms already felt like rubber bands, and every breath burned, but I wasn't ready to quit. I was twelve, swimming across the lake with my cousins, and what was supposed to be a fun challenge suddenly felt like a battle. About halfway across, I realized I was gasping too fast and totally out of rhythm.

For a moment, fear almost got me. But then I remembered something my uncle always told me: Don't fight the water, work with it. I flipped onto my back, floated, and just stared up at the endless sky until my heart slowed. Then I rolled over, took slower strokes, and stopped racing against myself. Somehow, stroke by stroke, I made it. That day I learned persistence isn't about brute force. It's about listening, adapting, and staying calm when panic rises.

That idea has shown up again and again in my life. In eighth grade, I joined the debate team even though I had no clue what I was doing. At my first tournament, my hands shook so much I could barely shuffle my notecards. Halfway through my opening, I blanked out completely and just stood there staring at the judge. Every part of me wanted to walk out. But the lake came back to me. I took a deep breath, smiled awkwardly, and started over. We lost, but I stayed. And staying turned out to be the most important part.

Over time, debate became something I actually loved. By junior year, I was team captain, helping younger debaters the way my uncle once helped me. I still remember a freshman who froze in her first round. She looked at me with wide eyes, and I told her what I had once needed to hear: Don't fight it. Take a breath. Start again.

The same rhythm carried into my family life too. When my grandmother's health started declining, I spent hours after school with her, cooking simple meals, keeping track of her medicines, or just sitting with her while she told stories. Balancing AP classes, debate practices, and family responsibilities was like swimming against a strong current. But I found ways to make it work.

Now, as I look toward college, I keep going back to that day on the lake. Calm persistence is stronger than fear. And that belief is what steadies me as I take the next step.

## Essay — Prompt #2
> The lessons we take from obstacles we encounter can be fundamental to later success. Recount a time when you faced a challenge, setback, or failure. How did it affect you, and what did you learn from the experience?

Amidst the chaos of cracked eggs, overbeaten yolks, fears, and failures, I found myself confronting the fear of imperfection that once dictated my life.

I frantically separated the egg whites from the yolks, my hands fumbling clumsily. I watched in horror as the egg yolk burst into the bowl of egg whites. Perfection meant a flawless dessert to impress the guests, but tonight, I would serve failure. I tried again, this time splitting the whites from the yolks perfectly. Next, I tried to whisk the whites into soft peaks. A whole minute. Nothing but a soupy froth stared back at me. I had overbeat the whites.

Two attempts, 15 eggs, 60 minutes wasted, no dessert. My mind began to spiral, reminding me of my failures. This wasn't just about eggs. This was about the basketball game where I missed a free throw. The tennis match where my serves were either a double fault or out. The performance at school where I couldn't hit that high note.

Perfection to me meant excelling in everything, but instead of quitting, I saw each failure as an opportunity for growth. I realized that perfection isn't about flawless performance; it's about persistence. Embracing the process, learning from mistakes and pushing through challenges is more valuable.

With this reminder, I tried to salvage the egg whites. Thankfully, the overbeaten whites had separated, leaving a foam I could still use.

Then I discovered Environmental Systems and Societies. What began as a school subject became a passion, altering my perfect plan. Attending COP28 inspired me to pursue a career in sustainability. Volunteering for plantation activities, cleanups and field research heightened my passion further. But every time I plunged my hands into the soil, there was fear of the insects beneath.

As I served the mousse that evening, everyone enjoyed it. The mousse, despite the chaos behind its creation, symbolized my broader journey. Each experience taught me that life isn't about perfection. It's about embracing the process, finding joy in the mess, and growing stronger with each challenge.

## Essay — Prompt #1
> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

Growing up as one of two boys and one sister, I couldn't do anything except watch and see the uneven distribution of work at home. While my brother and I just sit and play, my sisters are tasked with all the household chores. This division wasn't seen as unfair but it was a natural thing as it seemed like girls were expected to handle the behind-the-scenes work. This shaped my belief that women are destined to support as they are less capable while men pursue their important things. This expectation changed when I started school and met girls who were academically excellent than me.

One morning at home my sister was supposed to do all chores while me and my brother were doing other things. I never questioned why because the division of work seemed normal. My brother and I always joked about how lucky we were to not do home chores. I didn't realize that my sister's role was to support us from behind while we got to aim for greater things.

I went to school with the same assumption boys are stronger and smarter. But as I continued with my studies, I began to notice that many of the top students in the class were girls. Those who were not only better than me in principal courses like Maths but also organized and driven. It wasn't just their academic capabilities that was changing my perception but I started to see that strength wasn't how you could lift something. These girls were hardworking, good leaders, and more disciplined.

I saw that everyone regardless of their gender has strengths and weaknesses. It became clear that qualities and character mattered, intelligence and hard work weren't tied to whether you were a boy or a girl. This realization made me reflect on the unfairness my sister was experiencing at home. I began to help her by doing some household chores not because it was a fair thing to do. But because I have learned that strength isn't about who does the chore but it's about respect and equal opportunities.

## Essay — Prompt #1
> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

The sound of sirens pierced the air, but inside my head it was quiet. Too quiet. My thoughts had frozen, like pieces of a puzzle scattered on the floor, impossible to put together. Just hours earlier, I had been excited about a simple vacation in Washington, D.C. Now I was following an ambulance carrying my mother into an unfamiliar hospital, seven hours from home, with no idea what would happen next.

I was fourteen. I didn't have a driver's license. I didn't have nearby relatives. What I did have was a sudden, crushing fear of losing the one person I depended on most.

My mother needed three blood transfusions before she was stable enough for surgery. In those first long hours, I felt completely powerless. But gradually, fear turned into something else. I began talking with her doctors, writing down what they told me so I wouldn't forget. I called her medical team back in North Carolina and arranged the emergency operation she needed. Faith and optimism were all I could offer her in that waiting room.

The surgery saved her life. But when we finally returned home, I realized the crisis wasn't over. It had only changed shape.

Recovery left my mom exhausted most days, and suddenly I was the one running the household. I biked to the grocery store and balanced bags on my handlebars. I kept track of her medication and made trips to the pharmacy. I cooked dinner and I reassured my younger sister when she was scared.

The shift was jarring. I didn't feel ready for it, but there was no choice except to keep going. I stumbled plenty of times, but each day gave me more proof that I could handle things I never imagined handling at fourteen. What started as survival slowly became growth.

That summer didn't just take me from middle school to high school. It pulled me from childhood into something else. I had always thought independence would mean freedom. But I learned that real independence often comes when you take responsibility, when you realize that someone else is counting on you.

Even now, years later, the memory of almost losing my mother shapes my days. We are closer than ever. Each morning, I get up ten minutes earlier than necessary just to eat breakfast with her. Those quiet moments remind me how quickly life can change.

I know now that strength isn't something you wait for; it's something you discover when circumstances leave you no other option. That discovery, though born out of fear, became the beginning of my independence.


---

## Essay — Prompt #5
> Discuss an accomplishment, event, or realization that sparked a period of personal growth and a new understanding of yourself or others.

Nak (kid), you overcharged me! one mom snapped. My manager stepped in, looking even more frustrated. We had three people complain to customer service today. We need to get things right. No more mistakes!

You might be wondering how I got here. I was planning my summer when to my surprise, my father suggested I work at a grocery store. A few weeks later, I entered the grocery store with full confidence, naively picturing a simple job with minimal challenges. I approached the job with my typical perfectionist mindset: work quickly with zero mistakes.

Immediately, I realized that the challenges of this job were far different from the challenges that the classroom presented. I was assigned to the fruit counter where a labyrinth of product codes confounded me. I was obsessed with getting them right, yet I was constantly entering the wrong codes in. Each mistake amplified the frustrations of the customers and myself.

I was struggling, yet I was too afraid to ask for help, making it more difficult. Admitting that I was not good enough was embarrassing. My insistence on appearing as perfect and self-reliant was related to my self-worth as a student. At school, I was afraid to show my flaws and weaknesses because performance is equated to personal value.

One weekend, an angry customer slammed a bag of mangoes onto the weighing scale, frustrated that I had used the wrong sticker label. As I braced myself for further condemnation, my co-worker, Irene, stepped in and apologized for my mistakes. I was surprised. In school, it was everyone for themselves. But instead of reprimanding me, Irene told me about her early mistakes during her first few days on the job, and she assured me that my co-workers never cared about blame. They just wanted to fix things together.

My co-worker's empathy showed me the faults of my original approach and the power of being part of the team. Her leadership helped me realize that my initial insecurity was holding me back from learning from others. From then on, I recognized that mistakes are meant to be embraced and asking for help is part of the learning experience.

I've learned that every mistake is a learning opportunity which values perseverance, honesty and humility more than outcome. The experience taught me how empathy can transform a stressful situation into a learning experience. There is care and responsibility in every job, and I carry these values with me beyond the grocery store.


---

## Essay — Prompt #7
> Share an essay on any topic of your choice. It can be one you've already written, one that responds to a different prompt, or one of your own design.

Awakened by a jolt at three in the morning, my six-year-old hand extends in autopilot. There it is, nestled on my pillow right where I left it two hours ago. Time to get to work! I tiptoe carefully into my parents' bedroom, knowing that my actions could ensure the well-being of my family, a responsibility I take to heart even at such a young age. Impatience takes over me as I await its final verdict: will it flash red (a cause for immediate concern), yellow (a sign that something is amiss), or green (a reassurance that everything's OK)?

My grandmother jokingly calls it my best friend, and perhaps she's onto something. With my trusty thermometer, I'd always been prepared to read a situation and quickly jump into action. The numerical value it provided, assuring me that the situation is comprehensible and within my control, represents the harmony and stability I've always valued in my life.

As I grew older, I abandoned my physical thermometer, internalizing its functions instead as I pushed myself to read the temperature of people and situations. I no longer agonized only over the health of my family; the world itself became what I wanted to bring to equilibrium. By learning new languages, I ensured I remained in the know of a situation. This need to understand also propelled me on the conference floor in PANAMUN, as I gauged the debate's temperature and surgically guided discussion to the best possible conclusion.

But then, my thermometer began malfunctioning, giving me false positives in interpersonal situations. Whether at home, zealously guarding passports whenever my brother and I traveled for fear of him losing his, or at school, struggling to keep up with my favorite sports without sacrificing my academic health, it felt more and more as if my thermometer had lost its balance.

By focusing only on exterior readings, however, I had forgotten to point the thermometer where it mattered most: at myself. When I finally did, the symptoms felt conclusive. My constant search for stability had given me tunnel vision, as I became oblivious to information not communicated by my internal sensor.

Today, I point the thermometer inwards and it flashes yellow. It signals more than just caution: it confirms the fact that there are things I don't know, or can't control, and that's okay. My thermometer is still indispensable to me, but it no longer dictates my actions. I now know that what matters most is not the reading, but how I make the most of it.


---

## Essay — Prompt #1
> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

Messy handwriting, broken pencils, and paper airplanes.

That was the landscape of my fourth-grade desk. If you had asked me then, I would have told you I was destined to be an inventor. My notebooks were filled with sketches of gadgets I thought would change the world: automatic dog-walkers, self-erasing whiteboards, even a backpack drone that could fly me to school so I'd never miss the bus again. Most of these blueprints were disasters in ink, with arrows pointing in every direction and notes like ?? how to power??? written in the margins.

By middle school, however, I started to notice that my inventions weren't seen as impressive, they were weird. Classmates raised eyebrows, teachers chuckled politely. Once, a kid flipped through my notebook and asked, Why do you waste your time with this? His question echoed in my head, and slowly, my sketches disappeared.

But neatness never really worked for me. My ideas were too restless. They bubbled up during math class, or during orchestra rehearsals, where I once imagined a violin bow that could tune itself mid-performance. It felt safer to keep my what-ifs inside rather than risk the smirks.

Then came a science fair in seventh grade. I cobbled together a clumsy prototype of a smart pencil holder that lit up when a pencil was missing. The wiring barely worked, the tape peeled at the edges. But when the lights flickered on during my demonstration, a group of younger kids gasped. They didn't see the flaws; they saw magic. That gasp stayed with me longer than the participation ribbon I took home.

Since then, I've stopped treating my messy ideas as something to hide. I launched a tiny Maker Mondays club in our school library, where anyone could bring half-baked ideas and we'd try to build them together. One Monday, we ended up fashioning a cardboard bridge that held ten textbooks before collapsing. Another time, a seventh grader brought in a shoebox contraption she called a mouse hotel.

Looking back, I realize I never stopped being the kid with the crowded desk and crooked blueprints. The difference is, I no longer see weird as a label to avoid; it's a starting point. Standing out isn't about inventing something flawless. It's about showing up with the courage to share half-formed sparks, and trusting that a community will help fan them into something brighter.

## Essay — Prompt #2
> The lessons we take from obstacles we encounter can be fundamental to later success. Recount a time when you faced a challenge, setback, or failure. How did it affect you, and what did you learn from the experience?

I am definitely going to sink. That thought pulsed through my mind as I clawed my way to the edge of the pool, gasping. Chlorine stung my eyes, but it couldn't mask the deeper sting in my shoulder; a dull, persistent ache that had been growing for weeks. I had tried to ignore it, convincing myself it was just fatigue. But now, as I winced through another lap during the final practice before regionals, I realized I wasn't just tired. I was hurt.

Swimming had always been my safe space. As the only girl on my club team who wore a swim cap over cornrows, I had grown used to standing out. It never bothered me, until it did. One afternoon, after a rough race, a teammate joked that maybe I wasn't built for this sport. I laughed it off. But later that night, staring at my ceiling, those words echoed in my mind: was I pushing through the pain because I loved swimming, or because I needed to prove that I belonged?

Each practice became a war between pride and pain. I iced my shoulder, rotated stretches, downplayed the discomfort, until I couldn't anymore. Coach, I whispered, blinking back frustration, I think I need to stop. That sentence broke something in me. But in that silence, I found something more valuable than stubbornness: honesty.

My grandmother, a retired physicist who immigrated from Ghana, always said: Precision matters, in experiments and in life. She reminded me that listening to your body is a form of precision too. That it takes strength to stop before breaking.

Stepping back from swimming meant letting go of control. But it also gave me time to rediscover why I loved the sport in the first place. I started volunteering with WaveMakers, a learn-to-swim program for younger kids. Watching them float for the first time rekindled something in me. I wasn't chasing medals anymore. I was sharing joy.

That season, I missed the championship meet. But I gained something deeper: perspective. Now, I know that pausing doesn't mean failure; it means wisdom. And that sometimes, choosing the slower lane is the fastest way to grow.

- ✅ Haverford College

## Essay — Prompt #1
> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

The first time I walked across the old Nyabarongo footbridge, I believed it was utterly invincible. Wooden planks rattled beneath my sandals, muddy water hissed below, and my grandmother squeezed my shoulder, murmuring, Hold the rope, mwana wanjye. I was eight, and the bridge was half magic, half menace.

Three years later the bridge collapsed after a storm. No one was hurt, but the village felt cut off. I watched adults gather at the bank, shaking heads. My uncle said, We will wait for engineers. I thought: why wait?

That afternoon I searched how to build a suspension bridge, and fell into a universe of load paths and tensile forces. Within a week I gathered five friends, three rusty nails, bamboo poles, and sisal rope. Our prototype stretched two meters over the drainage ditch behind my house; when my sister tiptoed across and stayed dry, she shouted, It works! My heart cracked open.

The adults laughed. Children and their games, my mother sighed. But she didn't stop us. Instead she said, If you are serious, learn why bridges fall before you build ones that stand. Her challenge became my compass.

I began haunting the public library every Saturday. Terms like shear, moment, and fatigue grew less like threats and more like tools.

That rainy season, swollen waters cut off the Saturday market ferry. Elderly neighbors, forced to wade, slipped on slick stones. My friends and I salvaged timber and built a temporary foot-railing along the shallows. For three weeks it kept people upright. One morning Ms. Uwimana, the baker, pressed a loaf into my hands. You saved my knees, she laughed. I realized then that engineering is not abstract math; it is dry shoes, spared knees, and community moving freely forward together.

Back at school I started Span, an after-class club for building models from scrap. Our first exhibition drew skepticism until a teacher stacked three textbooks on a truss and it held. Applause followed.

Last December a new steel footbridge opened at Nyabarongo. I didn't design it, but I attended the opening. While children dashed across, I knelt, tracing a girder. I whispered, I'm still listening.

Now I see bridges everywhere: between classroom and community, dream and blueprint. The bamboo model rotted long ago, yet its lesson endures: start without permission, finish with knowledge.


---

- ❌ Stanford University
- ❌ Columbia University

## Essay — Prompt #7
> Share an essay on any topic of your choice. It can be one you've already written, one that responds to a different prompt, or one of your own design.

I am no stranger to change. I was born and raised in California with strong Mexican influence, to Lebanese and Moroccan parents, and later moved to Spain. Just as I start to settle into one identity, I am often swept off my feet again. Yet, even when I've felt my world turned upside down, music has remained a constant.

I first heard Rosas by La Oreja de Van Gogh on the iPod that lived on our kitchen counter, from which the Mexican hits of Mana and Romeo Santos permeated through our home. The melodies blended with the warm aromas from the dinner Martha, my nanny and second mother, was preparing.

Although we aren't related by blood, the way she loved and cared for me made Mexican culture feel like home. She shared her culture with me effortlessly: eating tajin by the handful, bringing pan de muertos for my classmates, and belting Luis Miguel with Martha as we danced around the kitchen.

Yet, stepping outside my home, I found myself immersed in a different atmosphere. Driving down the I-5 in my mom's red Mustang convertible, the music shifted to the Red Hot Chili Peppers and Hotel California. With each guitar riff, a wave of freedom washed over me.

Fairuz's dramatic melodies and Nancy Ajram's pop hits made me realize that my mom also started her mornings listening to her favorite songs. Discovering the songs that shaped my parents' upbringings, I realized they weren't really so far from my own.

Though I've often felt too Arab to be American, too American to be Arab, and not Mexican enough to claim that part of myself at all, music has always been my connection. Just like the music I listen to, I am a mix of all that has impacted me.

## Essay — Prompt #1
> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

I was trembling with fear as I walked up to Odin, a dark bay gelding with a thunderbolt marking on his forehead. When I reached near, he flared his nostrils and stepped back, his large brown eyes locking onto mine. I froze in my tracks, my fear making way for panic. I felt that Odin was as afraid of me as I was of him. The formidable horse had sensed and mirrored my fear before I'd acknowledged it myself.

That was when I realized that animals have an uncanny ability to reflect human emotions. It was a language in itself. To understand animals, I needed to understand myself, my inner emotions and outward actions, or as we call it: human psychology.

I was barely nine years old, so I wasn't aware of the term but had figured out its meaning in truer terms. It was also my first time away from home in a horseback riding camp. I had never seen, touched, or rode a horse, but I was always fascinated with animals. I often brought stray puppies and kittens home after school. There I was, at Japalouppe Equestrian Centre in Maharashtra, India, trying to control my fear of Odin.

Japalouppe was a picture-perfect farm, and I felt at home soon after I learned to ride Odin. It led me to spend every weekend and holiday at the stables, grooming, feeding, mucking stalls, and caring for other farm animals. But more importantly, I was learning about the behavior of each animal as we forged a bond each day.

I didn't realize when I transformed from a shy child into a confident adolescent through these interactions, but they helped me pay closer attention to subtle cues, body language, and unspoken communication in my fellow campers. It prompted me to understand the connection between animal-assisted therapy and psychology, so I started interning at a therapy center called Pawsitive Synergies.

Looking back at my time at both Japalouppe and Pawsitive Synergies, I realize that life is messy, but if I can handle the dirt, the stink, and the occasional rebellious horse, I know I can handle anything.

## Essay — Prompt #1
> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

Forty kids salivated at the sight of the bright orange basketball in my hands. The whistle around my neck and the white shirt that read Coach supposedly conveyed my authority. Instead, I felt foolish.

I coached a weeklong basketball camp for kids from a rural village on the outskirts of Manila. On day one, all sides of the court were lined with food stalls: fried quail eggs and barbecue grilling under thick plumes of smoke. Speakers blasted Pinoy rap songs, while teens rode motorcycles to and from each baseline, unconcerned that Holy Mass was being celebrated in the church next door. Amid this charming chaos, overwhelmed, I stood frozen.

I felt foolish that I couldn't speak the language of my home country. Since middle school, I've volunteered with orphanages in Manila such as Child Hope and the Virlanie Foundation, where I learned to connect with local kids through our mutual love of basketball. This gave me the confidence that I could coach regardless of the language barrier.

After an overwhelming start, a small boy approached me and asked, Taga saan ka? (Where are you from?). I paused for a second. I'm from Hyderabad. He looked puzzled because he assumed that, since I had an American accent and towered over everyone, I was guaranteed to be an NBA player.

Ethnically, I am Filipino-Indian, a citizen of the United States, and a lifelong resident of Manila. Many believe having a multicultural background connotes comfort and fluidity, but for me, it's quite the opposite. I often struggle to navigate the complexities of three distinct heritages. Defining my culture has always felt like a dance between worlds, with me constantly out of step.

There is one place where I am perfectly at home: the basketball court, my sanctuary. Basketball never changes, never disappoints. Whether in an LA Fitness, a barangay court in Makati, or a recreation center in Hyderabad, a ball and a hoop speak the language that connects me to every player on the court.

As the day progressed, the universal language of basketball bonded me with these forty kids. Concluding day one, I began to walk toward the exit but quickly found the camp children clinging to my arms and legs. Even though our exchanges were articulated through a collection of broken phrases and charades, basketball dissolved the apparent cultural barriers.

Culture, I realized, is much simpler. Culture is created by exchanging passions with kindred spirits. Through my lifelong passion for basketball, my greatest learnings weren't on the court. Basketball allowed me to connect with my Filipino community.

# The Value of Integrity: Lessons from a Broken Friendship

| Ethnicity | Hispanic |

## Decisions

## Essay — Prompt #4
> Reflect on something that someone has done for you that has made you happy or thankful in a surprising way. How has this gratitude affected or motivated you?

As a child, I always heard my parents repeat the phrase the end does not justify the means, but I didn't get it. It wasn't until I discovered that my best friend had repeatedly lied to me for the past two years that I understood why my parents insisted on repeating this aphorism.

I met my best friend in 4th grade. We were assigned to work on a presentation together. By "th grade, we were inseparable. Every afternoon, we FaceTimed and rambled on about everything. He knew everything I did, and I knew everything he did, or so I thought. In sophomore year, our conversations naturally shifted towards fantasizing about our future plans and where we wanted to go to college, even dreaming about applying and attending the same place, together.

A year and a half later, I met a mutual acquaintance of ours. She spoke so highly of him, which didn't surprise me. However, when she spoke of how she met him, I became confused. How could he have attended an SAT bootcamp in Boston if he told me he was in Greece all summer? She revealed something that made me understand everything: Yeah! We've been working for almost two years with the same private college counselor!

Concealing the gut-wrenching surprise I felt, I remembered asking him a few months ago if he was getting a private college counselor, to which he replied, Nah, I really don't think it's necessary.

Looking back, I'm still not sure when things changed for him. I found myself wondering when he forgot that I was his friend and not his competitor. Confronting him, the trust I once believed unbreakable crumbled under my feet.

Reflecting on what occurred evoked conflicting emotions within me. I felt grief for the dishonesty that tainted our friendship, but I also felt gratitude because I understood the principles and values with which I want to live my life. I'm still someone who wants to accomplish ambitious objectives, but not at the expense of lying to others. I want to pursue my goals with integrity. I realized it is not just about the end, but about the path towards it, and, above all, those who will achieve it with me.


---

# Embracing Kaizen: The Power of Small, Thoughtful Changes

## Applicant Profile

| Application Year | 2024/2025 |

| Ethnicity | Asian |

| IB Score | 39 |

## Decisions
- ✅ Vanderbilt University (Enrolled)
- ✅ University of Southern California
- ❌ University of Pennsylvania
- ❌ University of Virginia

## Essay — Prompt #5
> Discuss an accomplishment, event, or realization that sparked a period of personal growth and a new understanding of yourself or others.

The day Kobe Bryant passed away, I found myself watching his most iconic speeches, reminiscing over the essence of his greatness. Amid his words, one message stood out: the power of small, daily improvements. Inspired, I began researching this approach and stumbled upon a Japanese philosophy called kaizen. Meaning good change, it emphasizes continuous growth through small, incremental improvements. I created a journal to track, reflect on, and plan how to implement and build on these daily improvements. What began as a tool for improving as a student and an athlete soon became a roadmap for building deeper, more meaningful relationships.

When I first began volunteering at the National University Hospital, I struggled to connect with a long-term pediatric patient, Leon. I brought puzzles, card games and started countless conversations, but nothing seemed to capture his interest.

After weeks of struggling to connect with Leon, I reflected and realized I'd been choosing activities based on my assumptions, without really understanding Leon's genuine interests. Seeing this as an opportunity for growth, I applied Kaizen. Each day after volunteering, I sat at my desk with my kaizen journal and carried out hansei: self-reflection.

One day, I noticed Leon watching One Piece, a popular anime. That night, after journaling about it, I decided to watch some episodes myself. During my next visit, I casually mentioned the calm belt from One Piece, and his eyes lit up as he launched into a conversation about his favorite character, Luffy. This slight breakthrough reinforced my belief in kaizen's power: small, thoughtful changes can yield meaningful progress.

I continued tailoring each visit to Leon's growing interests. When I discovered his love for paper planes, I studied some unique designs and taught him the Sea Glider, a plane resembling his origami swans. Each week, I modified my approach, and our relationship blossomed into a genuine friendship.

Inspired by my progress with Leon, I realized I could apply kaizen to my challenges in economics class. At first, her critiques felt overly harsh, leaving me discouraged. But as I started reflecting on her feedback in my journal and planned small adjustments, I began to see her critiques anew. Today, she is letting me teach concepts to my classmates.

Today, kaizen has become more than just a tool; it has sculpted my core values of perseverance, altruism, and adaptability. Through consistent journaling, reflection, and planning, I've learned that the slightest refinements, if consistent, can lead to significant growth.


---

- ❌ Stanford University

## Essay — Prompt #1
> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

Pick a card, any card, I fanned the deck with a dramatic flourish.

At that moment, I wasn't just holding a deck of cards. I was holding my audience's curiosity, their anticipation of something wonderful. My love of magic began under a sprawling Banyan tree during a sweltering summer vacation in Akola, a small Indian village.

My cousin Manas, a dreamer with big ambitions, held the key to that world. Armed with an old deck of cards, he performed trick after trick, leaving me wide-eyed. How did you do that?! I asked. He just smiled. I mentally dissected the trick, trying to piece it together. Was it math? Was it deception? And then it hit me: it was both. The trickery of magic relied on a mathematical formula, and I was hooked.

But for Manas, magic wasn't just a game. I saw how his tricks brought joy to everyone around him. His magic was about creating happiness in a world where it was often hard to find. It was his way of bringing people together. And somewhere along the way, that sense of purpose transferred to me.

I immersed myself in online tutorials, learning the mathematics and misdirection behind each sleight of hand. Hours flew by as I practiced in front of the mirror. Soon, family gatherings became my stage.

That same sense of wonder followed me into a hackathon I was organizing. The room buzzed with stressed students. I pulled out my deck. Anyone up for a card trick? I asked. For those who paused, I performed a trick that dissolved the stress. I'd chat with them about their projects, offer a suggestion or two, and move on, leaving behind a ripple of lightness. Magic taught me that joy is a powerful force that can be spread.

Magic and technology, though seemingly unrelated, share a fundamental similarity: creating experiences that resonate. I approach coding with the same commitment I give to every trick, knowing that the real magic lies in crafting something that leaves others in awe.

For me, technology isn't just a means to solve problems; it's a way to make life a little brighter. And yes, I'll still carry that deck of cards with me, a reminder of where it all began.

# The Power and Perils of Discipline

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
