| IB Score | N/A |

## Decisions
- ✅ Stanford University
- ✅ University of Pennsylvania
- ✅ Yale University
- ✅ Princeton University
- ✅ Columbia University
- ✅ Johns Hopkins University
- ✅ Duke University
- ✅ Brown University
- ✅ Rice University
- ✅ Emory University
- ❌ Washington University in St. Louis

## Essay — Prompt #1
> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

"E-D#-E-D#-E-B-D-C-A-... E-D#-E-D#-E-B-D-C-A-... E-D#-E-D#-E-B-D-C-A...

E-D#-E-D#-E-B-D-C-A... E-D#-E-D#-E-B-D-C-A..."

Was I practicing piano? Or was I being punished?

With the way those notes repeated, you might think I was mimicking a broken record instead of trying to

master the opening of "Fur Elise." Hours stretched into days that stretched into weeks which stretched

into months, until my sanity stretched so much I thought I would snap from all the torture my teacher

called "training."

It wasn't until my first concert that I realized what discipline had done for me. While my heart

jackhammered, my arms felt heavier than if they swam through cement, and although my fingers

couldn't stop trembling, they managed to land on all the correct keys. After that concert, I realized at

10-years-old the power of practice: the dreadful boring work I despised saved me from a disastrous

debacle. Awoken, I converted into a devoted disciple of discipline.

But discipline has a dark side.

One day, while stretching during a class at the Vietnam Academy of Dance, a shrill scream shattered the

air. My head whipped towards the sound and my eyes widened as I saw two students pushing a girl into

her 225-degree oversplits, her hamstrings looking so taut that stretching one more inch might just tear

her apart.

Our teacher, Mr. Dao, then reminded us where progress is made--past our limits. Already a devout

believer, but wanting to impress him while he stretched me, I kept curling my back until I looked like a

scorpion when, suddenly, my spine felt like it exploded. The next day in class, my back--crushed like a

can--couldn't even handle the easiest of exercises. Apparently, I strained it.

At the highest levels of dance, pain is inescapable. When I danced with Ellison Ballet, Mr. Ellison

warned us, 'If you're afraid of fatigue, I'm sorry, but you're in the wrong business.' And with mantras

from other instructors like 'more pain in the studios means less pain on stage,' many dancers and

athletes end up honing a mindset that struggles to distinguish between progress-limiting discomfort

and genuinely detrimental injury. While discipline has helped me push past numerous limits, my peers

and I must exercise its wiser form--restraint--to discern when discipline risks edging on becoming

double-edged.

Despite its dangers, I wouldn't trade away the ability to become unwaveringly committed in my focus.

It's allowed me to stretch my work ethic to reach the higher standards required to achieve my goals. My

discipline has enabled me to become monk-like, comfortable with vows of silence to self-study

sufficiently for AP Biology and Economics, which served as the launchpad for my curiosity when

exploring the subjects' real-world applications. By focusing on the future impact actualized by a strong

academic foundation, I found further inspiration when researching CRISPR technology's advances in medicine and grew excited when imagining how understanding muscular hypertrophy can contribute to

athletic development.

But I don't aim to direct my discipline solely towards my own individual achievement. I care deeply about

helping others, which--honestly--isn't always easy, even if well-intentioned. This past year, after driving

my youngest sister 7 hours to Chicago for another 1-hour violin lesson with Mrs. Vamos, one of the most

sought-after and expensive instructors in the world, her repeated mistakes in basics like bow placement

throughout the lesson continually eroded my temper until, unexpectedly, I lost it and started scolding

her carelessness, emphasizing how much our parents sacrificed personally and financially for this opportunity.

Seeing her shut down, I felt ashamed, remembering she was just 10-years-old. The most advanced

discipline I've encountered? Patience--which, as compassion, is essential for empathy. After apologizing

for my outburst, I guided her aside and began sharing stories of disciplined practice, about how it can not

only unlock your greatest gifts, but also--if paired with patient empathy--nurture the gifts of others.

---

# Finding Complexity in the Darkness: A Writer's Journey

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |

| Ethnicity | N/A |

## Decisions

- ✅ Yale University
- ✅ Columbia University
- ❌ Massachusetts Institute of Technology
- ❌ Stanford University
- ❌ California Institute of Technology

## Essay — Prompt #2
> The lessons we take from obstacles we encounter can be fundamental to later success. Recount a time when you faced a challenge, setback, or failure. How did it affect you, and what did you learn from the experience?

Night air crackles. Crickets chirp a frantic song as wind whispers through the trees. Fire's

hungry glow swallows the twilight, painting the sky black. A house burns.

I open my eyes and remember where I am. The downtown C train, just after 5:00. My wool hat is

damp with melted snow. The car is nearly empty. I'm sitting, and my slowly thawing jeans crinkle

against my shivering legs. The train jerks back into motion. I shove my fingertips deeper into my

jacket pockets. Not much better. What I desperately want right now is a warm crackling fire. I

pull my hands out of my pockets and type the scene in my head into a note on my phone.

Who started the fire? An arsonist? But I need a motive. I narrow my eyes and nearly miss the

overhead speakers announcing, 'next stop is seventy-second street.' My eyes widen as I look

up and swing my backpack over my shoulder. 'Stand clear' I nearly trip on my shoelaces as I

catapult myself out the door and towards my writing class.

I write because I want to share my perspective of the world. I love writing about dark, twisted

things because I deeply enjoy exploring the contrast between the beautiful and the wicked. It's

typical to see goodness portrayed as pretty and evil as ugly, but life is never so straightforward.

In the stories I write, I want the audience to see the complexity that often gets missed around

them. Each time I sit down in a subway and look about, there is so much to see: names

scratched into window panes, stains of early commuters' coffee spills, pins and buttons on

strangers' backpacks, children clutching onto their parents' arm every time the train lurches

back into motion.

All the little details I see on the train tell the stories of people I don't know. Perhaps the kid who

carved his nickname into the walls of the subway car was desperate for attention. Maybe he

was just bored. Perhaps the coffee still sloshing around in the corner was spilled by a woman

shaking with anxiety for an interview. The characters I create in my mind run wild, making their

own stories that intertwine and fall apart over and over again. The city is full of stories of love

and hate, success and failure, and every aspect of human life; I see these tales wherever I go.

As I leave the station and walk through the dark winter afternoon, I think back to the arsonist.

Clearly, this man is not 'good.' He is, quite literally, setting the world ablaze. He still needs a

motive. All characters have motives. I've made up so many pieces of characters' stories and

motives on a whim just because I've needed to. Many have turned out petty and unrealistic, like

a thick patch of unmatching fabric sewn over a jacket with a hole in it. I've regretted not working

harder on these stories.

Perhaps the arsonist wants nothing more than to watch the house burn. Perhaps he's

destroying it with the intention to rid himself of his regrets. The crackling of the fire as his

memories turn to ashes is his motivation. What will the reader think? Will they be able to

sympathize with someone with so simple a motive? Perhaps the reader can forget the horror of arson because they too can understand his plight. We are all complex. As a writer, I want to

show the world what life really is.

I finally reach the door and reluctantly drag my hand through the frigid air to ring the doorbell. I

can't wait to get inside, to pull my frozen cap off my head and get back to writing. I shiver. I ache

for a fire.

---

## Essay — Prompt #7
> Share an essay on any topic of your choice. It can be one you've already written, one that responds to a different prompt, or one of your own design.

Dear Mom,

My birthday is coming up soon, and I've been thinking a lot about getting a pet!

Hedgehogs seem interesting, and I'm curious to learn more about their care needs.

Before making a decision, I wanted to do some research to make sure it's a good fit for

our family's lifestyle. What do you think about starting with some research together?

The financial breakdown is as follows:

Fixed costs:

Cage* - $0 Wheel - $30 Pouch - $13 Cloth - $15 Heat lamp - $20 Thermometer - $5 Food and Water Dishes - $6 Hedgehog - $220 TOTAL START-UP COST: $306

Recurring costs:

Food: $3.42/month Shampoo: $7 every 6 months / $1.10 Vet: $120 every 6 months / $20 Bugs: $5 per month Heat lamp bulb: $1 every 6 months / $1.66 Litter: $5/6 months $0.83 TOTAL MONTHLY COST: $32.01

*A note on the cage: My friends have offered to build me a hedgehog cage for my birthday, saving us approximately $200.

**A note on the food: The good news is that hedgehogs eat cat food, so we won't have to purchase specialty food for it. That said, we will need to shift our cat food purchasing strategy to account for the hedgehog. I propose the following in order to optimize for efficiency and cost: we currently purchase 16 lb./256 oz. bags at $11.88 for our two cats. Cats consume 4 oz. each per day, making for a 32-day bag-consumption cycle at 1 cup/"oz. per day. Hedgehogs will consume 1/4 cup/2 oz. of cat food per day. Two cats plus one hedgehog would consume our current bag size in a 25-day timeframe.

Thus, I suggest we begin purchasing a larger, 22 lb. bag of cat food to keep our purchasing cycle consistent. This bag would cost $16.73, creating a daily cat cost of $0.38 and a monthly cat cost of $13.31 for a 35-day cycle. The hedgehog's food cost would be $3.42 every 35 days, accounted for in this model. I am prepared to cover this monthly cost increase.

Moving on. You might be experiencing sticker shock at the start-up cost, but in reality it's quite reasonable in comparison to other animals. As you know, I babysit Lydia's bearded dragon often and was considering adopting one. Bearded dragons eat 10-15 crickets a day at 10 cents per cricket. That's $1-$1.50 a day on crickets. Additionally, the crickets need to be fed--cricket food costs $6/month. This ends up costing between $37-$52.50 per month on food alone.

You may have concerns about if the cats and the hedgehog will coexist peacefully. In the wild, hedgehogs fight off big birds and wolves with their quills by rolling up into a ball and activating their spikes. They're more than equipped to handle two house cats. Cats adapt to respect the quills and will inevitably get bored and stop paying attention to the hedgehog.

Another foreseeable concern is the time commitment. Hedgehogs are perceived to need more care than household or domesticated pets because they are 'exotic' animals. That is not the case here. I must hold it everyday to maintain comfort around people, much like a cat or a dog. I commit to cleaning its cage and washing its sleep cloth each week, checking food and water levels daily, and bathing and trimming its nails every 2 weeks. Besides holding him, the weekly time investment is only about 2 hours, which is well worth it for a new best friend.

Hedgehogs also help with anxiety and stress. Animals rely on you, which is both motivating and comforting. Cuddling acts as a calming effect and mood booster.

I hope that you strongly consider adding a hedgehog to our family as my sole birthday present.

Sincerely, Celeste

---

## Essay — Prompt #5
> Discuss an accomplishment, event, or realization that sparked a period of personal growth and a new understanding of yourself or others.

The Solomon Guggenheim holds a special place in my heart. Witnessing Cai

Guo-Qiang's gunpowder "paintings" there sparked a lifelong fascination with science, the ultimate tool for artistic expression.

For my sister's birthday in 2011, my family went to see Blue Man Group NYC, an offbeat comedic music show. A powerhouse of color and sound, the show's musical ingenuity was impressive and an engineering wonder.

Halfway through the show, the Blue Men wheeled out a monstrous musical instrument. It was a collection of plastic plumbing pipes attached to one another. There's a strange phenomenon that occurs when the end of an open pipe is struck with a hand or blunt instrument. The resulting sound from this sort of collision is a perfect, pure note. By arranging many tubes of various sizes, Blue Man Group had put together a hacked piano of sorts.

One day I was with my friend Daniel, someone with whom I have many shared enthusiasms. The story of Blue Man Group and the pipe organ came up a lot due to our passion for tinkering and learning. When I first suggested that we try to make one of our own, it clicked right into place. What musical, engineering-minded teenager doesn't want to make a mind-blowing musical instrument?

Our machine would be our machine alone. The pipe organ would have 10 total pipes, and each would have a note in a two-octave minor pentatonic scale. The minor pentatonic scale has only five notes in an octave, which means fewer pipes are needed to establish a wide range of sounds. Yes, we wanted the creation to be grandiose and majestic, but also reasonable.

The planning process was two-fold: first, figuring out the lengths of all the pipes needed, and second, planning out their organization. The first required learning the frequencies of specific notes and how those notes could be produced by the pipes. The length of the pipe would determine the wavelength of the sound wave produced. Luckily, most notes used in music have wavelengths on a human scale. For example, the wavelength of middle C is about 1.3 meters. We finished the planning process with an organized list of the lengths of all the notes we needed.

The process of cutting the pipes wasn't very interesting. We went out one day, bought some pipes, and hacksawed them to the lengths on our chart.

If you will, I'd like to tell you about the Guggenheim now.

For a field trip in the fourth grade, I went with my class to the Guggenheim. We explored the building, getting a taste of what the front lines of modern art had to offer. Eight years later, I can remember only one of the many pieces we saw that day: Cai Guo-Qiang's "Gunpowder Drawings". They were a series of very large pieces of paper, upon which had been laid gunpowder, fuses, miscellaneous rocks, and debris. The explosives were lit, and the resulting mess was preserved on the paper and shipped to the museum. The creations were gigantic and so full of energy; one could almost feel the heat of the explosions from which these works of art were born. I had a revelation. I realized exactly what it was that made the gunpowder art so cool: the greatest human creations come about when art is used to express science. The experience I had with the pipe organ was a perfect example of what wonderful things can happen in the magical realm between science and art. Since that day, my interest has resided in exploring the boundary between the arts and sciences; only in that space can I feel as powerful as Cai Guo-Qiang's gunpowder drawings made me feel.

---

# The Enchantment of Shake Shack

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| Intended Major | N/A |
| Gender | N/A |
| Ethnicity | N/A |
| Country/Region | N/A |
| SAT Total | 1590 |
| SAT Math | 800 |
| SAT Reading/Writing | 790 |
| GPA (Unweighted) | 4 |
| IB Score | N/A |

## Decisions
- ✅ Stanford University

## Essay — Prompt #6
> Describe a topic, idea, or concept you find so engaging that it makes you lose all track of time. Why does it captivate you? What or who do you turn to when you want to learn more?

Braces and putty couldn't stop my New York excitement. My orthodontist, a true New Yorker,

rattled off a "must-do" list with each appointment. Shake Shack, he'd remind me (mouth full

of putty!), was a non-negotiable.

So even before moving from San Francisco to Battery Park, not unlike a desert mirage, the

burger joint held a sort of mysticism for me. There were places that sold burgers, and then

there was Shake Shack. Not a restaurant, but surely not fast food. Leaving the dentist's

office with a goody bag of mouthwash, I convinced myself an excursion to Shake Shack

would surely mark the start of my becoming a New Yorker.

Imagine my elation when, after flying across the country to our new home, I discovered we

were just one block from the lauded establishment. As I lugged cardboard boxes through

the building's front door, my eyes watched the line winding around the block and fading off

into the distance, or, at least, out of my line of sight.

"Is it really that popular?" I asked a heavily-caffeinated neighbor.

"You've got no idea," she said, shaky grip tight around the handle of a three-seat stroller.

Planning my first visit became a game. Hoping to avoid waiting hours in the sweltering heat,

I opted to spend time strategizing in front of scarce air conditioning vents. From careful

observation, I realized the line was always three blocks long by 1:00pm, but the soccer

team from the grade school down the street arrived promptly at 11:00am. Maybe they knew

something I didn't. Something someone like me, who still called New York "The Big Apple,"

was missing.

My first attempt ended in disaster. I messed up and managed to arrive in the midst of an

unexpected evening rush hour.

My second attempt nearly broke me. I sauntered over boldly. There was no line! Ah-ha! A

"Closed for the Holiday" sign greeted me at the door. Only then did I remember that it was

the Fourth of July. Defeated, I drooled against the glass windows — yearning for their

generous condiment stand.

On my third attempt, the line only took up half the block. Like a weekend warrior, I'd

brought a water bottle and a baseball cap — ready for the endurance test of waiting in line

with hundreds of other hungry people. After an hour or so, I was at the front of the line

without any plan whatsoever. I panicked and scrambled to skim the menu while patting

myself down for loose bills and change. How could I have not looked at the menu?

All this time, I'd been so caught up in the logistics that I'd practically forgotten Shake Shack

made food. It was too late for serious deliberation, so I quickly unfolded crumpled singles

while blurting out, "I'd please, uh, love to try a hot dog, fries, and a chocolate shake."

As I waited for the food, I tried to manage my expectations. Don't get your hopes up. You

probably just got scammed for overpriced Nathan's.

But as I bit into the hot dog, I could've sworn doves flew overhead and the ding of the cash

register sounded like a choir. The shakes are sweeter than ambrosia and we mortals simply

don't have the words to describe the nostalgic crinkle cut fries. Yes, it's overpriced simple

food, but we all have to work for something in this life.

For the past seven years, I've experimented with chicken burgers and lemonades, but no

order thus far has topped that of my initial order at Shake Shack. Perhaps, if I venture too

far beyond the hot dog, the mustardy enchantment will end. Or maybe it's just that their

hot dogs are really that good.

---

# The Flexibility of an Impious Vegetarian

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| Intended Major | Biomedical Engineering |
| Gender | N/A |
| Ethnicity | N/A |
| Country/Region | N/A |
| SAT Total | 1580 |
| SAT Math | 780 |
| SAT Reading/Writing | 800 |
| GPA (Unweighted) | 3.98 |
| IB Score | N/A |

## Decisions
- ✅ Stanford University
- ✅ University of California, Berkeley

## Essay — Prompt #1
> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

I call myself a vegetarian because it's easier.

As a kid, I couldn't get enough tuna melts. The salty tang and the creamy, crunchy contrast were pure heaven. I gobbled them down, sometimes two a week, until I was four. Then, one day, a tiny beetle in my sandwich stared back at me, laughing (or at least, that's what my four-year-old self imagined). Tears welled up. It was a confusing cocktail of fear and betrayal. I gently released the beetle outdoors, and though I tried to salvage my lunch, all I saw were phantom beetle footprints. That was the end of our tuna melt love affair.

At that moment, I decided to become a vegetarian. For the next few years, I wouldn't eat meat at all; I was repulsed by it. Today, however, I call myself an impious vegetarian.

Why impious? Well, because I'm not very good at it. While I prefer to not eat meat, I eat bacon whenever I can. Not every day, but, you know, sometimes in the morning or for a midday snack.

This continued love of bacon created an obvious paradox. While I found meat disgusting after that fateful day, I couldn't figure out why bacon still tasted so good. Was I a hypocrite? Where should my preferences start and my convictions end? In order to examine my values, I reflected on history, one of my greatest passions.

In 1925, Mussolini enacted a law that placed a ridiculously high tariff on pasta. Mussolini thought that pasta represented the "old Italy," and that it wasn't quite right for his people. Mussolini's rigidity of thought when it came to pasta was reflected in his obviously more dangerous political life, which led to catastrophe.

So, armed with my love of bacon and my desire to demonstrate more creative thinking than an Italian dictator, I decided to practice flexibility. Throughout this imperfect practice, I've discovered that those who are inflexible usually fail to achieve their goals.

A few years back, I found myself at a friend's house for dinner. It was pasta night, and I love pasta (sorry, Mussolini). But, the pasta was bolognese. I sat down and examined my plate. Sure, if I was cooking dinner for myself, there wouldn't be meat on my plate. But, here I was. I ate it. The act of enjoying the plate of bolognese was kinder and easier than pulling the vegetarian card and demanding a meat-free meal. For me, there's a difference between habits and rules in the same way that there is a difference between opinions and laws. My experience has been that the world makes a lot more sense when I am less rigid in my actions and preferences.

The challenge, in practice, is in deciding when to maintain my convictions and when to practice flexibility and kindness. Inflexibility can be harder than saying yes to an occasional bite of bolognese.

If you come to my house for pasta night (which you're welcome to, by the way), I'll make a crowd-pleaser that we can all share. When I cook, I tend to grab for the pasta in the back of the cabinet that seems to be getting the least amount of love: a half-used box, or a weird shape. I especially love racchettes, little tennis racket-shapes that were easy to find when I was growing up in Hong Kong, but are tougher to find here.

Good pasta, cooked al dente, smothered in olive oil (or, better, butter) and a heaping pile of parmesan, is what this impious vegetarian's dreams are made of. That's what I'll be having. Add a little bacon if you want or even an anchovy. You can eat whatever you want in my home, free of judgment.

---

## Essay — Prompt #3
> Reflect on a time when you questioned or challenged a belief or idea. What prompted your thinking? What was the outcome?

6:55 am: A sharp ding pierces my ear, dragging me from a deep sleep. I fumble

for my phone -- a text.

Kate: Things didn't go as planned last night.

6:57 am: Devastated; she picked up the phone. 'I saw K's name and he has a

new girlfriend,' she choked out. At seventeen, when happiness seems to

depend entirely on others, seeing your first love with someone else can feel like

the end of the world. It's hard to reason with pure emotion, but through her

tears, I gently reminded her that sometimes the past seems rosier than it was.

We've had this conversation almost daily for the past month. I assure her

she's not crazy and this feeling won't last forever.

7:20 am: Our call ends with her calm and confident. I decide to make banana

pancakes because it's Saturday and I have the kitchen to myself before anyone

wakes up.

I gather everything for the pancakes and begin by mushing the bananas. I find

that gross sound of squishing a banana extremely satisfying. I'm no prodigy

during lunch and dinner, but when it comes to breakfast I'm a culinary

weapon.

7:37am: In the middle of folding in the dry ingredients, I hear my phone buzz

from my bedroom. I scrape the sides of the bowl with the silicone spatula and

get my phone. The text is from my best friend Jess.

Jess: Ally's leaving and I don't know how to deal with this.

Jess's older sister, Ally, is moving to Paris. Jess had been excited about Ally's

move, but I realized we had never spoken about how hard it would be for Jess to

say goodbye to the person she looks up to most. This conversation is better on

the phone, so I ask if she wants me to call her. Two seconds later, my phone

rings. I put her on speakerphone so I can continue with my pancakes. Jess is

quiet as the butter sizzles in the pan. She sighs, 'I wish she wasn't leaving.'

My job right now is to listen. Jess just needs to know I'm here for her. I hear her

swallow hard and try to slow her breathing. She says she's calm and thanks me

for listening. As she's about to hang up I ask her to tell me a story. It's the best

way to assure she'll leave the phone call in a better mood. I tell her she's

caught me in the middle of making banana pancakes and I'd love for her to

keep me company. She tells me about a recent family dinner where she and Ally

sneakily picked out all of the tentacles from the fried calamari for

themselves -- they don't like rings, just the tentacles -- before anyone realized

it had even arrived. I hear the smile on her face.

8:30 am: As I ladle the last bit of pancake batter into the pan, my brother

waltzes into the kitchen. He blurts out that his girlfriend broke up with him. No

'Good morning, Lindsey,' or 'Your pancakes look great.' I plate some

pancakes for him and bring them to the table. Giving John advice is always

difficult. We are so similar -- two minutes apart in age -- that it's like having to

give myself advice.

17 year old girls are hard to understand, I tell him. His mouth is stuffed but he

nods in agreement. As he explains the ups and downs of his three-month

relationship, I see him realize that his ex-girlfriend triggered his anxiety and

made him feel like he was constantly at fault. Appearing to be relieved, he asks

me how ballet is going. I update him, we talk about our quirky older brother,

and forage for more food, both of us feeling a distinct sense of tranquility.

After all, it's pretty hard to be upset when you're eating banana pancakes on a

Saturday morning.

---

# Breaking Barriers: Advocating for Equity and Opportunity in Education

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2023/2024 |

| Gender | N/A |
| Ethnicity | N/A |
| Country/Region | N/A |

| SAT Reading/Writing | N/A |
| GPA (Unweighted) | N/A |
| IB Score | N/A |

## Decisions
- ✅ University of Virginia

## Essay — Prompt #5
> Discuss an accomplishment, event, or realization that sparked a period of personal growth and a new understanding of yourself or others.

During my junior and senior years, I have had the privilege to serve on the

Superintendent's Youth Equity, Diversity, and Opportunity Committee (Youth EDOC). The

purpose of Youth EDOC is to formulate ways to provide all students within Stafford County

Public Schools with equitable opportunities and to reduce bias related to race, gender, and sexual

orientation.

As a Youth EDOC member, my role has been part of a sub-committee with a focus on

access to opportunity. My personal focus is to dismantle labels that impede the path to academic

success. Specifically, I believe school staff should focus on providing services to students and

their families that are not based on labels. Opportunities should be shared with students and their

families via methods to avoid excluding any students; every student should be given the power

of choice.

Youth EDOC has provided me with a platform to formulate and share my suggestions.

For example, one avenue to expand opportunities to all students is for schools to explore making

the workload more flexible for students enrolled in advanced programs. Specifically, the work

could be front-loaded, in the form of a college-style syllabus, which would provide students with

the ability to schedule out their work, or, in the alternative, more time could be given prior to due

dates. Additionally, parameters could be set for teachers of advanced classes, such as regulations

governing the quantity of work that is assigned, which would make the workload more equitable.

Implementing these standards could make student-athletes and working students reconsider

advanced programs if the workload was made to be more manageable, thus increasing access.

Another one of my suggestions resulting from my Youth EDOC participation is that

schools should improve their communication of opportunities that are available in the school

division and broader community. Some students and their families miss opportunities due to language barriers or a lack of communication methods; if schools distribute information in home

languages, the scope of communication will improve significantly. For example, some families

may not have access to text messages or email, thus, sending letters home with students in home

languages would likely increase school-to-home communication. Additionally, school division

staff could have interpreters present at all school and community events, to make non-English

speaking families feel more welcome.

During my sophomore year, I was able to share my ideas and recommendations during

two large events, a Stafford County School Board meeting and a school administrators' training.

Both events allowed me to bring these issues to light, make a difference in my community, and

use my platform to benefit others. Once invited to these two events, I became nervous about

speaking in front of so many people. However, after realizing the potential impact, my anxiety

subsided. The most significant result was that I spoke up for what is important to me, hopefully

making a difference for those who are less privileged. This realization helped me grow as a

person and not be afraid to speak up for what I value; this was a significant moment of growth in

my life.

When I found out about Youth EDOC, I decided to apply because of the opportunity to

speak for voices that are not frequently heard. This Committee has strengthened my character by

making CA Murgo, Emily CEEB: 472147 Fall 2023 9 FY ED CAID: 31213076 me step out of

my shell, speak up more (and louder), and share ideas that I previously would not.

Communication is a key element of being a good leader, without communication, there would be

a lack of listening, sharing, and elaborating on ideas when aiming to achieve a goal. Youth

EDOC has opened my eyes to many barriers that minority students face and how infrequently

their voices about inequities are heard. This committee has helped me to improve my character as I will continue to learn, serve, and be a role model in my community, so I can advocate for

those who are in need.

---

## Essay — Prompt #1
> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so

meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If

this sounds like you, then please share your story.

I prepare to leave as the foul smell of solder permeates my room and fogs up the

air. Most people know that these fumes are a long-term health hazard, yet my mission is

much more urgent. I carefully prepare my traveling troupe of educational electronic

devices 3 robots that dance, signs that shine, boxes that make noise. Like any other

day, I am going to school. Yet, today, like many other days, I go as a teacher, not a

student 3 this school, like many other in the region, is critically understaffed. With my

skills and reputation, I took on the role of lecturer, with my goal for the day being to give

a talk to around two hundred students and get them interested in studying robotics.

In my work I see a sense of duty towards my community, my city, my country. I

want to give back to all the people who believed in me, enabled me to learn the wide

array of topics that I know, while sacrificing the little material wealth they had. However,

I also feel a most intimate sense of duty towards my grandfather, who I know for certain

would have given anything to be in my position. Knowing him briefly, I caught a glimpse

of his life and his story. He was born in a poor family, in a village punished by the state

for being politically disobedient during World War II. Refusing to live in squalor, without

water and electricity, he ran away to the city seeking to embrace the gifts of the 20th

century. Yet his origins could never leave him; they were a scarlet letter which made him

ineligible for education and advanced work. Saving up his minimum wages, he

scrounged up enough money to buy textbooks. In dimly-lit factory break rooms, he

carefully spent every spare second to study the science prohibited to him. Armed with

this knowledge, he became an upstanding citizen and campaigned to bring electricity to

his village, whose inhabitants were living in the same conditions as their ancestors from

centuries prior.

On the other hand, I reap the rewards of the 21st century: the internet, plentiful

books, and generous people gave me an education which my ancestors could have

never imagined. Yet, all around me I see people stuck in the same painful cycles as my

ancestors before my grandfather. I also see an inefficient, underfunded school system. I

see a government unwilling to change, negative traditions which destroy progress, and I

see myself as a catalyst for change. From a young age I have held the conviction that

education and technology can bring lasting change. Thus, for many years now, I have

assisted my classmates and members of my community with tutoring and have created educational content focusing on the field of computer science, which has reached

viewers globally. Still, I believe that my skills would be best enhanced and applied

through an education abroad, because I dream that I can return home as one who

brings the gifts of modernity to a place stuck in time.

In the students that I teach, I see hope for change. My style of presentation

resonates with them, they are enthralled by my electrical circus 3 who would know what

works best other than a fellow student? I encourage them to ask questions, to contact

me, and share their struggles and achievements 3 something I always make time for. At

the end of my presentations, I make sure to distribute materials to all who want to learn

more. I know what it feels like to be a beginner starting from zero. Yet I am still a

beginner in life, with the benefit of being ceaselessly curious and always willing to learn

more. I want to go beyond what I can do alone; I want to reach the goal my grandfather

was never allowed to. I want to learn.

## Decisions
- ✅ University of Pennsylvania (Enrolled)
- ✅ Pennsylvania State University
- ✅ Georgia Institute of Technology
- ✅ University of Georgia
- ✅ The Tulane University of New Orleans
- ✅ Brandeis University
- ✅ Babson College
- ✅ Bowdoin College
- ❌ University of Chicago

## Essay — Prompt #7
> Share an essay on any topic of your choice. It can be one you've already written, one that responds to a different prompt, or one of your own design.

I add the critically measured sugary tea mixture to the gallon jar containing

the slimy, white, disc-shaped layers of the symbiotic culture of bacteria and yeast.

Now to wait.

After exactly seven days, I pour the liquid into a fermentation-grade glass bottle

with a ratio of 20% pomegranate juice and 80% fermented tea. I place it on my

kitchen counter, periodically checking it to relieve the built-up CO2.

Finally, after an additional seventy-two hours, the time comes to try it. I crack the

seal on the bottle, leaning over to smell what I assume will be a tangy, fruity,

delicious pomegranate solution. and it smells like rotten eggs. The insufferable

stench fills my nostrils and crushes my confidence. I'm momentarily taken aback,

unable to understand how I went wrong when I followed the recipe perfectly.

My issue wasn't misreading the recipe or failing to follow a rule, it was bypassing

my creative instincts and forgetting the unpredictable nature of fermentation. I

needed to trust the creative side of kombucha4 the side that takes people's

perfectionist energy and explodes it into a puddle of rotten egg smelling 'booch

(my preferred name for the drink- not "fermented, effervescent liquid from a

symbiotic culture of acetic acid bacteria and yeast"). I was too caught up in the

side that requires extreme preciseness to notice when the balance between

perfectionism and imperfectionism was being thrown off. The key, I have learned,

is knowing when to prioritize following the recipe and when to let myself be

creative. Sure, there are scientific variables such as proximity to heat sources

and how many grams of sugar to add. But, there's also person-dependent

variables like how long I decide to ferment it, what fruits I decide will be a fun

combination, and which friend I got my first SCOBY from (taking "symbiotic" to a

new level).

I often find myself feeling pressured to choose one side or the other, one extreme

over the alternative. I've been told that I can either be a meticulous scientist or a

messy artist, but to be both is an unacceptable contradiction. However, I choose

a grey area; a place where I can channel my creativity into the sciences, as well

as channel my precision into my photography.

I still have the first photo I ever took on the first camera I ever had. Or rather, the

first camera I ever made. Making that pinhole camera was truly a painstaking

process: take a cardboard box, tap it shut, and poke a hole in it. Okay, maybe it wasn't that hard. But learning the exact process of taking and developing a photo

in its simplest form, the science of it, is what drove me to pursue photography. I

remember being so unhappy with the photo I took; it was faded, underexposed,

and imperfect. For years, I felt incredibly pressured to try and perfect my

photography. It wasn't until I was defeated, staring at a puddle of kombucha, that

I realized that there doesn't always have to be a standard of perfection in my art,

and that excited me.

So, am I a perfectionist? Or do I crave pure spontaneity and creativity? Can I be

both?

Perfectionism leaves little to be missed. With a keen eye, I can quickly identify

my mistakes and transform them into something with purpose and definitude. On

the other hand, imperfection is the basis for change and for growth. My

resistance against perfectionism is what has allowed me to learn to move forward

by seeing the big picture; it has opened me to new experiences, like bacteria

cross-culturing to create something new, something different, something better. I

am not afraid of change or adversity, though perhaps I am afraid of conformity. To

fit the mold of perfection would compromise my creativity, and I am not willing to

make that sacrifice.

# Lessons from the Fast Food Symphony

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |

## Decisions
- ✅ Emory University (Enrolled)
- ❌ Harvard University
- ❌ Stanford University
- ❌ University of California, Berkeley
- ❌ University of Pennsylvania
- ❌ Cornell University
- ❌ Princeton University
- ❌ Columbia University
- ❌ University of California, Los Angeles
- ❌ Brown University
- ❌ Dartmouth College
- ❌ Georgetown University
- ❌ University of Notre Dame

## Essay — Prompt #7
> Share an essay on any topic of your choice. It can be one you've already written, one that responds to a different prompt, or one of your own design.

The constant beeping never quite fades into background noise, no matter how many times it may have pierced my ear; each electronic chirp and chime is a coded message in a language I've subconsciously learned to decipher. The air hangs heavy with the essence of grease, a scent that clings to my clothes; the smell was initially somewhat nauseating. However, I've found it's grown familiar.

I would have never imagined being here. McDonald's: the only place that offered to hire me.

At first, I recoiled at the thought of being known as the kid who works at McDonald's. But to me, money outweighed pride, so here I am.

My fingers move with practiced efficiency, dancing and gliding around screens with a dexterity that can only be learned with time. The steady hum of machinery gives a constant tone to the kitchen's symphony, with the sizzle of frying oil adding to this ensemble.

As I work, I'm surrounded by an unlikely cast of characters who've become an unexpected source of joy. There's a guy my age, his infectious positivity radiating through the store with the kind of warmth you can't ignore. One of our managers, who thinks he's cool with his cringe-your-mom jokes, has become someone I genuinely enjoy being around (not that I'd admit it to his face).

Suddenly, a sharp beep pierces my ear. My headset, which has a hard time fitting over my dense 4C hair, demands my attention. I straighten my posture; clear my voice; my rehearsed smile, though unseen, instinctually forms as I proclaim those familiar words:

"Welcome to McDonald's, are you using the mobile app today?"

It might be the lady who quickly learned my name, always ready to share our little downfalls and accomplishments when I work in the lobby. Or maybe it's "June, not July," the guy who dances with old ladies just to make them smile. Maybe it's the single African mom with a thick accent and two children by her hip, reminding me so much of my own mother's determination.

Even the not-so-nice customers became a part of this experience.

Listen. Learn. Adapt. De-escalate: skills that have proven valuable in my personal life, from having disagreements with my siblings to taking on leadership roles at my school and church.

I will admit I wasn't too keen on the occasional "Karen" or those who yelled at me, I learned not to take their behavior personally, realizing I don't know what battles they're fighting. Instead, I try to add a bit of warmth to their day.

Working at McDonald's made me realize that everyone has deep, complex, and beautifully passionate stories I want to hear; this only strengthened my desire to be a civil servant, helping as many people as I can with a genuine smile. In a world that often seems gloomy, those moments when I can brighten someone's day, even just a little, give me purpose.

These experiences, and the people who illuminate my shifts with unexpected brightness, shape me in ways I never thought when I haphazardly took this job. They've heartened me to be in turn, a source of light for others, transforming what I thought would be a paycheck into a lesson of humanity and personal growth.

The values I learned at McDonald's now impact every facet of my life, whether at home with my family, at a Black Student Union meeting, or at my church youth group as I try to lead a little group of hooligans. In the end, McDonald's didn't just teach me to flip patties, it taught me to flip perspectives, turning everyday interactions into opportunities for connection and growth.

As I trudge on through the beeps, the grease, and the steady flow of orders, I realize that in this fast food symphony, I've found something far greater in this community: a better version of myself.

---

# Embracing Order and Chaos: A Journey of Control and Flexibility

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2024/2025 |
| Intended Major | Mathematics |
| Gender | Male |
| Ethnicity | Asian |
| Country/Region | Australia |
| SAT Total | 1590 |
| SAT Math | 800 |
| SAT Reading/Writing | 790 |
| GPA (Unweighted) | 4 |
| IB Score | N/A |

## Decisions
- ✅ Stanford University (Enrolled)
- ❌ Massachusetts Institute of Technology
- ❌ Harvard University

## Essay — Prompt #7
> Share an essay on any topic of your choice. It can be one you've already written, one that responds to a different prompt, or one of your own design.

Rewriting your notes again? For the third time this month? my best friend asked, confused as anyone would be upon seeing 14-year-old me seemingly waste hours on end meticulously perfecting my notes.

Yeah, I mean 50% of my b's are close looped and the other 50 are cursive style, it makes my notes impossible to read, I answered, still individually erasing those pesky, cursive b's, turning them back into their looped form.

Nobody cares, you're the only one reading them anyways, he hit back. But he was wrong. I cared. I would always care. Otherwise, I wouldn't be doing this.

I like to think of my life as an eighteen year war: alternately embracing order and embracing chaos.

Order won most battles. Order was what was passed down to me. Order was more than a habit -- it was a way of proving to myself that I had control of every aspect of my life. From pre-school me insisting on using a ruler to connect the dots, to 14-year-old me redrawing b's, order gave me clarity, structure, and reassurance. My belief was simple: discipline and precision led directly to success.

Order dominated the battle of jerryjin.dev. Designing my personal website, I approached the task with meticulous planning -- every page was mapped out, every colour carefully considered to formulaically match in both light and dark modes. The layout was symmetrical and consistent; I spent day and night ensuring each page was a near replica of the last. I had built something perfectly in order; the website was intuitive, efficient, and polished. Order had written laws for the art of design.

Yet, despite its resounding dominance, something nagged at order, preventing it from true victory. That same best friend critiqued the website: "Just another Squarespace site". Order had built a not-so-personal website; not inspiring to others. I needed a more-than-personal website; truly displaying the nuances of my life. Something must change, and that something I felt was to embrace chaos. This chaos, however, wasn't about recklessness or abandoning effort; it was about flexibility, instinct, and embracing the unpredictable to unlock potential I didn't even know existed.

Thus, chaos initiated its counter-offensive -- not just in web design, but in what my coach later described as "the best and worst match of table tennis I have witnessed": my game in our grand final. With the matches tied and me facing off against the most consistent player in the final game, I knew it would be a laborious grind to outmanoeuvre him. Order takes control; we trade topspin rally after topspin rally; I was playing his game of order, but he was playing it much better. Time to change it up; for all the reasons order could not win, chaos could. Chops, lobs, and unpredictable spins -- ugly but effective -- disoriented him. For every textbook shot he attempted, I countered with something wild, uncalculated, yet brilliant. It wasn't just a win for the team; it was a revelation for me. Chaos, I realised, took me beyond the limits order had set.

That lesson carried through to their most recent battle at the five-hour IChO theory exam. Order started well: my systematic, step-by-step mechanisms guided me through the organic problems to the best of my ability. But as the hours dragged on and my focus began to waver, order faltered. Those mechanisms became unwieldy, consuming time I didn't have. Chaos, however, was waiting. Less agonising over steps, more trusting my intuition. I sketched molecules with a fluidity I hadn't known I possessed. Molecules fell into place, connections clicked, and solutions emerged -- not from rigour, but from momentum and gut feeling. Chaos reigned supreme again.

So, now as I reminisce upon 14-year-old me's hand slowly cramping; his eyes blending the curves and loops of all those b's together, I think to myself, "order is the way to work to our limits; chaos is the way to blow past them".

I might put down the pencil next time -- your move, chaos.

---

- ❌ Brown University

- ❌ Vanderbilt University

## Essay — Prompt #5
> Discuss an accomplishment, event, or realization that sparked a period of personal growth and a new understanding of yourself or others.

I was determined to get from the Empire State Building to the Louvre in under a minute.

With my pajamas on, sprawled out on the floor, I hardly looked ready to go anywhere. Yet, as I

glimpsed at the clock, the steady tick-tock drowned out by the blood rushing through my ears, I

positioned my finger to make the click that would begin this journey.

I may not be the fastest in gym class or have the best endurance, but my eyes can fly

down a Wikipedia page. The rules of Wikipedia racing are simple3 given a start page and target

article, players navigate from one Wikipedia page to another, only using the internal hyperlinks

to move. It's a game of mental agility, eyes bouncing across the page, my hand instinctively

scrolling until I encounter the ideal link: the most familiar one. In a maze of uncertainty, with

dead end pages around every corner, there is comfort in finding a link I understand. The tried and

true, straightforward path is guaranteed to bring me one click closer to the finish line.

My first few moves are as expected3 "Empire State Building', "skyscraper', "glass', and

"jar'. With each click, the fog in my mind clears, revealing the easy path up ahead. But as I scroll

through the Wikipedia page about jars, my head begins to dizzy. The links on the screen arrange

themselves in a mosaic of blue and purple, each one begging to be chosen. The purple links

make the case of comfort: having already visited their articles, I know the directions they will

take me. The blue ones, however, hold secrets. Thinking of them, the path in my mind disappears

and is replaced with a void that can only be filled by succumbing to their allure.

Suddenly, my hand moves on autopilot, ready to select the link to the Metropolitan

Museum of Art, a direct connection to the Louvre. But before I can make the click, my eyes

wander downwards and meet a hurricane of purple, its eye that distinct shade of blue. It's almost

comical3 the irony of the words "Pandora's Box' standing out among a sea of letters. My mind

enters lockdown mode, screaming that it's a distraction, but my hand finally free of its clutch, moves on its accord, selecting "Pandora's Box'.

My eyes widen3 there's no going back in the game. I try to convince myself it's alright,

but my confidence deflates as I take in the short article in front of me. The lack of links on the

page further discourages me, and I move to shut the computer in defeat. But before I can, I

remember why I clicked the page in the first place. Maybe there was no use finishing the game,

but I could still enjoy the consequences of my impulsive choice. For the first time, my eyes stop

skimming, and shift to reading.

There may be an optimal method to the madness that is Wikipedia racing, but by learning

to take the risk of following unconventional paths, I've been able to value exploration over

certainty and look at what piques my curiosity. This experience inspired me to take an

unconventional path when researching trade empires in history class. Rather than looking into

the traditional narratives that led to Portuguese dominance of the Indian Ocean Trade empire, I

decided to look into the scientific side, and explore what role the Indian Monsoon played. The

path of this research was anything but straightforward, often leading me to dead ends that I didn't

believe I could back myself out of. However, the twisty process enriched my understanding of

the connection between nature and history in a way that the simplest path could not. The ringing

of an alarm brings me back to reality. But even as it continues to sound, I realize there is no time

limit to discovery.

---

## Essay — Prompt #1
> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

I snipped out two eyes and a snout. From brown corduroy, I continued with the path of my

scissors3moving to old t-shirts, cream cotton, and gray silks. My laptop screen propped open an

image of [x], my close friend [y] current and late dog. Encircled with my handy tools3fabric glue

sticks, seam rippers, and my beloved sewing machine3I was prepared to tackle this patchwork

design. As I glanced from the picture to my configuration of fabrics, I paused to take a step

back. Instead of a perfect layout of dogs reflecting back at me, I saw an awkward collection of

fabrics that looked closer to manatees. But I couldn't settle for defeat.

Restarting, I took a new approach. Instead of cutting ovals, I started with bulbous-shaped

muzzles and continuously layered small, serrated scraps from there. Through zig-zag cutting,

contrast stitching, and ten times the fabric, the patchwork pieces of [x] finally came to life.

Moving from my sewing table, I embody this process of creative problem-solving when at my

math desk, writing proofs and flipping through math books. The cotton fabric is now blank paper

and my needle and thread turn to pencil3instead of rearranging fabric scraps to build features, I

move around expressions to find possible solutions. When stumped on a problem, I adapt by

shifting perspectives: Can I model this combinatorial concept by generating a function? Should I

approach this probability problem geometrically? This is not far from the questions I navigate

amidst the uncertainties in my small business projects: How can I restructure the eyes of a

Persian cat? How can I capture the grumpy folds of the French Bulldog? By taking a step back

and reassessing, I transform my sewn designs into polished pieces, complex mathematical

problems into efficient proofs, and the funny-looking mishmash of fabrics into designs that

resemble [x].

With the patches of the two dogs done, I sewed them onto a sweater. When [y] birthday came

around, she hugged me with tears blurring her eyes and softly whispered, "It looks just like

them."

I tucked her reaction into my mental box of moments that remind me why I sew, joining

memories of me beaming when my friend [z] wore his long-sleeve or customers sending photos

of them happily cheesing with the clothing I've sewn. These reactions are not only the result of

my own design process but also the contributions of other's ideas. My process to make the

perfect sewn piece isn't just one-and-done3it's a dynamic amalgamation of ideas from both

myself and others. When I asked [z] what he thought of the patchwork, he suggested that I add

abc3an ingenious detail to perfect the product. This back-and-forth tango of ideas is similar to

working on math problems with peers. Collaboration was vital in the [x] summer program when

tackling challenging problem sets: <What if we move variable x here?= a classmate suggested.

<Yes! Now we can factor that expression,= I responded. Her idea spurred a domino effect where

we kept building upon each other's thoughts3and, at last, a solution.

Throughout math and sewing, the circle of failure and pursuit of unique solutions has become

second nature to me. Many of my creations, though, would not be possible without the power of

< with=. I remember avidly knitting wonky-looking scarves with friends at the ripe age of 10, like a

group of grannies, or going through multiplication tables with Baba. At the time, I didn't understand how formative the presence of others' ideas and help could be3how momentous

< with= was. Now, as I create personalized landscape paintings with my teacher or solve

competition problems with my Math Club, I realize my circular process is more like a blob3able

to stretch and bend with the insight of others.

## Essay — Prompt #5
> Discuss an accomplishment, event, or realization that sparked a period of personal growth and a new understanding of yourself or others.

The doorknob clicked, and I was a blur. Mom barely got her shoes off before I whisked

her to the kitchen's cramped whiteboard corner, marker thrust in hand. Three perfect

circles, my masterpiece, awaited her inspection.

<So, when an object making sound travels faster than the speed of sound, the collision

between wave fronts causes this thing called a sonic boom,= I squealed in excitement,

redrawing the diagram to show the circles, each its own wave front, superposing.

Only a few minutes earlier, I was eating pizza at my kitchen table with my physics

textbook wide open. As I gnawed away at my cheese slice, I üipped through special

relativity, quantum mechanics, and particle physics.

To my left, Grandmother was sipping green tea and reading her daily prayers. Across the

table, two of my siblings were arguing about who could do the better cartwheel. They

laughed about peripheral details while my mind was üipping over nuclear behavior.

I heard the kettle on the stove begin to whistle and I imagined the water's particle

interactions and the heat transferring between the water and its surrounding material.

I heard an airplane overhead and I wondered about the turbulence that its wings created

as it whipped through the air.

I saw the stars lighting up the sky through the window and I pondered the nature of light

and its unreachable speed.

A singular sentence in my book made me zoom out from my reality as if I could see the

entirety of the universe for just a split second. Each new sentence felt like a jolt of

adrenaline, each new concept an adventure. The diagram of sonic boom on the corner

of page 216 had materialized itself as a monumental concept, perfectly illustrating the

feasibility of science.

I looked up at my mom for the ûrst time since I'd started explaining. She rubbed her

eyes, smudging her eyeliner.

< Leah, this is great, but I just got home. I'm very tired=

<But mom 4 = <Please, can we do this another time?=

She sighed and walked away, missing out on the intricacies of wave phenomena I was

just about to share. I didn't understand why what lit a ûre in my mind didn't seem to

kindle my mother's. All these concepts had been accumulating within me, and I yearned

to share them with her, with someone.

For me, learning is euphoric. New information hits my bloodstream like a sugar high. It

indulges not only my mind, but my entire presence. I crave to be surrounded by thrillingly

complex concepts and hunger for an environment where my level of enthusiasm is

reciprocated.

<So, when an object making sound travels faster than the speed of sound, the collision

between wave fronts causes this thing called a sonic boom= I squeal in excitement. This

time, my mom's gaze is closely following my marker.

<Woah, this probably correlates with those unsolvable Navier-Stokes equations we

talked about at dinner last night, right?,= my mom responds just as eagerly.

<Deûnitely!,= I answer, <because the strength of the sonic boom in the atmosphere would

have to correlate with air pressure...=

When I pulled my mom to the whiteboard, I wanted her to respond like this.

But I had forgotten her long day at work, her indifference to asymmetric circles, and her

shoes that were still on her feet. It was okay that she did not share in my excitement, but

I still wish that she'd been as transûxed as I was.

After she walked away, I wiped the board clean, sat back down at my kitchen table and

turned to page 217. <Electromagnetism.=

Three pages in, I picked up my expo marker once again.

I don't want anyone to miss out on this.

## Essay — Prompt #6
> Describe a topic, idea, or concept you find so engaging that it makes you lose all track of time. Why does it captivate you? What or who do you turn to when you want to learn more?

> The kitchen wasn't a battlefield, but a sensory wonderland. Long before I held a knife, I'd tiptoe to steal sauce-laden spoonfuls, savoring the symphony of flavors. As I grew, I graduated to vegetables, mastering the satisfying crunch of diced peppers and the tear-inducing onion chop. My first bacon-grease burn was a badge of honor, a testament to my growing skills. I mimicked Dad, draping a dishtowel over my shoulder, a tiny soldier in his culinary army. Now, each dish I create is a point of pride - a stepping stone towards the cookbook of my dreams, filled with the tastes that shaped my journey. As any good cookbook should, mine will start with a recipe that can serve as a foundation for future culinary success. This is the first page: Use a frying pan and turn the gas to medium-low. If you cook eggs at a high temperature, the proteins tense up like an introvert in the spotlight, and you'll get crisp egg whites and raw yolks. Some people like this contrast in texture, but I like mine cooked with a more delicate touch. Once the pan is warm, add roughly a tablespoon of olive oil. I don't use butter even though it tastes better because I like to get my healthy fats in early. But be warned: don't let the olive oil get too hot. Olive oil has a low smoke point so I suggest watching it like a hawk. It's time to crack the eggs when the olive oil smoothly coats the pan and shimmers just a little. I crack them directly into the pan because I like to live my life on the edge. You want the eggs to lightly sizzle. When the whites are mostly cooked, it's time to get serious. We're at The Flip, the make or yolk-break moment. I like my eggs runny but not too runny, which means the top of the yolk has to be slightly cooked. I've experimented with various methods of achieving this. Some people put a lid on the pan to bathe the top of the yolk in steam, but this method overcooks the bottom portion of the yolk. Instead, we're going to do The Flip. The 4-Step Flip: Get your spatula. If you're making multiple eggs, separate any whites that have run together. Lift up an edge of the egg and swiftly shimmy the eggs onto the spatula. Flip it GENTLY. After The Flip, I tenderly nudge each yolk with my finger to see how they're hanging. You want them to be bouncy and responsive. If you messed up Step 4, which I've done many many times, you might want to throw some cheese on it and make an egg sandwich, but that's another recipe. If you didn't mess up Step 4, congratulations! Welcome to the Salt & Pepper level. Season your eggs to your liking. Personally, I like a lot of pepper and a little salt. Over the past four years, I've made my way through omelets, poached, soft-boiled, and now I'm on fried. Not every egg comes out perfect. In fact, many are total disasters. Disasters come with the kitchen territory, like when I tried to make brownies with coffee grounds instead of instant coffee, and the best response I got from my normally affable family was 'interesting,' accompanied by poorly-hid grimaces. But mistakes happen, and the next time I served them something they didn't hold the prior disaster against me. After all, cooking is like science. In fact, it's the best kind of science because I get to eat the results when they're good, instead of having to write them up in a Lab Report. As for what recipe I'll fixate on next, who knows? I'm still trying to master The 4-Step Flip.

---

## Essay — Prompt #2
> The lessons we take from obstacles we encounter can be fundamental to later success. Recount a time when you faced a challenge, setback, or failure. How did it affect you, and what did you learn from the experience?

> Late afternoon sun dipped low as I prepped for my final lesson before dusk. My student, a Manhattan transplant with zero surfing experience, stood at the surf shack, a cocktail of excitement and jitters. Months of teaching a diverse crew had honed my skills, and I projected calm confidence as I assured her, "You're in good hands." She was eager to get in the water but appeared distracted as we went through all the steps on land. We laid on our boards in the sand, repeatedly squatting and standing, to help her develop the muscle memory she would need out in the water. I kept stressing the importance of practicing on dry land and remembering the safety techniques we would use in the water. 'Anything could happen once we're in the waves,' I told her. Unknowing of the sheer power of the water, she dismissively nodded in agreement. We took off paddling towards the horizon. After only a few minutes of paddling, I could tell she was exhausted. The waves began to smash into her. Non-stop walls of water buffeted her until she was hopelessly flailing. 'STOP, MAKE THE WAVES STOP, NOW!' she yelled at me. Terror and helplessness fueled her screams for me to stop the waves. I realized this woman must have never experienced the feeling of being completely powerless, at the mercy of something neither she nor anyone around her could control. Although the woman had appeared to be so put together in the safety of the beach, the open water had led her to act alarmingly unreasonable. Unlike this woman, I had experienced the overbearing power of waves many times before. As a surfer I relish in the feeling of being subject to something greater than myself. I had considered picking her up and swimming her to the beach, but I knew it would have been dangerous for me to go near her. The waves could have caused us to collide our heavy, 10-foot boards into one another. I turned toward her and said, trying to muster what I thought of as stern serenity, 'Look directly into my eyes. I'm sorry, but the ocean does not stop for anyone. Take a deep breath and focus on what we learned. Move to the back of your board and point it towards the beach. You can do it.' She calmed down just enough to follow my directions and let the waves push her back to shore. When we were safely on the sand the woman looked at me with disgust. She was livid. It seemed she didn't understand why I had not done more for her while she was being slammed by the waves. I had no more power over the waves than she did. The best thing for her to do was to relinquish her desire for control and focus on executing what we had practiced. I empathized with her helplessness, but my only option was to calm her. Through my lesson with her, I gained a new perspective. I realized that I am in control of my own fate, but that there will be times when I might feel powerless to the obstacles that I am faced with. Like the woman, I have two options. I can choose to stay focused and let all that I've learned guide me or I can let my discomfort overcome me. Just as she found her way back to shore, I know that by maintaining my composure and using my knowledge, I will be able to reach my destinations and navigate the waves of life.

---

## Essay — Prompt #1
> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

> The Brighton Beach train station rises above a labyrinth of old brick apartment buildings and a gray, polluted ocean. Every morning from fifth to seventh grade, my grandfather and I navigated this maze, passing windows with steaming tea kettles and vendor women in scarves setting up their pierogi and fruit stands. We would ascend the stairs to catch the B train, always sitting in our usual spot. My grandfather entertained me with Russian anecdotes as we cracked sunflower seeds and listened to Chopin on his portable CD player. We laughed like lunatics at six in the morning. Eventually, he grew tired, often sleeping through his stop and ending up in the Bronx. He started showing up with different shoes on each foot, slippers sometimes too, and soon the seat beside me grew cold. From then on, I kept myself company with the people I met on the train. An actress sat next to me once, distraught by the disinterest of her audiences. Her brother sat next to her trying to calm her down. I could not help but think of my own audiences, who slouched in their seats while I played the piano, as if my music was just another nuisance in their appointment books. As we headed towards the Manhattan Bridge, Zooey, the brother, told her that we weren't doing this for anyone but ourselves and we were fools if we didn=t do something with the artistry we possessed. If we performed for our own fulfillment we would be bound to affect someone else. Zooey, with his common sense and snarky remarks, reminded me of my grandfather. They taught me how to be an artist. Later a disheveled journalist squished beside me with a typewriter bigger than she was. She wrote about water; not about the ocean or sailing, but about pipes and dams and her desire to control them. Water was fundamental to Joan Didion's Californian existence, much as the subway was to my own. Didion came to realize that controlling the water was as futile as trying to prevent the deaths of her husband and daughter. I control the trains as much as I control my drunken biological father's abandonment or my mother and grandfather's cancers. Didion taught me that our words are the only things we can ever control. With her essays on water and mortality she made me want to be a writer. I sat next to a Colombian man with a bushy moustache. He told me about a family with many repeating names, and like my grandfather's stories, his were woven with humor, and wisdom. But instead of jungles and gypsies, my grandfather sold forbidden chocolates in the USSR. Just as my grandfather's stories eventually came to a close, so did Garcia Marquez's. I close my book and walk through the train doors out into streets of the city. I do not smell the ocean or pierogies, but coffee carts and the businessmen's cologne. I walk with opera singers past the chandeliers and red velvet stairs of the Metropolitan Opera to the new Steinway that waits for me at my school. At the end of my day, I walk back to the 59th Street station, and think about the 3,780 hours I've spent on the B train. I think about how much I love the city, its opportunities and spontaneity, and how much I love Brighton and all it has given me that New York cannot. I think about the steel current below me that strings together these different worlds and how it has allowed me to form my own story that may keep another commuter company some day. I stand smiling like Franny, eating a peach from a fruit vendor like Joan, and feel the subway(air blow from the train that will take me back to my little Odessa by the Sea.

---

# Challenging the Status Quo: A Journey of Experimentation and Improvement

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |

| Ethnicity | N/A |

| IB Score | N/A |

## Decisions

- ❌ University of California, Berkeley

## Essay — Prompt #1
> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

> Questioning isn't demolition. I dissect ideas before tweaking, expanding, or even upending them. It's not about tearing down existing theories, but building upon them with a critical eye. This was illustrated to me in my chemistry class last year when we studied the history of the Atomic Model. John Dalton, in his <Atomic Theory= posited that all matter is composed of atoms. Although this theory was widely accepted, British physicist J.J. Thomson believed something was missing. He delved deeper into the subject by shooting atoms through a cathode ray tube and discovered that they had a partial negative charge. He believed the charge was caused by a component within the atom, and deduced that there must be sub-atomic particles. The modern understanding of the electron was born. Thomson didn't challenge the inherent validity of Dalton's Atomic Theory. Instead, he sought to build upon Dalton's idea. Thomson wanted to delve more deeply into the theory, not knowing what he would find, or if he would find anything. Ultimately Thomson didn't invalidate Dalton's theory by challenging it; he simply enhanced it. I recently had my own "Thomson' moment. There is an infamous braised short rib recipe in my family that I have known and loved forever. I recently decided it was time to be inducted into my family's culinary tradition by attempting to make it. I had to find the spices listed in the recipe. I started looking for the jars but my family did not have the necessary paprika. I found cumin instead, and decided to challenge myself to use the recipe as a guideline. Since the only required ingredient I had was the short ribs, changing the recipe was imperative. Instead of onions I used shallots; instead of carrots I used parsnips; and instead of beef broth I used vegetable broth. The only things I kept consistent with the original recipe were the ribs, cooking time, and temperature. After an anxiety filled hour and a half I turned off the stove and took off the lid. The short ribs looked delicious but I knew the true test was taste. My first bite brought pure joy when I realized that I had not only carried on a family tradition, but I improved it, too. I realized following formulas, equations, and recipes will yield the same result, but experimenting and challenging the limits can produce something equally as good or, as in my case, even better. My lack of ingredients might have been the impetus for changing the recipe, but the underlying premise of taking risks and challenging the status quo have been consistent themes throughout my life. The ease involved in following recipes might satisfy some, but I seek to challenge, amend, and ultimately leave my mark in the way Thomson did. It is important to challenge accepted norms and customs even if they work because, like Thomson, I believe there might be something deeper lying within.

# Finding Refuge in the Deep

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| Intended Major | N/A |
| Gender | N/A |
| Ethnicity | N/A |

| IB Score | N/A |

## Decisions
- ✅ Yale University

## Essay — Prompt #1
> Some students have a background, identity, interest, or talent that is so meaningful they believe their application would be incomplete without it. If this sounds like you, then please share your story.

> Nobody cares down here. Below the surface, distinctions dissolve. Sunlight surrenders to swaying fans, mirroring the fading of societal hierarchies. Vibrant hues dim, mimicking the disappearance of prejudice. Eighty feet down, silence descends, a stark contrast to the surface world where my British accent marks me as an enigma 3 neither American nor fully belonging. This underwater refuge, a world of shared breaths and unspoken understanding, beckons as I yearn for a place where I can simply be. The asphyxiating world above soars from view as I descend below into the water. Distinctions and inequalities wither away as the gorgonian sea fans grow denser, limiting the streaks of sunlight that still manage to penetrate this deep. Marine colours fade, along with human discrimination. It is still possible to discern the surface 3 the silhouette above of a green sea turtle navigating the waves 3 but nobody bothers to look up, to look back. Eighty feet from a dependable source of oxygen we are each other's lifelines, and all we have to communicate with is our hands. No words are uttered 3 no words can be uttered. The sound of my regulator hissing and gurgling fades to a gentle murmur, then nothing. There's absolute silence. What initially seems terrifying, what seems absurd, could not have been more beautifully engineered. Scuba diving is a refuge from the culture clash that shadows me: down here I am the same as everybody else. But when I surface, I am a riddle no one can solve. I am neither British nor American but an amalgam of the two. The last eight years of my life have been spent in Massachusetts, yet when I open my mouth I am indistinguishable from any British teenager. Every day without exception my nationality is acknowledged. A boy who has held a mere four conversations with me since freshman year, all of which were compulsory in one way or another, asks me if I woke up at 4 o'clock this morning to catch the Royal Wedding; my sophomore year World History teacher glances in my direction, requesting a tacit confirmation of the accuracy of her details regarding the religious beliefs of Mary I and Edward VI; my reaction of <Ouch! That really hurt= to a friend who parks her chair leg on my foot in the library three years ago results in a tableful of laughing girls, mesmerized by my unintentional rendition of the YouTube sensation <Charlie bit my finger.= I hesitate, perplexed as to why I've been singled out. Now, a little wiser, I pause in frustration, hoping for this attention to be some type of joke. It never is. Most revel in my English accent. Sooner than later I assume the disguise of a magician, my trick being effortlessly simple, yet consistently engaging. In my repertoire: "dodgy', "wonky', "bloody hell', "loo', "rubbish' 3 just to name a few. I once naively exposed the act to a nagging friend and now it's a full-time show. I am a full-time show. I am not a proficient riddle-teller. I don't even know the solution to my own riddle. I am divided between two linguistic cultures, waiting for time to tell me which one to call home. Underwater I can escape: I can be myself without managing to perplex others. The silence of the deep lures me while I wait.

---
