# Building Blocks of Growth: A Journey through 118 Elements and 88 Keys

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| Accepted To | Princeton University, Johns Hopkins University, Boston University |

## Essay — Prompt #5
> Discuss an accomplishment, event, or realization that sparked a period of personal growth.

> My composition is inspired by 118 elements and 88 keys. Hydrogen. Helium. Lithium. Colorful cartoon illustrations portrayed the elements as a cast of characters. My sixth birthday party had just ended, and I launched myself up the stairs to my bedroom clutching my new book. Its cover was bright blue, and its title exclaimed in bold orange letters, The Periodic Table: Elements with Style! Stretching myself out on the floor of my room, I began flipping through the elements one by one. I admired each element's individuality, but my favorite was carbon; I felt we had a lot in common in wanting to bond with everyone. I got lost in that book, taking a journey through the universe, from its inception to the present day. Alongside my new element friends, I became as large as hydrogen clouds in stellar nurseries and as tiny as silicon semiconductors in computer chips. I learned that the elements are the building blocks of all that is seen and unseen, from things beyond comprehension to things I used every day. And then I reached the last page. "Only 118 elements?" I said aloud, incredulous. In that moment, my wonder transformed into a new awareness. No longer was the infinite variety and complexity of the world mysterious; it was all made up of only these 118 components. It occurred to me that like the elements, perhaps I too could build little by little towards something great. Over the next few weeks, I kept that book close and worked to memorize every element and its properties. I was one step closer to uncovering the secrets of the universe, excited rather than overwhelmed by the wide-open frontier before me. In many ways, this scientific revelation laid the groundwork for the musical epiphany I had three years later. I had been studying the piano, dutifully playing teacher-assigned piano pieces, when one day I decided to play music of my choosing. I sat down at the piano with a book of sheet music from Islands, an album I loved by Ludovico Einaudi. "Those pieces are pretty complicated, Ryder," my mom said doubtfully as I flicked through the pages looking for the shape of my favorite song, "Primavera." I set the book against the stand and began playing the notes slowly. Measure by measure, the melody emerged, and I felt euphoric. The same music that had transported and enthralled me when I had first heard it on the radio was now alive in my hands. I could control it note for note. I was experiencing something surprising that would keep me invested in music for years to come: the thrill of breaking down seemingly complex music into its fundamental parts, then reassembling it in performance. I was reminded that everything, no matter how breathtaking, can be deconstructed to be understood. I kept practicing that piece, until I could play it without thinking, until I could play it with feeling, and finally until I could play it with my own experimental variations. And then it dawned on me that if I had command of the components - these 88 keys of the piano - I could not only recreate something beautiful, I could also invent something entirely new. These realizations about composition, both chemical and musical, have informed my own composition. Together, these moments led me to recognize that just as basic elements build on each other to create the intricacy of nature, or as simple notes combine to form magnificent symphonies, so can I work incrementally toward significant achievements and innovations. This belief has become part of my identity. I like to approach what seems unfathomable with a willingness to take the first of many small steps, and I have found that if I apply curiosity, imagination, and passion, giant steps may well follow. I take 118 elements, 88 keys, and one step at a time toward infinite possibility.

---

<!-- essay_id: 662adfab0bdbbaaf10fce1db -->
<!-- source: https://www.clastify.com/essays/common-app/662adfab0bdbbaaf10fce1db -->

# Discovering Wonderland: How Books Ignited My Passion for Social Justice

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| Accepted To | Stanford University, Johns Hopkins University, University of Wisconsin - Madison |

## Essay — Prompt #6
> Describe a topic, idea, or concept you find so engaging that it makes you lose all track of time.

> I've always had a fascination with rabbit holes. Perhaps this fascination is thanks to my early connection to Alice from Alice in Wonderland. Fleeing from what she cannot process, Alice runs away from her life to escape down a rabbit hole leading to Wonderland. Her time there allowed her to see her own strength, understand others, and most importantly, find herself. Even though my rabbit holes come in the form of novels, I consider my story to be very similar to Alice's. My story, however, began alone in a hotel room. The night before my grandmother's funeral I laid in the room of a Holiday Inn and, like Alice, I found myself incapable of coming to terms with everything going on around me. I could not face the fact that the woman I had cherished in my youth was now gone forever. Little did I know, this horror, this loss, would catalyze the discovery of my own Wonderland. Some inexplicable force came and pushed me to pick up what would become my new favorite book: The Wishing Spell. I fell down a rabbit hole and escaped into the same portal as the characters, the Land of Stories. As I explored this new world, I befriended numerous characters and journeyed alongside the protagonists to find their way home. I saw the story unfold in my mind and momentarily forgot my grief until sunrise. The solace I found became addictive, so, I picked up another book and then another. Eventually, I found my life encapsulated by the mountain of books I had read. However, although I fell in love with the magic and escapism of stories like Alice in Wonderland and The Wishing Spell, I was missing characters who looked like me. Unsatisfied and yearning to feel seen, I turned to books about strong black characters. As I read these books, I was the character I was reading about. I saw myself in their curious minds, ambitious hearts, and feisty spirits when met with injustice. These portals in the form of novels unlocked a door that led me to pursue a newfound interest in the law and social justice. Inspired by these fictional characters, I joined my school's pre-law program and began emulating them in my real life. Like Starr Carter from The Hate U Give, I found myself going to marches like the 57th March on Washington and getting caught up in the rallying chants and smell of determination in the air. Like Justyce McAllister from Dear Martin, I began hosting discussions and speaking out about the need for social justice. I held an online seminar and forum for students in my school to talk about our experiences and uploaded one of the hardest videos I had ever filmed about systemic racism, racism in the book community, and how others can educate themselves through literature. Like Maya Rehman from Yes, No, Maybe So, I volunteered for political campaigns like the Nina Turner for the Congress campaign out of an admiration for Turner's progressive goals. What began as a means of escape became an outlet for self-discovery. The books and their characters were a rich cake and the result of my consumption was much needed growth. Like Alice, the rabbit holes have opened my eyes. I now see the power in books about stigmatized groups and controversial topics: they can change minds, end prejudices, and even change the world. As I diversified my reading, my experience interacting with characters from different cultures, racial groups, and backgrounds has fundamentally changed me for the better in building my compassion, emotional intelligence, and the fire to continue activism. With the hundreds of books that I've read, I will never stop seeking out portals to connect to new stories and experiences. I can't wait to discover new Wonderlands, and look forward to seeing what these new worlds will teach me and inspire me to do next.

---

<!-- source: https://www.clastify.com/essays/common-app/662b32200bdbbaaf1012f32c -->

| Field | Value |

I was reminded that everything, no matter how breathtaking, can be deconstructed to be understood. I kept practicing that piece, until I could play it without thinking, until I could play it with feeling, and finally until I could play it with my own experimental variations. And then it dawned on me that if I had command of the components — these 88 keys of the piano — I could not only recreate something beautiful, I could also invent something entirely new. These realizations about composition, both chemical and musical, have informed my own composition. Together, these moments led me to recognize that just as basic elements build on each other to create the intricacy of nature, or as simple notes combine to form magnificent symphonies, so can I work incrementally toward significant achievements and innovations. This belief has become part of my identity. I like to approach what seems unfathomable with a willingness to take the first of many small steps, and I have found that if I apply curiosity, imagination, and passion, giant steps may well follow. I take 118 elements, 88 keys, and one step at a time toward infinite possibility.

---

# What Haven't I Yet Considered?

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| Accepted To | Stanford University, University of Pennsylvania, Columbia University |

## Essay — Prompt #7
> Share an essay on any topic of your choice.

> All my life, I have been poking, observing, and questioning the world. My parents endued me with curiosity from childhood I now can't help but share. I treasure memories of engineering sandcastles with friends at the beach with a pail and shovel. The salty smell of the sparkling sea reminds me of Elysian days under the sun fighting the water as we dug trenches and erected walls to prevent the waves from claiming our fortress. I still love building sandcastles — a childish indulgence, some say. My creative genesis, I counter. As a child, I marveled at Los Angeles' burgeoning skyline: "How could humans build so tall!?" I had to find out: I poked marshmallows with toothpicks and built towers, then shook my desk to test their structural integrity. Though, I hadn't yet considered the follow-up to functionality concerns: "What about aesthetics?" Often, I derailed my family's travel itinerary, as they'd wait for me to lose interest in the scale models of buildings and cities we visited. I'd want to see every detail from every angle, and then, see it again. My sketchbooks teemed with imagined marvelous structures. And I've remained curious. Many of my eclectic interests intersect in the built environment: "Where to find design inspiration?" I designed an amphitheater in Minecraft inspired by marine imprints Mom and I found fossil hunting. "What are the social implications of design?" I persuaded my parents to visit Rigaud's Louis XIV with me in person so I could conscientiously articulate my Art History paper's thesis: Art as a Vehicle for Authoritarianism. "Can I traverse LA solely on public transit?" I enjoyed commuting by Amtrak to SciArc so much last summer that I convinced my initially reluctant friends to take the train to explore Little Tokyo. I began tutoring partially because of my interest in the built environment as it pertains to considering the needs and lived experiences of community members. Tutoring not only tests my knowledge — it also challenges my biases. What haven't I yet considered? David, Michelangelo 1504. My tutee studied the image and question. "Why is this the answer?" I tried explaining the Renaissance. Michelangelo? The Papacy? I finally asked: "Do you know the story of David and Goliath?" Raised Catholic, I knew the story but, as I learned, her family was Hindu. I naively hadn't considered she wouldn't know the Biblical tale. "What haven't I yet considered?" reframed my approach to life. This lexical arrangement has become a tool for further discovery, personal relationships, and effecting change. Growing up in Greater LA, I understand auto-centricity firsthand. For non-motorists, my suburbia's design is hostile, excluding children from independent mobility. Nonetheless, I concede my privilege, as I've made a politically and environmentally motivated choice to cycle around town whenever possible: to school, to friends, to the library. Freedom to move is freedom to discover. I began sketching, researching, and theorizing ways to improve mobility in my community. Road diets? Lane narrowing? Accessible public transit? I considered alternative transportation interventions in my internship with California Walks where we discussed how to achieve intersectional mobility justice. While gazing down from the 4th Street Bridge at the L.A. River, a desolate scar of cement, my imagination ran wild. I saw greenery, running water, and bustling cycle-paths. I heard chatter: people and birds. As I rode by underutilized storm drains in my city, they mirrored what I saw in LA. Had our city considered cycleways adjacent to the storm drains? "Every child should feel empowered to walk, bike, or roll to school," I declared. Nods and whispers swept the audience, including council members and planners, at our city's Climate Action Workshop. "So then let's consider solutions we haven't yet considered." As I reflect on my past and look toward my future, I know my curiosity will remain constant. Solutions waiting to be experienced, waiting to be discovered, waiting to be understood. What haven't I yet considered?

---

# Accepting the Unconventional: My Journey with Scoliosis

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| Accepted To | Harvard University, University of Pennsylvania, Yale University |

## Essay — Prompt #2
> The lessons we take from obstacles we encounter can be fundamental to later success.

> I have an unconventional spine. It contradicts the diagrams you observed in high school biology, the skeleton on your neighbor's porch on Halloween, and virtually everything you know to be true about human anatomy. While the vast majority of spines resemble the letter "i", mine is "s"-shaped, akin to a windy road or question mark. For a while, I was haunted by the fact that such a distressing abnormality resided inside of me. A routine sports physical with Dr. Barnes had turned into something grave. An orthopedic referral and a short car ride later, I found myself in a new office listening to a man in a white coat use the phrases "32-degree curve," "back brace," and "possibility of surgery." Throughout middle school, I would occasionally miss school to get x-rayed at the Children's Hospital. It was an unending guessing game. Would I continue to grow, potentially worsening the curve in my back, or would I sustain this 32-degree sweet spot? I hated that there was something wrong with me, that I had a diagnosis. The thought of having to wear a brace for 22 hours a day, or, even worse, undergo a spinal fusion, terrified me. I was unable to quiet my spiraling mind and only exacerbated my predicament by scouring the web for more information. I quickly realized that I had to learn to live with it, as we all must do when confronted with the unchangeable. I spent hours staring at my uneven shoulders in the mirror and countless nights watching "My Scoliosis Story" YouTube videos uploaded by girls my age. What was so new and daunting to me had just become another mundane part of these girls' lives. I wanted so badly to get to that point, to not be so afraid. Slowly, I learned to be okay with joking about the wonkiness of my spine and moved toward acceptance. I grew to see my scoliosis as simply another attribute that sets me apart, treating it more as a fun fact than a burden. It now resides in a little nook in my brain alongside a number of other traits that I have decided to stop regarding as weaknesses. Two years after that first visit to the orthopedist, I would unknowingly spend my morning at Children's for the very last time. Every other appointment had ended in the scheduling of my next. But this time, Dr. Martin told me that I didn't have to come back. My growth had plateaued and the curve in my spine was projected to stay the same, seated perfectly in the "moderate" range. In those two years of unknowing, I learned that overcoming challenges is about identifying and taking the necessary steps to be more comfortable in your situation. Neither diminishing nor wallowing in my scoliosis would have helped me get through. Instead, it was the relatable YouTube videos, the jokes, late-night conversations with my mom, and lots of introspection that truly made a difference. In this sense, scoliosis has come to be a symbol for the other less-than-ideal obstacles that life throws my way. From dealing with depression during quarantine in 2020 to navigating challenging college classes for the first time in my junior year, I no longer shrink in the face of adversity. Instead, I develop better habits, ask for help, give myself grace, search for comfort in other areas of my life, and self-reflect. From even the most trying hurdles, I have been able to find motivation, self-drive, a willingness to improve, and curiosity. I now refuse to diminish my scoliosis or any of the similarly distinctive traits that frame my psyche. My journey with scoliosis has provided a framework for a valuable way of thinking. I have become accepting of the fact that I have an unconventional spine.

---

# Choosing to Jump: A Life of Perseverance and Adventure

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| Accepted To | Massachusetts Institute of Technology, Stanford University, Yale University |

## Essay — Prompt #
> Common App Essay

> Jump. Pull. Prepare for landing. These 3 steps race through my mind as I brace myself for my first skydive, 14,000 feet above the ground. It hits me that I9ve been practicing these three steps all my life. I thought jumping would be the hardest part - it requires deliberate choice. Yet, as I shuffled to the plane door, I didn9t hesitate. I chose to jump out of that plane just as I chose to fling myself across the flying trapeze rig, pick up those dropped tangerines until I finally taught myself to juggle, or hop back on my unicycle, not stopping until I found my balance. Whether it9s being the first in my family to play an instrument or leading a state championship cheer team, waking up at 4 am to try crabbing in the Pacific or leading discussions on unfamiliar topics like combinatorics and game theory with fellow math enthusiasts, I always choose to jump. After witnessing the courage of countless brave Army soldiers - my dad included - take their own leaps of faith in service of the nation, I realized the daunting tasks I confront pale in comparison. It turns out, when instilled with curiosity, ambition, and affinity for chasing unique challenges, jumping isn9t all that hard. The next step, pulling the parachute cord, is critical. It9s the difference between plunging towards the ground at 120 mph and peacefully floating under a deployed parachute. While nerve-wracking, I9m no stranger to fighting for that pulled cord. I9ve moved 11 times, spanning nine states, two countries, and ten schools. The sacrifices that most Americans remember every Veterans Day are my constant reality. On August 8th, 2012, two suicide bombers attacked 15 soldiers in Afghanistan. Five men were declared dead, my father included. Miraculously, a fast-acting medic afforded him a second chance at life. That second chance hit my family harder than falling 120 mph out of the sky ever could. My dad9s left leg was mangled, with every artery blasted away and amputation looming on the horizon. Every surgery was yet another turbulent freefall, but our family fought to keep pulling those parachute lifelines. To measure my dad9s daily walking goals, my eight-year-old self counted and mapped every 1-foot tile throughout the hospital9s entire 4th floor. My life is governed by circumstance but driven by choice, and I will always choose to pull that parachute cord of perseverance and optimism. The last step, preparing for landing, had breathtaking impact. The moment was fleeting, yet I was compelled to savor the journey and soak in the visual perspective before it was all over. With every place I9ve lived, adventure I9ve encountered, and challenge I9ve faced, the chapter inevitably comes to an end, and I must prepare to keep trekking forward. Chapters of my past aren9t forgotten but instead, come together as an amalgamation of moments that mold my perspective. Through the influence of these memories - President Obama walking into our hospital room; visiting the graves of soldiers not as fortunate as my father; volunteering at the Wounded Warrior trials, watching awestruck as men with no legs play basketball - my perspective has come to revolve around gratitude and service. Some may think I resent my nomadic lifestyle, but I am grateful for the diversity of culture and thought gleaned from every experience that lands in my life. There is one final important step: repeat. I didn9t go skydiving with the mindset that it would be my only jump, and I don9t face life9s challenges that way either. Whether it be tackling an unsolved Millennium Prize Problem in the math department, improving life for the Wounded Warrior community, or even making an appearance at the Collegiate Skydiving Championship, I9m ready to jump into college and pull the cord on new experiences. While I don9t know where my new landing zone will be, I9m confident the sky isn9t the limit.

---

# Transformative Lessons: Embracing Change and Finding Purpose

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| Accepted To | Stanford University, University of Pennsylvania, Cornell University |

## Essay — Prompt #
> Common App Essay

> The day before my half-sister was born, I had issues with the idea of my family changing. I felt angry and hurt at the idea of my dad starting a new family and, in my mind, leaving behind the one he had already made. I did not want another sister. The day my half-sister was born, we were stunned to learn that she had Down syndrome. My feelings of anger immediately felt ridiculous, and my thinking shifted from, <I don9t want this baby,= to <What can I do to help?= During my junior year, another major aspect of my life shifted unexpectedly. Coming from a family of artists, I had always placed painting, ceramics, drawing, and photography at the center of my world. But as I thought about a career, I longed for something that would combine intellectual engagement with my love of the arts. I just couldn9t figure out what that might be. Because I love all types of art, when I learned of internships at two different museums, I decided to apply. At my first internship at the Craft Contemporary Museum, I was one of fifteen students from across Los Angeles who was selected to make ceramics and learn how a museum runs. While there, I gained an appreciation for the effort that goes into mounting a show. And for the first time, I started to think about how important the placement of art was, because it influences how the viewer interprets the pieces. A few months later, I was accepted into a two-year paid internship at The Huntington Museum. It has been an amazing experience that combined my love of art and nature. Every weekend, I worked a six-hour shift helping to coordinate a program for teen volunteers. My main task was creating activities for volunteers, and my hope was to offer more than surface-level knowledge about the museum. I wanted to challenge the interns to learn and understand how to analyze art. One activity I created was about symbolism in Grand Manor portraits, which included how artists used visual metaphors to reveal details about their subjects. Another activity was providing information and examples of the elements of art such as line, shape, and form, so volunteers could see how such elements were used in the paintings around them. One day, as I walked through the museum, I saw a staircase I9d never noticed. As I approached it, a magnificent stained-glass window came into view, with ten panels illuminating values such as generosity, truth, and love. Climbing the stairs, which were flooded in brilliant colored light, I felt my breath being taken away. Not just by the art, but from the realization that I had not accidentally stumbled upon the stained-glass window but had been drawn there intentionally; by enticing me up the stairs, the curator helped me discover the window and made me feel as if I alone had found this hidden gem. At that moment, I was filled with wonder and delight. I recognized the creativity and intellectual rigor that goes into displaying art, and I realized I wanted to create that experience for others. I transformed from an artist into someone with a new purpose to study Museum Studies and pursue curatorial work. Last December, when my sister arrived so different from how I had expected her, everything inside me rearranged itself not only to make room for her in my life but to embrace her exactly as she is. I know now that things don9t always happen the way we expect or plan, and sometimes change can be scary. At other times, it can feel like opportunities are materializing all around us. Either way, I will be okay, because I9ve learned that in every transformation, I leave a piece of myself behind to make room for new growth, possibility, and even love&which gives me reason always to have hope.

# Seizing the Unexpected

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| Accepted To | Stanford University, University of Pennsylvania, Yale University |

## Essay — Prompt #
> Common App Essay

> <You're my son. Listen to me. = My mother and I had a tumultuous relationship. I followed her around and imitated her actions in an effort to please her, but twice in my life, I ran away from my mom and biked after a nasty fight. She was always very particular about planning; if it felt like something was slipping out of her control, she9d fight to bring it back in line. One day in the sixth grade, I told her I wanted to quit piano to focus on my cello lessons. That wasn9t an option, she said4she wanted me to play until I turned eighteen. I made a snide comment and she yelled at me in response, so I stormed out of the house, hopped on my bike, and left. I pedaled all the way down the street until she stepped out the door, yelling at me to come back if I didn9t want to be in more trouble. After weighing my options, I decided to turn around and listen to her. Who was I to question her plans for me? During the next four years, nothing fundamentally changed. I9d ask her if I could start a new activity or take an interesting class. She9d say, <No, that9s not what I have in mind for you.= I grew up with this order, and anytime I thought I might want something more, I was scared to ask. When I decided to muster up some courage for once and ask her to let me move from my old high school to attend an early-college high school up north in [Place Redacted] after my sophomore year, I expected the same response. Unsurprisingly, she told me no, hemming and hawing about how family sticks together and how scary it would be on my own. One night, I pressed her for the real reason she said no, and she bluntly replied: <I don9t trust you to go up there by yourself. You need to listen to me and what I have in store for you.= For the second time in my life, I stormed out of the house, hopped on my bike, and left. I pedaled all the way down the street, and just like that day four years ago, she stepped out the door and yelled at me to come back if I didn9t want to be in more trouble. This time, however, I kept pedaling. I rounded the street corner, and I was off on my own. After some time en route to nowhere, my physical sense of being became entwined with my emotional sense of being; that is, I lost direction and found myself off the side of a decently-busy road. I decided to stop there to sit on the grass and think, and as I heard the cars zoom by and inhaled their acrid exhaust, a wave of realization crept over me. Life was never something to be planned out. It was always something to be seized upon, and I would never forgive myself if I didn9t seize the opportunity in front of me. I rushed home to confront my mom, who was standing in the hallway. Instinctively looking down at her feet, I stammered. <I know you said no, but I really think I should go to this school next year. This is what I want to do.= She thought for a long while, and then she spoke. <Fine. If you end up going, you will throw away everything we have worked towards. What will your plan be then?= I looked her in the eyes. <I9ll take whatever life throws at me.= I am surely my mother9s son4but I am so much more than that. I am someone who will forgo plans and seize the unexpected opportunities that lie ahead. And I am someone no longer motivated by the desire to please others4but by the desire to explore what9s out there.

# A Lesson in Kindness

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| Accepted To | Stanford University, University of Pennsylvania, Princeton University |

## Essay — Prompt #
> Common App Essay

> I remember the school. I remember the studio. I remember the music. But most of all, I remember the fear. In the summer of 2014, I was involved in the Anne Arundel County Summer Dance Intensive, a three-week-long event that ended with a grand public showcase. I was one of only four dancers from my school in attendance, so with the program being so popular and me being incredibly introverted, you can imagine how scared I was to speak to everybody. Cut to the Jazz Class Nightmare. The teacher told us to get into groups of 4 and create a few eight-counts of choreography. This class, luckily, included the other three dancers from my school, so when the teacher told us to split up, I naturally gravitated towards them. Keep this in mind; we were not extremely close, but we knew each other well enough for me to not feel odd about asking to join them. I made my way over to where the three of them were standing in the corner and whispering amongst themselves. We made eye contact as I walked nervously to where they were. I9ll never forget how quickly they grabbed the girl standing next to them when they saw me heading their way. The combination of the nerves I felt having to talk to people I knew, let alone didn9t know, combined with the fact that it felt like I had been publicly rejected in front of such a large class meant I was but seconds away from an embarrassing amount of sobbing. As I hung my head in defeat and looked for the teacher, I faintly heard a group of high schoolers calling out for someone. <Hey!= <You in the black leotard, join our group!= <Come over here!= It9s shocking how long it took me to realize that they, these seemingly grown women in my eyes, genuinely wanted me, a random, pathetic nine-year-old, to join their group. Needless to say, I didn9t hesitate. Thinking of that day, I am perplexed when I reüect on what was possibly the saddest and happiest I had ever felt within ten minutes. Why did those girls ask me to join them? Was it pity? Did they watch as I tried to join my <friends=, only to see them reject me? Did they even know who I was? After almost eight years, I am still confused, but regardless of why they spoke to me, I was and still am incredibly grateful. Those girls may not have realized this, but they saw something in me that completely shattered my need to follow others or stay in my comfort zone. They showed me how genuine people can be and how much one can care for someone they don9t even know, a lesson that was bigger than I could fully comprehend at the time. Today, I do anything I can to emulate the spirit and kind-heartedness of those girls. All I want to do is make as many people possible feel what I felt after that deûning moment. If I see someone sitting by themselves, I go and sit with them or invite them to sit with me. It9s the small things. It9s encouraging someone who9s taking pictures outside and uplifting them. It9s asking someone how their day is going. It9s promoting fewer cliques and more inclusion. While I ultimately did end up making a fool of myself when I slipped and fell on stage at the showcase, there was nothing that could have hurt me after just one act of kindness. I wish I could remember the names, or even the faces, of the people who changed my outlook on life. If I could, I9d let them know how proud I was to be a part of their group and how thankful I was, am, and always will be.

---

# The Power of Unheard Voices

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| Accepted To | Stanford University, University of Pennsylvania, Yale University |

## Essay — Prompt #
> Common App Essay

> <Aleppo, Beirut, Istanbul, seemed like the tour of the world for someone like me who had never travelled before. My feet are in the sea, hands holding the ropes tightly, I am in my mother9s warm hug, or so I imagine. We crossed Evros in an inflatable boat and hid in a wooded area waiting for our recipient. As soon as the green Peugeot that would take us to Thessaloniki appeared, it was clear that we would not fit easily. We were squeezed into the back seat with the order to keep our heads bowed.= Narrating his journey to me was a Syrian refugee, who in two months would turn 10. His name was Asid - <it means lion in Arabic!= he told me, proudly. There was an incandescent glow in his eyes, yet his revisiting to those events, brought back memories and an expression of feeling trickled down his cheek. I met Asid at the [Organization Redacted] where I have volunteered as a [Place Redacted] language tutor. Shortly after, he moved to Germany with his family. However, by sharing his story with me, Asid taught me the profoundest of lessons: that our voice holds unimaginable power; as long as it9s heard. Little did I know that this realization would prove to be the catalyst for the soul-resonating, heart-gripping, mind-expanding metamorphosis that I so desperately needed. So I began to explore. The exploration led me to [Organization Redacted] a shelter for unaccompanied refugee minors in [Place Redacted]. I couldn9t believe that children with so much to say had no way to do so. This is how I began the exciting journey of launching [Organization Redacted]. For the children whose light, while clearly distinguishable, is constantly diminished, a close friend and I managed to create a forum that marshals their inexhaustible imagination in order to offer them a platform to speak their minds and get their stories known beyond their community. With the support of our sponsors we publish magazines (in [Place Redacted], English, French, Arabic) featuring the kids9 creations but also our own articles, fairy tales, riddles etc. Then we offer them to immigrant housing units in [Place Redacted] and post them online. Finding sponsors was the hardest part; Yet once you share your vision with others, once you present them the impact you can have, they will fervently support you until you achieve it. Not only did publishers allow us to use their content in our magazines, but they were willing to send books at the housing unit and cover the print costs. In designing [Organization Redacted] I have always kept one thing in mind; <there is no greater agony than bearing an untold story inside you=. Indeed these children (aged 7-13) didn9t hesitate to narrate their experiences, to express their dreams and to capture the unique culture of the Middle Eastern and African Diaspora. Seeing them fill the pages of their magazine with their cheerful disposition, childlike directness and disarming honesty, was an invaluable experience. Because these kids though no strangers to pain and loss, are still glimmering with a spark that looks like it could illuminate the whole world. <In the hopeless swamps of the not quite, the not yet, and the not at all, they never let the hero in their soul perish and leave only frustration for the life they deserved, but never have been able to reach=. Never again have the words of Ayn Rand held such relevancy in my life. Truly magical how in the crisscross of our stories, these children passed on to me the greatest legacy; making me redefine the society I dream of. Not the one of cultural assimilation but a colorful <mosaic= of different ethnicities, beautiful and awe-inspiring. This is exactly the community I expect to find in the US, in the context of which I hope to continue working on developing a global incarnation of [Organization Redacted]

---

# The Transformative Power of Volunteering

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| Accepted To | Stanford University, Johns Hopkins University, University of Michigan - Ann Arbor |

## Essay — Prompt #
> Common App Essay

> I held the responsibility of laying the Styrofoam blocks out across the üoor. It was eerie4the colorful blocks were reminiscent of my youth, yet the stark physical therapy ofûce reminded me that this was no childhood game. Parkinson9s patients would soon stream into the building, and they would use these blocks to reach from side to side, engaging their core and strengthening their muscles. The goal of this exercise was to preserve mobility, so that the patients could continue everyday tasks like gardening or driving. I spent most of the summer before my junior year volunteering at the physical therapy ofûce. I checked patients in, kept track of attendance, and led a number of activities, ranging from boxing to yoga. Day after day, I grew closer with those who attended the classes4Mark always told me I had a <mean right hook= during boxing drills, Heather loved mountain-biking, and Dan had a grandson who went to school with me. The more I connected with these people, the angrier I became: the fact that a deûcit of dopamine, a compound I had memorized for AP Chemistry, could have such cruel effects stunned me. The feeling only grew as I thought of my own grandmother, and how she'd been diagnosed with the disease herself months earlier. Suddenly, chemistry no longer represented an abstract collection of concepts, but a critical part of my world. The functional groups that I studied became personal, as the amines, benzenes, and hydroxyls I once struggled to differentiate now made up the very compound used to treat Parkinson9s, the very compound these patients lacked. I returned to my chemistry class that year with a new fervor, suddenly realizing the subject9s importance in deûning our health and wellbeing. I desperately wanted to understand this magical ûeld that held the key to medical advancements and technological improvements. The mathematical equations that once lay plainly on paper were now jumping off it, telling me the things I could invent with them, the problems I could solve. I even undertook my own research project, performing complexometric titrations to determine the most effective method of ion-replacement for water-softening. My volunteer work reinvigorated me; it struck me with the realization that I could use the chemistry I learned in ways far more important than passing a test. I suddenly saw a path for my life in which my academic interests could improve the world, a path towards research programs focused on the chemical components of neural networks. This vision, along with the hope of a better future for both the patients I met and my grandma, inspired me to further my education in the ûeld of chemistry. Now, I still volunteer with the physical therapy ofûce whenever possible, helping them organize events from potlucks to rafües in hopes of improving Parkinson9s treatment options. Staying involved has allowed me to keep in touch with patients, who provide me with invaluable knowledge about Premier League Soccer, the process of making Kombucha, and the best restaurants around. In school, I have continued to expand my knowledge of chemistry, spending much of my most recent summer in the science lab helping my peers to run their own research projects. Though chemistry was far simpler when it did not extend beyond my ûrst period class, the complexity of medical research does not scare me. The same Styrofoam blocks that were once eerily reminiscent of my past hold a new meaning, representing my present and future. I now plan to pursue a career in biomedical engineering, remembering exactly who I am ûghting for as I face setbacks in my studies. And, until then, I will continue bringing my overbaked mufûns to potlucks, ûlling out hundreds of rafüe tickets with names and phone numbers, working on my right hook, and reaching out to my grandmother4doing what I can with the resources I have to shape these patients9 lives for the better.

---

# Challenging My Boundaries: A Teacher's Impact on My Passion for Dance

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| Accepted To | Massachusetts Institute of Technology, Stanford University, Columbia University |

## Essay — Prompt #
> Common App Essay

> I would not say that I am a very grateful person, but recently I experienced something that made me believe cultivating more gratitude in my life is a good thing. For a long time, I9ve believed that some people are talented and others are not, and that9s just the way things are. And when you have a talent for something, you should use it and deserve to be recognized for it. As a talented dancer, I hold this mindset deeply ingrained, I think. Dance has always been natural to me. It comes easy and I know I excel at it. I9ve never failed to impress my teachers. Except for my latest dance teacher. Ms. Martins is a new addition to my dance club, and she is known for being strict and having high standards, like many dance teachers. As I have high standards myself, I expected we would get along just fine. To my surprise, we did not. Every class, she would provide who I considered to be weaker students with plenty of encouragement. For me, she had only critiques. One of her favorite comments was that I wasn9t pushing myself far enough. Of course, this attitude aggravated me and created some resentment. I finally confronted her when she passed me over for a lead spot in the club9s upcoming production. I asked her why she wouldn9t put her best student in the lead role. Her reply shocked me. She said that while I may be her best student, I wasn9t her strongest. When I asked what she meant, she remarked that I would never grow as a dancer if I kept coasting on my natural talent and stopped challenging myself. Determined to prove her wrong, I promised to challenge myself. At the next internal club showcase, I performed a difficult dance in front of my classmates and the faculty, including Ms. Martins. She complimented me on my performance but remarked that I hadn9t truly challenged myself. With renewed vigor, I did as she suggested, and attended a class on hip-hop, instead of my specialty of ballet. Ballet is considered extremely rigorous, despite its appearance, but I learned in a hip-hop class, it takes more than talent to succeed. I had never struggled so much in my life to perform a dance properly. Being the stubborn sort of person I am, I didn9t throw in the towel and instead went back for another class, then another. The next time I had the chance to perform in front of Ms. Martins, I chose a hip-hop routine. It was far from my best dance performance, but it felt surprisingly satisfying to see her smile in the audience. I was even more surprised to hear her say that she was impressed after my performance. What was even more baffling to me was that I felt a surge of gratitude for Ms. Martins for convincing me to push my boundaries and work outside my discipline. When I was able to learn a new style, it gave me a sense of satisfaction that I could still excel outside my preference. When I performed my next ballet routine and found I9d even incorporated some of the lessons I9d learned in hip-hop class into my ballet, I couldn9t believe it, but I wanted to thank Ms. Martins for what she did. Without her, I might never have pushed my boundaries. I might never have discovered that I could go beyond my best performance. Her push for me to challenge myself has made me realize that its important to keep challenging myself and growing in my skill. That I had grown complacent, and it was in fact affecting my passion for dance. Somehow though, my gratitude to my teacher has reignited the spark of passion for dance and encouraged me to never grow complacent again.

---

# The Ultimate Present: Building the Best Possible Future

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2023/2024 |
| Accepted To | Stanford University, Dartmouth College |

## Essay — Prompt #
> Common App Essay

> It was Veteran's Day, and my kindergarten class was listing family members who had served in the military. I raised my hand, waving it intensely, intent on sharing mine. Regardless of the lack of honesty in my statement, I went for it. "My grandpa was in the army." I needed an explanation for his absence, so my imagination filled the void where truth didn't exist. He was now a soldier, a hero. Still, my statement was untrue. Cambodia: My parents would romanticize it. And I would listen. It was a beautiful place, dotted with stone temples, rice fields, and water buffalo. My dad would tell me of when he sat on his water buffalo, tending to the crop, climbing trees for coconuts, and taking the occasional nap. My mom would tell me of her bicycle ride to school and her trips to the market. An image of a tranquil, green, Cambodia, filled my mind. At age 12, this image was shattered. My mother's voice pierced my ears, revealing all. For years, my parents hid the chaos that the once peaceful land endured: War, Violence, Instability. They hid the hunger, loss, and pain. They hid the regime that chased away its own children, choking its own people with soulless hands. They hid that at my age, my father ran from home to a refugee camp in Thailand. They hid that at my age, my mother struggled in constant hunger. They hid, that for years, death hungrily floated above my grandparents, aunts, and uncles. Never will I truly know their struggle, but that was their intention: I will never truly know their struggle. When I was born, my parents made a promise, stating that I would never be exposed to the things that they were, that my aspirations would never be cut short by hunger or war. America would be our safe haven, and they sought to make the best possible present out of the past that they were given. For me, they bore a huge burden. For me, they worked tirelessly. "Work hard, son." My mother had repeated to me. My parents seemed to live by these words themselves. "Work hard." At first, I did not pay them much attention, but that soon changed. One day in second grade, I missed the school bus. An unconcerned seven year old, I walked back home. After I knocked on the apartment door, my mother opened it in surprise. Realizing that my dad had gone to work and that she had no other way to take me to school, she grabbed me by the hand, and we began a seemingly endless trek to school. When we had arrived inside the school building, she looked at me. Her eyes housed neither disappointment nor shame. Her voice sounded fulfilled, as it said the same words in Khmer that she had told me for years: "Work hard, son." She began the long walk home. It wouldn't be until later that I realized the importance of those words. They were the reason why we were here today. They were the beginning of a cycle: Use what you are given. Do what you are able. Live well. Strive for better. "Work hard, son." On my first day of my senior year, my mother hugged me, and said the same words that I'd heard for so long. I smiled, and I listened to those words gently echo inside of my head. "I will," I promised. My parents did not deserve to go through life as they did, but because of their sacrifices, they made sure that I was given all that they could possibly give. They made sure to give me the ultimate present: the present. With it, I've been entrusted with the challenge of building the best possible future, and I gratefully accept this challenge.

---

# The Power of Empathy: A Call to Action

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2023/2024 |
| Accepted To | Harvard University, Columbia University, Rice University |

## Essay — Prompt #
> Common App Essay

> This summer I flew down to Tuscaloosa, Alabama to attend the University of Alabama Honors Academy. For a week, I lived in the UA Honors College dorms and attended college- level classes and lectures. There were many fascinating lectures, but only one will stay with me for the rest of my life – in a very real sense, it has changed the way I look at the world, and my place and purpose in it. It was the morning lecture on the second day of the program –Stephen Black, the Director of the University of Alabama Center for Ethics and Social Responsibility, was the speaker. He began by describing a conversation he once had with the late Millard Fuller, the founder of Habitat for Humanity. Fuller told him that he was proud of his life's work, but that he was increasingly concerned with the success and legacy of his organization. "How could you be?" Mr. Black asked. "Habitat for Humanity has helped millions of people. It's one of the most successful charities in the world." "It's true that we've helped a lot of folks," Fuller replied. "We've helped over four million people build homes over the years. But since I founded this organization in 1976, the rate of homelessness in America has increased, not decreased. Fewer people own homes now than they did then, and more people are living on the street. How can we look at that and say that we've been truly successful?" Mr. Black looked around the room. "You know," he said, "your generation is the most charitable, most service-oriented generation in history. No American generation has ever been so enthusiastic about volunteer work and community service … But the fact is, in today's world, that's just not enough. I hope that your generation can avoid making the same mistake that other generations have made – mistaking sympathy for empathy. And that starts with each of you as individuals." I've been turning that thought over in my mind ever since. Ours is the most sympathetic generation in history, but at the same time it is among the least empathetic. Sympathy identifies a problem. Empathy compels us to demand a solution. Sympathy eases pain. Empathy demands an end to pain. In the same way, charity treats the symptoms of a profound illness in society, but true empathy demands something greater. When we realize that the Third World is also part of our world, that "those poor children" are our children, that violence, poverty, and injustice are too often a result of our own ignorance and our own apathy, then we can no longer merely donate an hour of our time or a few dollars of our money and feel that we have done enough. The world will demand more of us, so we must demand more of ourselves. I've decided to do my part to meet that generational challenge – to understand people rather than feel sorry for them, to solve problems rather than treat symptoms, to act on empathy rather than feel sympathy. Whether I am organizing community service events through NHS, raising funds with the MSHS Nerdfighter Club to help build wells in Ethiopia, or simply trying to act more selflessly and responsibly in my daily life, I always find Mr. Black's words in the back of my mind. It is easy to feel powerless before such great responsibility – I know that there are many days when I worry that despite all my efforts to the contrary, I will not succeed, that, to borrow from David Mitchell, "my life may amount to nothing more than one drop in a limitless ocean." "Yet what is any ocean, but a multitude of drops?"

---

# Embracing the Pillars of Adem

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2022/2023 |
| Accepted To | Princeton University, Boston University, University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill |

## Essay — Prompt #
> Common App Essay

> According to medical professionals, I should have been fatherless before I entered high school. Dementia began to take control of my father long before I knew what it was, much less how to spell it. To others, Tosun was a charismatic professor of economics and a caring friend, husband, and father. To me, however, he has just been Baba, the man who slowly paces the house with a blank, curious stare plastered on his face. I have cared for Baba with my mom for the past eight years, and growing up in these circumstances has urged me to discover my three <Pillars of Adem=, which are as follows: 1. Flamingos are the best animal: Flamingos are not naturally adapted to camouüage into their environment to hide from predators. They are unmistakably pink, and the fashionista trendsetters of the Earth. Baba was the üamingo of his ûeld. According to a lifelong friend and colleague of Baba's, he unwaveringly pursued comparative and interdisciplinary work in economics even though career opportunities in that concentration were faint. I chose to stop playing baseball to devote more time to rock climbing, despite being offered a spot as the varsity catcher as a freshman, because I knew what I was more passionate about. Similarly, I gave up competition climbing, while being a national caliber competitor, to pursue outdoor climbing, where I found more peace and happiness. Today, I let my own motivations, rather than others9 expectations, guide my decisions. I do not embrace the Phoenicopterus ruber solely because I like the pink birds. I embrace it to remind myself that standing out is a gift that I choose to embrace. 2. Piano Man is immortal: I relate with this one Billy Joel song, which illustrates the Piano Man uplifting a crowd of bar patrons who listen to his music to escape from their dead-end, repetitive lives. Piano Man reminds me that one9s uniqueness can touch the lives of others. Despite being in just the same life position as the others, the Piano Man uses his gift to give them a ray of hope. While I cannot control the dementia that has taken away my greatest role model, I can shape the person I become to enrich the lives of those who enter my metaphorical bar. 3. Big Ossipies ûll the world: <Adem 4 Big Ossipie'' are the seemingly incoherent words Baba scribbled onto a piece of paper years ago, but they serve as my sole indication that he remembers me. These words are now the backbone of my most meaningful realization: I can never fully understand anyone other than myself. Am I supposed to believe that my father, who suffers from dementia, had an intent when writing those words? If so, what was that intent? These questions opened my eyes to how complex the human consciousness is, even if it is purportedly fading away at an accelerated rate. Whatever <Big Ossipie= means, it helps me realize that I do not know everything. It teaches me that presuming to know others9 thoughts and actions only leads to misinterpretations, and that it is okay to accept the unknown. Although I outperform my peers on the ping pong table and the track, have proven to be in the 99th percentile on multiple standardized tests, and am a loving caretaker of my dementia-suffering father at nights and on the weekends, I am more than these things multiplied together. I am Adem Aricanli, the second generation American son of a Chinese psychotherapist and a Turkish economist living in an area that is demographically 93% white. My pillars will continue to stand long past my teenage years, much like how the pillars of the Parthenon still stand today. No matter what adversities are thrown my way, I know that the circumstances in which I have grown up have prepared me to work through anything.

# Breaking the Stencil: Embracing Innovation and Growth

## Applicant Profile
| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Application Year | 2023/2024 |
| Accepted To | University of California, Berkeley, California Institute of Technology, Princeton University |

## Essay — Prompt #
> Common App Essay

> It was a day like any other. Bright, the metal strap of my belt reflected the fine rays of sunlight permeating through my car's windshield; enveloping me in a sense of comfort as I drove to school. The pleasant morning was underscored with a fresh suit and a red tie, exclusive to prefects, affixed to my uniform. I took pride in wearing this attire to school, daily. My arrival in school seemed no different to that of a salty chip in a jar of Nutella. The school's corridor, with an array of students in a vibrancy of colors, created a vista of identity. Meanwhile, I presented myself in sharp uniform; a clear rejection of the individuality on display before me. My classmates' necks turned toward the gate, where I awkwardly stood. Their eyes met mine and a growing sense of misery embraced me as their smiles narrowed at the sight of my suit and tie. Among students in jeans and t-shirts, I felt like popcorn in candy. Recognizing that a mundane aspect in life has the ability to engrave its effect on my mind escorted my brain into a realm of query. I wanted to understand why I was the only student in uniform. I checked my school's e-mail and weekly newsletter, and mention of the non-uniform day did not exist. Managebac, the online portal that my academy uses to communicate school-based information to parents and students, failed to provide information for this event too. I recalled missing school on Wednesday that week. It was on this day in the whole-school assembly, that the student government communicated information about this event. If the government relayed this information online, folks who missed school like myself wouldn't be left uninformed. When I brought these failures of communication before my fellow prefects and student government, I was told "This is how things are. whether you like it, or not." Inexplicably, the student government did not question why their stencils had failed They had skipped a platter on the table of life; the meal of simply asking why? Worse still. my failure to show up in unconventional attire induced undue judgment, whose depressing impact brightened my light of wisdom; directing my perpetual growth to a better understanding of how our blind obedience to "the way things are" causes harm. Upon receiving my leadership roles for the subsequent week, I realized, I too, had subscribed to following a stencil; accepting a scheme without questioning its appropriateness. I perpetuated my school's culture of never questioning, never changing and never thinking, fundamentally, about how things work and how they ought to work. No more. As I anatomized this experience, I realized that what my fellow prefects justified as "the way things are", is what I labeled an opportunity One to express what I'm best at, coding. I created a blogging website for the student government through which prefects like myself could communicate online. I changed "the way things are" because they're ancient. I learnt that development is not about accepting concepts as stencils, it's about questioning why they're there to begin with; why the tools available to the student government failed and exploring sustainable ways to change this stencil for the better. Then, my definition of success changed. From "following things the way they are" to one that's innovative. One that would accept progression, development and growth. This growth fostered a new interpretation of life, which I use to this day in developing new projects. I never consider "how things were" when I dive into the ocean of coding. For it's not about throwing a stencil anymore, it's about making one, one that changes with time, with people, with technology. I no longer, merely, arrange characters on a computer screen, following gray-haired rules. Now, I challenge what others deem impossible; allowing me to take a blade of grass and transform it into the sword of a warrior.
